A Fine-grained Classification of Subquadratic Patterns for Subgraph Listing and Friends
Karl Bringmann, Egor Gorbachev

Abstract
In an m m -edge host graph G G , all triangles can be listed in time 𝒪 ⁡ ( m 1.5 ) \mathcal{O}(m^{1.5}) [Itai, Rodeh ’78], and all k k -cycles can be listed in time 𝒪 ⁡ ( m 2 − 1 / ⌈ k / 2 ⌉ + t ) \mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/{\lceil k/2\rceil}}+t) where t t is the output size [Alon, Yuster, Zwick ’97]. These classic results also hold for the colored problem variant, where the nodes of the host graph G G are colored by nodes in the pattern graph H H , and we are only interested in subgraphs of G G that are isomorphic to the pattern H H and respect the colors. We study the problem of listing all H H -subgraphs in the colored setting, for fixed pattern graphs H H . As our main result, we determine all pattern graphs H H such that all H H -subgraphs can be listed in subquadratic time 𝒪 ⁡ ( m 2 − ε + t ) \mathcal{O}(m^{2-\varepsilon}+t) , where t t is the output size. Moreover, for each such subquadratic pattern H H we determine the smallest exponent c ⁡ ( H ) c(H) such that all H H -subgraphs can be listed in time 𝒪 ⁡ ( m c ⁡ ( H ) + t ) \mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)}+t) . This is a vast generalization of the classic results on triangles and cycles. To prove this result, we design new listing algorithms and prove conditional lower bounds based on standard hypotheses from fine-grained complexity theory. In our algorithms, we use a new ingredient that we call hyper-degree splitting, where we split tuples of nodes into high degree and low degree depending on their number of common neighbors. We also show the same results for two related problems: finding an H H -subgraph of minimum total edge-weight in time 𝒪 ⁡ ( m c ⁡ ( H ) ) \mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)}) , and enumerating all H H -subgraphs in 𝒪 ⁡ ( m c ⁡ ( H ) ) \mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)}) preprocessing time and constant delay. Again we determine all pattern graphs H H that have complexity c ⁡ ( H ) < 2 c(H)<2 , and for each such subquadratic pattern we determine the optimal complexity c ⁡ ( H ) c(H) .



Saarland University and Max Planck Institute for Informatics, Saarland Informatics Campus, Germanybringmann@cs.uni-saarland.de
Saarland University and Max Planck Institute for Informatics, Saarland Informatics Campus, Germanyegorbachev@cs.uni-saarland.de

\Copyright
Karl Bringmann and Egor Gorbachev

keywords
Fine-grained complexity, graph pattern detection, minimum-weight subgraph, enumeration algorithms, listing algorithms

category

\relatedversion

1
Introduction

The subgraph isomorphism problem is one of the most fundamental graph problems: Given two graphs
H
and
G
, decide whether the host graph
G
contains a subgraph isomorphic to the pattern graph
H
. This problem and its variants have a large number of applications in areas such as databases, network motifs, statistical physics, and probabilistic inference.
In this paper we study the setting where the pattern graph
H
is fixed and thus of constant size, while the host graph
G
is given as the input, and we analyze running time in terms of the number of edges
m
of
G
(and sometimes its number of nodes
n
).
We study the
colored
problem variant, where each node of
G
is colored by a node in
H
and we ask for a subgraph respecting these colors. This variant naturally arises in database applications. See Section
1.4
for formal problem definitions.

Fine-Grained Perspective

In 1978 Itai and Rodeh solved triangle detection in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1.5})

[
49
]
. Alon, Yuster and Zwick found a generalization to
k
-cycle detection, achieving time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/\lceil k/2\rceil})

[
9
]
. These running times can be improved using fast matrix multiplication, in particular triangle detection is in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2\omega/(\omega+1)})
where
\omega
is the exponent of matrix multiplication
[
9
]
(see
[
38
]
for
k
-cycles).
These works inspired a long line of research on algorithms for subgraph isomorphism.

In contrast, from the lower bound perspective, specifically from the perspective of fine-grained complexity theory, subgraph detection is a big and completely open problem: Even for the simple problem of triangle detection conditional lower bounds are elusive; the current state of the art cannot even rule out linear-time algorithms for triangle detection.
1
1

1

If one is willing to believe
\omega>2
, then there is a lower bound of
\Omega(m^{\omega/2})
, thus ruling out linear-time triangle detection, but even then no tight upper and lower bounds are known since
\omega/2<2\omega/(\omega+1)
.
Thus, we cannot hope for determining the optimal running times for a larger class of patterns.

For this reason, in this paper we study variants of subgraph isomorphism for which the triangle case is well understood, specifically we study
minimum-weight
,
listing
, and
enumeration
variants of subgraph isomorphism, as we will discuss in Sections
1.1
-
1.3
.

Parameterized Perspective

Subgraph isomorphism is intensely studied in the area of parameterized algorithms, for various classes of pattern graphs and with respect to various parameters (see, e.g,
[
63
,
65
,
10
,
46
,
69
]
).
In terms of the treewidth
\tw(H)
a standard dynamic programming algorithm detects an
H
-subgraph in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\tw(H)+1})
or
\mathcal{O}(m^{\tw(H)})
.
2
2

2

This works for the colored version of the problem. The uncolored version can be solved in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{\tw(H)+1})
or
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{\tw(H)})
by color coding
[
8
]
. Here and throughout the paper, by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
-notation we hide logarithmic factors in the input size
m
, i.e.,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(T)=\bigcup_{c\geq 0}\mathcal{O}(T\log^{c}m)
.
For some patterns faster algorithms are known, e.g., for
k
-Clique fast matrix multiplication yields time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\omega k/3})
if
k
is divisible by
3

[
66
]
. However, the best conditional lower bound only rules out time
n^{o(\tw(H)/\log\tw(H))}
(for any family of pattern graphs)
[
63
]
, which leaves a super-constant gap of
\Omega(\log\tw(H))
in the exponent.
It is a big open problem in parameterized complexity to reduce this gap to a constant.
(A similar gap exists for minimum-weight, listing, and enumeration.)

Therefore, for pattern graphs
H
with large treewidth, meaning high time complexity, it is impossible to determine the precise time complexity before answering a big open problem in parameterized complexity.
For this reason, in this paper we study pattern graphs with low time complexity, specifically we focus on
subquadratic time complexity
.

The previous two paragraphs lead us to study patterns with subquadratic time complexity for min-weight subgraph, subgraph listing, and subgraph enumeration, as discussed next.

1.1
Min-Weight Subgraph

In the Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph problem the host graph
G
comes equipped with edge weights, and the task is to find the minimum total edge weight of any
H
-subgraph of
G
.
Standard dynamic programming solves Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph in time
\mathcal{O}(m)
when
H
is a tree.
It is well-known that a generalization of Itai and Rodeh’s triangle detection algorithm
[
49
]
solves min-weight triangle in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1.5})
, and a generalization of Alon, Yuster and Zwick’s algorithm
[
9
]
solves min-weight
k
-cycle in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/\lceil k/2\rceil})
.
Min-weight subgraph problems are of key importance in fine-grained complexity (see, e.g.,
[
77
,
76
,
7
,
78
,
4
,
27
,
33
,
32
]
), mainly due to the equivalence of All-Pairs-Shortest-Paths (APSP) and min-weight triangle: The APSP hypothesis states that APSP cannot be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{3-\varepsilon})
for any
\varepsilon>0
, and this holds if and only if min-weight triangle cannot be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{3-\delta})
for any
\delta>0

[
76
]
.
Since
m\leq n^{2}
, it follows that min-weight triangle cannot be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1.5-\varepsilon})
for any
\varepsilon>0
assuming the APSP hypothesis, which is a tight lower bound.

For any pattern graph
H
let us define its
min-weight complexity

c_{W}(H)
as the infimum over all
c\geq 1
such that Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c})
. The min-weight complexity of the triangle is completely understood, specifically it is
1.5
assuming the APSP hypothesis. We ask how far this understanding can be generalized to further pattern graphs. As discussed before, we focus on patterns with subquadratic complexity:

Subquadratic Min-Weight Question:
Determine all pattern graphs
H
with min-weight

complexity
c_{W}(H)<2
, and for each such pattern determine the number
c_{W}(H)
.

In other words, the questions asks to determine for every pattern
H
the value
\min\{c_{W}(H),2\}
.
This number is known for some natural families of pattern graphs. In particular, the min-weight complexity of any tree is 1, of the
k
-cycle is
2-1/{\lceil k/2\rceil}

[
9
,
44
]
, and of the biclique
K_{k,2}
is
2-1/k

[
44
,
54
]
; see
[
44
]
for some further examples.
However, beyond some natural families of pattern graphs, in its generality the question is open.

Our Results on Min-Weight Subgraph

We answer the Subquadratic Min-Weight Question, conditional on standard hypotheses from fine-grained complexity. Specifically, for every pattern graph
H
, we either prove a fine-grained lower bound showing that Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph is not in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\varepsilon})
for any
\varepsilon>0
, thus showing
c_{W}(H)\geq 2
, or we determine a value
c(H)<2
for which we design an algorithm solving Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)})
and prove a fine-grained lower bound showing that Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph is not in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)-\varepsilon})
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

1.2
Subgraph Listing

For a pattern graph
H
, the
H
-Listing problem asks to list all
H
-subgraphs of a given
m
-edge host graph
G
. Denote the total number of
H
-subgraphs by
t
, i.e.,
t
is the output size of the listing problem.
A standard dynamic programming algorithm solves
H
-Listing in time
\mathcal{O}(m+t)
when
H
is a tree, see, e.g.,
[
15
]
.
It is well-known that a generalization of Itai and Rodeh’s triangle detection algorithm
[
49
]
solves triangle listing in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1.5}+t)=\mathcal{O}(m^{1.5})
, and a generalization of Alon, Yuster and Zwick’s algorithm
[
9
]
solves
k
-cycle listing in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/\lceil k/2\rceil}+t)
.
For triangle listing, further investigations
[
76
,
19
]
lead to an algorithm with running time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2\omega/(\omega+1)}+m^{3(\omega-1)/(\omega+1)}t^{(3-\omega)/(\omega+1)})

[
19
]
.
In a landmark result in fine-grained complexity, Pǎtraşcu proved that listing
t=m
triangles in an
m
-edge graph cannot be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{4/3-\varepsilon})
for any
\varepsilon>0
assuming the 3SUM hypothesis
[
68
]
, which matches the known algorithm
[
19
]
if
\omega=2
(see also
[
59
,
78
]
for further developments).

In this paper we focus on listing algorithms that are
output-linear
, i.e., whose running time depends (near-)linearly on
t
. Accordingly, for any pattern graph
H
we define its
listing complexity

c_{L}(H)
as the infimum over all
c\geq 1
such that
H
-Listing can be solved in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{c}+t)
.
Note that the basic listing algorithms mentioned above for trees
[
15
]
, triangles
[
49
]
, and
k
-cycles
[
9
]
are output-linear, but the improved triangle listing algorithm
[
19
]
is not.
It is known that the triangle has listing complexity
1.5
, as Itai and Rodeh’s algorithm has exponent
1.5
and no algorithm can have exponent
c<1.5
assuming the 3SUM hypothesis
3
3

3

This lower bound follows from slightly changing parameters in
[
68
]
, or from plugging in appropriate parameters in
[
78
, Theorem 3.4]
, or as a special case of the results in this paper.
.
So for output-linear listing algorithms the triangle case is completely understood from a fine-grained perspective.
We ask how far this understanding can be generalized to further pattern graphs. Again we focus on patterns with subquadratic complexity:

Subquadratic Listing Question:
Determine all pattern graphs
H
with listing

complexity
c_{L}(H)<2
, and for each such pattern determine the number
c_{L}(H)
.

In other words, this questions asks to determine for every pattern
H
the value
\min\{c_{L}(H),2\}
.
To the best of our knowledge, the number
c_{L}(H)
is known only for the triangle and for trees (assuming standard hypotheses from fine-grained complexity).
Joglekar and Ré
[
53
]
started an attack on this question, where they discovered some pattern graphs with subquadratic listing complexity which will also play an important role in our work.
This illustrates that our question has inspired prior work, but it was far from resolved.

Our Results on Subgraph Listing

We answer the Subquadratic Listing Question, conditional on standard hypotheses from fine-grained complexity.
Specifically, for every pattern graph
H
, we either prove a fine-grained lower bound showing that
H
-Listing is not in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-\varepsilon}+t)
for any
\varepsilon>0
, thus showing
c_{L}(H)\geq 2
, or we determine a value
c(H)<2
for which we design an algorithm solving
H
-Listing in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)}+t)
and prove a fine-grained lower bound showing that
H
-Listing is not in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{c(H)-\varepsilon}+t)
for any
\varepsilon>0
.
In fact, we obtain the same complexity as for min-weight subgraph, showing that
\min\{c_{L}(H),2\}=\min\{c_{W}(H),2\}
for all
H
.

1.3
Subgraph Enumeration

An
H
-Enumeration algorithm with preprocessing time
P(m)
and delay
D(m)
is an algorithm that lists all
H
-subgraphs of a given
m
-edge host graph
G
such that the time spent by the algorithm before writing the first solution is at most
P(m)
, and for any
i
the time spent by the algorithm between writing the
i
-th solution and the
(i+1)
-th solution is at most
D(m)
. Note that an
H
-Enumeration algorithm with preprocessing time
P(m)
and delay
D(m)
also yields an
H
-Listing algorithm that lists all
t
solutions in total time
\mathcal{O}(P(m)+t\cdot D(m))
. Enumeration algorithms come with the added benefit that they can be aborted early and still yield some solutions, while a listing algorithm might not have produced any solutions at the same point in time. See the survey
[
72
]
for more background on enumeration.
Any tree can be enumerated with preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m)
and delay
\mathcal{O}(1)
, see, e.g.,
[
15
]
.
Trivially, the listing version of Itai and Rodeh’s triangle detection algorithm
[
49
]
is an enumeration algorithm with preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1.5})
and delay
\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Enumeration algorithms are widely studied in the database theory community, and recently there is a growing interest in the fine-grained complexity of enumeration algorithms, see, e.g.,
[
72
,
42
,
16
,
41
,
40
,
28
,
29
,
31
,
24
,
23
,
30
]
.

In this paper we focus on enumeration algorithms with (near-)constant delay. Accordingly, for any pattern graph
H
we define its
enumeration complexity

c_{E}(H)
as the infimum over all
c\geq 1
such that
H
-Enumeration can be solved in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c})
and delay
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
. The analogue of our previous questions then is as follows:

Subquadratic Enumeration Question:
Determine all pattern graphs
H
with enumeration

complexity
c_{E}(H)<2
, and for each such pattern determine the number
c_{E}(H)
.

In other words, this questions asks to determine for every pattern
H
the value
\min\{c_{E}(H),2\}
.
To the best of our knowledge, the number
c_{E}(H)
is only known for the triangle and for trees (assuming standard hypotheses from fine-grained complexity).
Super-linear lower bounds for all non-trees were shown in
[
42
,
22
]
.

Our Results on Subgraph Enumeration

We answer the Subquadratic Enumeration Question, conditional on standard hypotheses from fine-grained complexity.
Specifically, for every pattern graph
H
, we either prove a fine-grained lower bound showing that
H
-Enumeration is not in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\varepsilon})
and delay
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
for any
\varepsilon>0
, thus showing
c_{E}(H)\geq 2
, or we determine a value
c(H)<2
for which we design an algorithm solving
H
-Enumeration in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)})
and delay
\mathcal{O}(1)
and prove a fine-grained lower bound showing that
H
-Enumeration is not in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)-\varepsilon})
and delay
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
for any
\varepsilon>0
.
In fact, we obtain the same complexity as for min-weight subgraph and subgraph listing, showing that
\min\{c_{E}(H),2\}=\min\{c_{L}(H),2\}=\min\{c_{W}(H),2\}
for all
H
.

We leave as an open problem to extend our results beyond subquadratic complexity, i.e., to determine the numbers
\min\{c_{W}(H),\kappa\},\min\{c_{L}(H),\kappa\}
, and
\min\{c_{E}(H),\kappa\}
for each pattern graph
H
for larger constants
\kappa>2
.

1.4
Formal Statement of Results

We start by formally defining the problems considered in this paper.

Definition 1.1

(Problem Definitions)
.

A
pattern graph
is a connected, undirected graph
H
with
|V(H)|\geq 2
; in what follows we fix
H
. A
host graph
is an undirected graph
G=(V,E)
together with a partitioning
V=\bigcup_{a\in V(H)}V_{a}
. An
H
-subgraph of
G
is a tuple of nodes
\overline{v}=(v_{a})_{a\in V(H)}\in\prod_{a\in V(H)}V_{a}
such that for each
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
we have
\{v_{a},v_{b}\}\in E(G)
.

In the
Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph
problem, given a host graph
G
and edge weights
w\colon E(G)\to\mathbb{R}
, the task is to compute the smallest total edge weight of any
H
-subgraph of
G
, i.e., the weight of
\overline{v}=(v_{a})_{a\in V(H)}
is
\sum_{\{a,b\}\in E(H)}w(v_{a},v_{b})
. If
G
has no
H
-subgraph, the result is
\infty
. We assume that arithmetic operations on edge weights can be performed in constant time.

In the
H
-Listing
problem, the task is to output all
H
-subgraphs of a given host graph
G
.

In the
H
-Enumeration
problem, the task is the same as in the
H
-Listing problem. However, instead of the total running time, we consider the
preprocessing time
, which is the time spent by the algorithm before writing the first solution, as well as the
delay
, which is the maximum time spent by the algorithm between writing two consecutive solutions.

We denote by
m:=|E(G)|
the input size, and we write
n:=|V(G)|
. In
H
-Listing, we denote by
t
the total number of
H
-subgraphs (i.e., the output size).

Note that the assumption that the pattern
H
is connected and has at least two nodes is without loss of generality. Indeed, if
H
is disconnected, then the above problems easily split over the connected components of
H
. If
|V(H)|=1
, then the problems are trivial.
We may further assume that there are no isolated nodes in
G
, because such nodes are not part of any
H
-subgraph of
G
and thus can be removed. In particular, we assume
m=\Omega(n)
.

To state our main result, we need two ingredients. The first ingredient is clique separators and the corresponding decomposition.

Definition 1.2

(Clique Separator,
[
48
]
)
.

Let
H
be a graph and
C\subsetneq V(H)
(possibly empty). We say that
C
is a
clique separator
in
H
if
H[C]
is a clique and
H[V(H)\setminus C]
is disconnected.

For example, the empty set is a clique separator of size 0 in a disconnected graph, and a cut vertex is a clique separator of size 1.

Definition 1.3

(Clique Separator Decomposition)
.

Let
H
be a graph. We define
D(H)
as the set of all induced subgraphs
H[V^{\prime}]
for
V^{\prime}\subseteq V(H)
such that
H[V^{\prime}]
has no clique separator and
V^{\prime}
is an inclusionwise maximal subset with this property.

The following lemma makes it easy to determine the decomposition
D(H)
for a given graph
H
, as it suffices to split along any minimal clique separator and recursively construct the decomposition for each part. For an example see Figure
2
.

Lemma 1.4

(
Section

3.4
)
.

Let
H
be a graph and
C
be a clique separator in
H
which is minimal, i.e., no proper subset of
C
is a clique separator. Let
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k}\subset V(H)
be the connected components of
H[V(H)\setminus C]
. Then
D(H)=\bigcup_{i\in[k]}D(H[S_{i}\cup C])
.

We remark that combining this lemma with an algorithm to find clique separators
[
74
]
allows to compute the decomposition
D(H)
in time
\textup{poly}(|V(H)|)
, see
Appendix

C
. (This is not needed for our main results because we anyways assume that
H
has constant size.)

The second ingredient is parallel path graphs, or
P
-graphs for short.

Definition 1.5

(
P
-graphs)
.

For positive integers
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{k}
, the
P
-graph

P(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{k})
is constructed by starting from path graphs of lengths
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{k}
and then identifying all their start nodes and identifying all their end nodes, see Figure
1
for an example. Note that at most one of
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{k}
can be 1 (as otherwise we would not obtain a simple graph).
We often abbreviate
P(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{k},\underbrace{2,2,\ldots,2}_{\gamma\text{ times}})
as
P(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{k},\gamma\times 2)
.

To unify notation, we also define
P(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},0\times 2):=P(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
and
P(\ell_{1},0,0\times 2):=P(\ell_{1})
.

Figure 1
:
A drawing of the
P
-graph
P(6,6,4,1,3,2)
.

For example, the
P
-graph with one path
P(\ell_{1})
is the path with
\ell_{1}
edges, the
P
-graph with two paths
P(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is the cycle
C_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}
, and
P(k\times 2)
is the bipartite clique
K_{k,2}
.

Definition 1.6

(Simple Patterns)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}:=\{P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2):(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\}
be the
P
-graphs corresponding to the set of triples
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{3}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle\{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma):\alpha\geq\beta\geq 2,\gamma\geq 1\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(k-2,2,0):k\geq 4\}

(
P(k-2,2,0\times 2)=P(k-2,2)
is the
k
-cycle
C_{k}
)

\displaystyle\cup\{(1,0,0)\}

(
P(1,0,0\times 2)=P(1)
is the single edge
K_{2}
)

\displaystyle\cup\{(2,1,0)\}.

(
P(2,1,0\times 2)=P(2,1)
is the triangle
K_{3}
)

We are now ready to state our main result. We start by defining a function that governs the time complexity of simple patterns.

Definition 1.7

(Savings Function)
.

For any
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
we define
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=

\begin{cases}2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+2,&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ even, $\alpha>\beta$, and $\beta<\gamma+2$;}\\
2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-3\gamma,&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ even, $3\beta<\alpha+6\gamma+8$, and ($\alpha=\beta$ or $\beta\geq\gamma+2$);}\\
2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+3\beta-\alpha-6\gamma-4,&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ even, $2\beta\leq\alpha+4\gamma+6$, and $3\beta\geq\alpha+6\gamma+8$;}\\
2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+\frac{3}{2},&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ odd and $\beta<2\gamma+3$;}\\
2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+2\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-4\gamma-\frac{3}{2},&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ odd, $2\beta\leq\alpha+4\gamma+6$, and $\beta\geq 2\gamma+3$;}\\
2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2},&\text{if $\alpha=0\bmod 4$ and $2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6$;}\\
2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{3}{8},&\text{if $\alpha$ odd and $2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6$;}\\
2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{1}{2},&\text{if $\alpha=2\bmod 4$ and $2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6$.}\\
\end{cases}

In
Lemma

8.1
we verify that
\mathcal{F}
is well-defined and in
Lemma

8.7
we show that its values are positive integers.
The function values in the last three cases can be unified to
\lceil 2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\rceil
(see Lemma
8.9
). We do not know comparable simplifications for the remaining cases.
An alternative way to define
\mathcal{F}
via only three polynomials is described in
Lemma

8.13
.

The following is our main result. For background on the hypotheses, see
Section

3.1
.

Theorem 1.8

(Main Result: Characterization of Subquadratic Patterns)
.

Let
H
be a pattern graph. If there exists
H^{\prime}\in D(H)
such that
H^{\prime}
is not isomorphic to any graph in
\mathcal{P}
, then

•

Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph cannot be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\varepsilon})
for any
\varepsilon>0
assuming the MinClique Hypothesis and MinConv Hypothesis;

•

H
-Listing cannot be solved in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-\varepsilon}+t)
for any
\varepsilon>0
assuming the ZeroClique Hypothesis and 3SUM Hypothesis; and

•

H
-Enumeration cannot be solved in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\varepsilon})
and delay
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
for any
\varepsilon>0
assuming the ZeroClique Hypothesis and 3SUM Hypothesis.

Otherwise, every graph in
D(H)
is isomorphic to a graph in
\mathcal{P}
, so up to isomorphism we have
D(H)=\{P(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1}\times 2),\ldots,P(\alpha_{k},\beta_{k},\gamma_{k}\times 2)\}
for some
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1}),\ldots,(\alpha_{k},\beta_{k},\gamma_{k})\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Let
c(H):=2-1/\max_{i\in[k]}\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i})
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0

•

Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)})
, but not in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)-\varepsilon})
assuming the MinClique Hypothesis;

•

H
-Listing can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)}+t)
, but not in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{c(H)-\varepsilon}+t)
assuming the ZeroClique Hypothesis; and

•

H
-Enumeration can be solved in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)})
and delay
\mathcal{O}(1)
, but not in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)-\varepsilon})
and delay
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
assuming the ZeroClique Hypothesis.

Informally,
Theorem

1.8
shows that all subquadratic patterns arise by stitching together graphs in
\mathcal{P}
along cliques, in a tree-like fashion. Actually, stitching along nodes and edges is sufficient, because no graph in
\mathcal{P}
contains a triangle as a proper subgraph (see
Lemma

3.3
). The overall complexity is the maximum of
2-1/\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
over all stitched parts
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
.

Let us discuss some examples, and check that we rediscover known complexities.

•

If
H
is a tree, then
D(H)
consists of all (subgraphs induced by) edges of
H
. So each graph in
D(H)
is isomorphic to
K_{2}=P(1)=P(1,0,0\times 2)\in\mathcal{P}
. We have
\mathcal{F}(1,0,0)=1
by case 4 of
Definition

1.7
, so
Theorem

1.8
yields complexity
c(H)=2-1/1=1
.

•

The triangle
H=K_{3}
has no clique separator, so
D(H)=\{H\}=\{P(2,1,0\times 2)\}
. We have
\mathcal{F}(2,1,0)=2
from case 4 of
Definition

1.7
, so
Theorem

1.8
yields complexity
c(K_{3})=1.5
.

•

The
k
-cycle
H=C_{k}
for
k\geq 4
also has no clique separator, so we have
D(H)=\{H\}=\{P(k-2,2,0\times 2)\}
. For even
k
, case 2 of
Definition

1.7
applies and yields
\mathcal{F}(k-2,2,0)=k/2
. For odd
k
, case 4 applies and yields
\mathcal{F}(k-2,2,0)=(k+1)/2
. In either case we have
\mathcal{F}(k-2,2,0)=\lceil k/2\rceil
, so we obtain complexity
c(C_{k})=2-1/\lceil k/2\rceil
.

•

The biclique
H=K_{k,2}=P(2,2,(k-2)\times 2)
for
k\geq 3
has no clique separator, so
D(H)=\{H\}
. We have
\mathcal{F}(2,2,k-2)=k
by case 2 of
Definition

1.7
, so we obtain complexity
2-1/k
.

•

See
Figure

2
for another non-trivial example.

Figure 2
:
Example application of our main result. All graphs in
D(H)
are in
\mathcal{P}
, and the maximum value of
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
over all parts is
8
, so we obtain complexity
c(H)=2-1/8
.

One implication of our result is that all subquadratic complexities are of the form
2-1/k
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, e.g., there is no pattern with complexity strictly between
1.5
and
1.66
. (This follows from the fact that
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
is a positive integer for every
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, see Lemma
8.7
.)

It is easy to determine all graphs from
\mathcal{P}
attaining
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=k
for a given
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Indeed, we show that every
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
satisfies
\alpha\geq\beta
and
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\geq\alpha/2+\gamma
(see
Lemma

8.11
), and thus
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=k
implies
\beta\leq\alpha\leq 2k
and
\gamma\leq k
. Hence, only a finite number of candidates in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
can attain value
k
, and we can calculate the value
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
for each candidate. This is tedious to do by hand, but simple for a computer search.
This search yields the following exhaustive list of patterns in
\mathcal{P}
attaining
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=1,2,\ldots,10
:

1:

K_{2}=P(1,0,0\times 2)
,

2:

K_{3}=P(2,1,0\times 2)
,
C_{4}=P(2,2,0\times 2)
,

3:

C_{5}=P(3,2,0\times 2),C_{6}=P(4,2,0\times 2),K_{3,2}=P(2,2,1\times 2)
,

4:

C_{7}=P(5,2,0\times 2),C_{8}=P(6,2,0\times 2),K_{4,2}=P(2,2,2\times 2)
,

5:

C_{9}=P(7,2,0\times 2),C_{10}=P(8,2,0\times 2),K_{5,2}=P(2,2,3\times 2),P(3,2,1\times 2),P(4,2,1\times 2)
.

6:

C_{11}=P(9,2,0\times 2),C_{12}=P(10,2,0\times 2),K_{6,2}=P(2,2,4\times 2),P(5,2,1\times 2),P(6,2,1\times 2)
,
P(3,3,1\times 2)
,

7:

C_{13}=P(11,2,0\times 2),C_{14}=P(12,2,0\times 2),K_{7,2}=P(2,2,5\times 2),P(7,2,1\times 2),P(8,2,1\times 2)
,
P(3,2,2\times 2),P(4,2,2\times 2)
,

8:

C_{15}=P(13,2,0\times 2),C_{16}=P(14,2,0\times 2),K_{8,2}=P(2,2,6\times 2),P(9,2,1\times 2),P(10,2,1\times 2)
,
P(5,2,2\times 2),P(6,2,2\times 2),P(4,3,1\times 2),P(5,3,1\times 2)
,

9:

C_{17}=P(15,2,0\times 2),C_{18}=P(16,2,0\times 2),K_{9,2}=P(2,2,7\times 2),P(11,2,1\times 2),P(12,2,1\times 2)
,
P(7,2,2\times 2),P(8,2,2\times 2),P(3,2,3\times 2),P(4,2,3\times 2),P(3,3,2\times 2),P(4,4,1\times 2)
,

10:

C_{19}=P(17,2,0\times 2),C_{20}=P(18,2,0\times 2),K_{10,2}=P(2,2,8\times 2),P(13,2,1\times 2),P(14,2,1\times 2)
,
P(9,2,2\times 2),P(10,2,2\times 2),P(5,2,3\times 2),P(6,2,3\times 2),P(6,3,1\times 2),P(7,3,1\times 2)
.

It is well known that all patterns with complexity
1=2-1/1
are trees, i.e., they arise by stitching together any number of
K_{2}
graphs in a tree-like fashion.
Our results show that all patterns with exponent
2-1/2
arise by stitching together any number of
K_{2},K_{3}
, and
C_{4}
graphs along nodes or edges, in a tree-like fashion, using at least one
K_{3}
or
C_{4}
. Similarly, all patterns with exponent
2-1/3
arise by stitching together any number of
K_{2},K_{3},C_{4},C_{5},C_{6}
, and
K_{3,2}
graphs along nodes or edges, in a tree-like fashion, using at least one
C_{5},C_{6}
, or
K_{3,2}
; etc.

1.5
Discussion

Our work is a vast generalization of prior results on min-weight, listing, and enumeration variants of subgraph isomorphism.
It was well known that trees are exactly the patterns with linear complexity.
We show the first results that extend this knowledge beyond the linear-time regime, characterizing the class of patterns with complexity
c
for any
1<c<2
.

Our framework is a unified approach to min-weight, listing, and enumeration, which essentially allows us to rederive prior results in either of these settings for all three settings. However, even the union of prior results for these three settings was far from a complete understanding: While for the triangle,
k
-cycle, and single edge the complexity is long known, for the remaining graphs in
\mathcal{P}
Joglekar and Ré
[
53
]
showed that their listing complexity is strictly below 2, but they did not achieve the optimal complexity (e.g., for
K_{k,2}
their framework gives exponent
2-2^{1-k}

[
53
, Example 37]
, while the optimal exponent is
2-1/k
). We thus in particular had to (1) design improved algorithms for these patterns, (2) prove matching fine-grained lower bounds, (3) show that stitching together such graphs along nodes and edges keeps the complexity below 2, and (4) show that all subquadratic patterns can be constructed in this way.
Points (1) and (2) are particularly challenging, since the complexity of graphs in
\mathcal{P}
is very complicated, as can be seen from the 8 cases in
Definition

1.7
.
Since we prove matching upper and conditional lower bounds, we show that this complicated complexity is not just an artifact of our algorithms, but is actually the true complexity of these patterns (conditional on standard hypotheses from fine-grained complexity).

2
Proof Overview

In this section we provide an overview of our tools and the structure of our proof.
At the end of this section we will prove our main result, assuming the lemmas that we state throughout this section. In later sections it then remains to prove the lemmas stated here.

2.1
Lower Bounds

For our lower bounds, we adopt the framework of clique embeddings from
[
44
]
.

Definition 2.1

(Clique Embedding,
[
44
]
)
.

Let
H
be a graph. We say that sets
A,B\subseteq V(H)

touch
in
H
if
A\cap B\neq\emptyset
or there exists an edge
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
with
a\in A
and
b\in B
.

Let
H
be a pattern graph and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. An
embedding
from a clique
K_{k}
to
H
is a function
\psi
that maps every node
u\in V(K_{k})
to a non-empty subset
\psi(u)\subseteq V(H)
such that
H[\psi(u)]
is connected for every
u\in V(K_{k})
, and
\psi(u)
and
\psi(v)
touch in
H
for all
u,v\in V(K_{k})
.

For an embedding
\psi
, the
weak depth
of an edge
e=\{a,b\}\in E(H)
is
d_{\psi}(e):=|\{u\in V(K_{k}):\{a,b\}\cap\psi(u)\neq\emptyset\}|
. The
weak edge depth
of the embedding
\psi
is
\wed(\psi):=\max_{e\in E(H)}d_{\psi}(e)
. The
clique embedding power
of
H
is
\clemb(H):=\sup_{k\geq 3}\max_{\psi\colon K_{k}\to H}\frac{k}{\wed(\psi)}
, where the maximum is taken over all clique embeddings
\psi
from
K_{k}
to
H
.

When describing clique embeddings, for
\psi(u)=B
we say that node
u
is embedded into the set
B
. We will see that it is often advantageous to embed multiple nodes of
K_{k}
into the same set
B
.
Since the nodes of a clique are indistinguishable, an embedding
\psi
is given by a multiset
\{\psi(u)\mid u\in V(K_{k})\}
. In particular, it is not important
which
nodes are mapped to a set
B
, but
how many
nodes are mapped to
B
. Let us see an example of a clique embedding:

(a)

Definition of the goggles graph.

(b)

Clique embedding for the goggles graph. Every number represents a subgraph into which one node is embedded.

Figure 3
:
Example of a clique embedding for the goggles graph.

Lemma 2.2
.

We define the
goggles graph

H_{\textup{gog}}
by taking a
4
-cycle
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{4})
and a
4
-cycle
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{4})
, identifying
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
and connecting
a_{3}
and
b_{3}
by an edge; see Figure
3(a)
.

We have
\clemb(H_{\textup{gog}})\geq 2
.

Proof 2.3
.

Consider the clique embedding
\psi
from
K_{4}
to
H_{\textup{gog}}
that embeds one node into each of the sets
\{b_{3}\}
,
\{a_{3}\}
,
\{b_{2},a_{1},a_{2}\}
, and
\{b_{4},a_{1},a_{4}\}
, see Figure
3(b)
. It can be easily checked that
\psi
is indeed a clique embedding (i.e., all chosen subgraphs are connected and all pairs of chosen subgraphs touch). It can also be checked that every edge of
H_{\textup{gog}}
sees at most two chosen subgraphs, so
\psi
has weak edge depth
2
. This yields
\clemb(H_{\textup{gog}})\geq\frac{4}{2}=2
.

Fan, Koutris, and Zhao
[
44
]
used clique embeddings to prove conditional lower bounds for min-weight subgraph finding (and generalizations to hypergraphs). Here we show that clique embeddings also yield analogous lower bounds for listing and enumeration.

Lemma 2.4

(Lower Bounds via clemb,
Section

4.1
)
.

Let
H
be a pattern and
\varepsilon>0
. Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph cannot be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{\clemb(H)-\varepsilon})
assuming the MinClique Hypothesis.
H
-Listing cannot be solved in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{\clemb(H)-\varepsilon}+t)
, and
H
-Enumeration cannot be solved in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{\clemb(H)-\varepsilon})
and delay
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
, assuming the ZeroClique Hypothesis.

Proof 2.5

(Proof Sketch)
.

For min-weight this was shown in
[
44
, Proposition 12]
.
For listing, there is a known reduction from detecting a triangle of total edge weight zero to triangle listing
[
78
]
. We generalize this reduction to start from detecting a
k
-clique of total edge weight zero and end at
H
-Listing.
For enumeration we use a simple reduction from listing.

We next show that induced minors have smaller or equal time complexity for min-weight, listing, and enumeration. The same holds for the clique embedding power.

Lemma 2.6

(Induced Minors for Subgraph Problems,
Section

4.2
)
.

Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be patterns such that
H^{\prime}
is an induced minor of
H
. Then for any functions
T,P,D
,

•

if Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph can be solved in time
T(m)
, then Min-Weight-
H^{\prime}
-Subgraph can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
,

•

if
H
-Listing can be solved in time
T(m,t)
, then
H^{\prime}
-Listing can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m,t))
,

•

if
H
-Enumeration can be solved in preprocessing time
P(m)
and delay
D(m)
, then
H^{\prime}
-Enumeration can be solved in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(P(m))
and delay
\mathcal{O}(D(m))
.

Lemma 2.7

(Induced Minors for clemb,
Section

4.2
)
.

Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be patterns such that
H^{\prime}
is an induced minor of
H
. Then
\clemb(H)\geq\clemb(H^{\prime})
.

This means that any lower bound for an induced minor
H^{\prime}
also gives a lower bound for
H
. Since the decomposition
D(H)
consists of induced minors of
H
, any lower bound for a graph in
D(H)
yields a lower bound for
H
.
It follows that it suffices to prove lower bounds for graphs without clique separators, since all graphs in
D(H)
have this property.

We next show that graphs without clique separators that are not a
P
-graph have clique embedding power at least 2, and thus complexity at least 2.

Lemma 2.8

(
Section

4.3
)
.

Let
H
be a pattern without clique separator. Then
\clemb(H)\geq 2
or
H
is a
P
-graph (i.e.,
H
is isomorphic to
P(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k})
for some integers
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}\geq 1
).

Proof 2.9

(Proof Sketch)
.

If
H
has an
H_{\textup{gog}}
induced minor then
\clemb(H)\geq 2
(by Lemmas
2.2
and
2.7
). Similarly, if
H
has a
K_{4}
minor then it is easy to see that
\clemb(H)\geq 2
. For a graph that has no clique separator and thus no cut vertex, it is known that
H
has no
K_{4}
minor if and only if
H
has treewidth at most 2 if and only if
H
is a series-parallel graph. Finally, we need to show that a series-parallel graph without a clique separator and an
H_{\textup{gog}}
induced minor is a
P
-graph.

Surprisingly, not all
P
-graphs admit truly subquadratic algorithms, but lower bounds based on clique embeddings are not sufficient to show this: We have
\clemb(P(3,3,3))=2-1/9
according to
[
44
, Section 7.1]
, but
P(3,3,3)
-Listing cannot be solved in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-\varepsilon}+t)
for any
\varepsilon>0
assuming the 3XOR hypothesis according to
[
53
, Lemma 35]
.

Here we replace the 3XOR hypothesis by the more standard 3SUM Hypothesis, and we prove analogous lower bounds for Min-Weight-
P(3,3,3)
-Subgraph and
P(3,3,3)
-Enumeration.

Lemma 2.10

(Lower bounds for P(3,3,3),
Section

4.4
)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Min-Weight-
P(3,3,3)
-Subgraph cannot be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\varepsilon})
assuming the MinConv Hypothesis.
P(3,3,3)
-Listing cannot be solved in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-\varepsilon}+t)
, and
P(3,3,3)
-Enumeration cannot be solved in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\varepsilon})
and delay
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
, assuming the 3SUM Hypothesis.

Thus, all
P
-graphs that have at least three paths of length at least three do not admit truly subquadratic algorithms.
We will argue that every
P
-graph that does not have at least three paths of length at least three and has no clique separator is one of our simple patterns in
\mathcal{P}
(recall
Definition

1.6
). All of these patterns indeed have complexity below 2.

It remains to prove lower bounds for graphs in
\mathcal{P}
. Here clique embeddings are sufficient.

Lemma 2.11

(
Section

5
)
.

For any
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
.

Proof 2.12

(Proof Sketch)
.

In our proof we present explicit clique embeddings for all graphs in
\mathcal{P}
. This is a long proof with several cases, due to the complicated definition of
\mathcal{F}
. See also
Appendix

A
, where we present our lower bound for the concrete example
P(9,2,2)
.

This finishes our lower bound journey.

2.2
Algorithms

To treat min-weight, listing, and enumeration in a unified way, we introduce an intermediate problem that we call
H
-Encoding. See
Section

3.2
for background on tree decompositions.

Definition 2.13

(Encoding Problem)
.

Let
H
be a pattern and
G=(V,E)
be a host graph with partitioning
V=\bigcup_{a\in V(H)}V_{a}
. A
partial
H
-encoding

\mathcal{E}
of
G
is a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
H
and for every bag
B
of
\mathcal{T}
a set
S_{B}\subseteq\prod_{a\in B}V_{a}
(which we call
submaterialization
). We say that an
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
is
encoded
by
\mathcal{E}
if we have
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B}\in S_{B}
for every bag
B
of
\mathcal{T}
. The
size
of
\mathcal{E}
is the total size of all sets
S_{B}
, summed over all bags
B
.

A (full)
H
-encoding
of
G
is a
collection of partial
H
-encodings
\mathcal{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that every
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
is encoded by exactly one
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. We require that the number of partial encodings
k
is bounded by a constant (i.e., it may depend on
H
but not on
G
).
The
size
of a full
H
-encoding is the sum of all sizes of its partial encodings
\mathcal{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

The
H
-Encoding problem
asks to compute a full
H
-encoding for a given host graph
G
.

Similar problems have appeared in the literature, e.g., the “Boolean tensor decomposition” from
[
55
]
(which differs in requiring that every
H
-subgraph is encoded by
at least
one
\mathcal{E}_{i}
).

H
-Encoding is a unifying problem by means of the following reduction lemma.

Lemma 2.14

(
Section

6
)
.

For any pattern
H
if
H
-Encoding is solvable in time
T(m)
then

•

Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
,

•

H
-Listing can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m)+t)
, where
t
is the output size, and

•

H
-Enumeration can be solved in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
and delay
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Hence, from now of we focus on designing algorithms for
H
-Encoding.
We first show that the complexity of Encoding is smaller or equal for induced minors, as for our main problems.

Lemma 2.15

(Induced Minors for Encoding,
Section

6
)
.

Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be patterns such that
H^{\prime}
is an induced minor of
H
. If
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
T(m)
, then
H^{\prime}
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.

A crucial property of Encoding is that it splits over a clique separator, i.e., we can combine algorithms for the parts induced by a clique separator to an algorithm for the full graph:

Lemma 2.16

(Encoding splits over Clique Separator,
Section

6
)
.

Let
H
be a pattern and
C
be a clique separator in
H
. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{k}\subset V(H)
be the connected components of
H-C
. If
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-Encoding can be solved in time
T(m)
for every
i\in[k]
, then
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.

Proof 2.17

(Proof Sketch)
.

We solve
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-Encoding on the corresponding parts of
G
and combine the resulting
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-encodings to an
H
-encoding of
G
. To this end, for each combination of partial
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-encodings we connect the corresponding tree decompositions of the subgraphs
H[S_{i}\cup C]
to a tree decomposition of
H
. This is always possible because
C
is a clique in
H[S_{i}\cup C]
and thus every tree decomposition of
H[S_{i}\cup C]
has a bag containing
C
; connecting these bags yields a tree decomposition of
H
.

Corollary 2.18

(
Section

6
)
.

For any pattern
H
, if
H^{\prime}
-Encoding can be solved in time
T(m)
for every
H^{\prime}\in D(H)
, then
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.

Thus, the complexity of
H
-Encoding is the maximum of the complexities of
H^{\prime}
-Encoding over all
H^{\prime}\in D(H)
. If
D(H)
contains a graph not in
\mathcal{P}
, then we know that the complexity is at least 2. It thus only remains to prove upper bounds on the complexity of graphs in
\mathcal{P}
.

Lemma 2.19

(
Section

7
)
.

For any
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)})
.

Proof 2.20

(Proof Sketch)
.

Algorithms for subgraph finding often follow the high-degree-low-degree idea of Alon, Yuster, and Zwick
[
9
]
: In a first step, the nodes of the host graph are split into high degree or low degree according to some threshold (this is known as degree splitting). More generally, one can partition all nodes into
\log n
parts depending on the interval
[2^{i},2^{i+1})
that their degree lies in (this is known as degree uniformization). It remains to solve subgraph finding with certain degree constraints in the host graph, which is solved in a second step by algorithmic ideas that exploit the facts that small-degree nodes have few neighbors and that there are few high-degree nodes.

We were not able to find optimal algorithms within this framework for all patterns in
\mathcal{P}
, so we had to go beyond the standard approach. Our new ingredient is a second level of high-degree-low-degree that could be called “hyper-degree splitting”: After the usual degree splitting, in some cases we can afford to enumerate a set of possible tuples
T\subseteq V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{\ell}
, i.e., we can compute a superset
T
of the projection of all
H
-subgraphs to
V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{\ell}
. We then consider the hyper-degree
\textup{deg}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell-1})=|\{v_{\ell}:(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in T\}|
, and we split the tuples
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell-1})
according to this hyper-degree into high degree and low degree.

In
Appendix

A
we present our algorithm for the concrete example
P(9,2,2)
. This shows our hyper-degree splitting in action, on an example where it seems necessary.
The full proof of the lemma is again long and has several cases, due to the complex definition of
\mathcal{F}
.

2.3
Proof of the Main Result

We are ready to prove our main result, assuming the lemmas stated in the last two sections.

Proof 2.21

(Proof of
Theorem

1.8
)
.

We start by showing that every
H^{\prime}\in D(H)
is a pattern, i.e.,
H^{\prime}
is connected and
|V(H^{\prime})|\geq 2
.
The first property follows from the facts that any disconnected graph has a clique separator of size zero, and graphs in
D(H)
have no clique separator.
For the second property, suppose for the sake of contradiction that
|V(H^{\prime})|=1
, and let
u\in V(H)
be such that
H^{\prime}=H[\{u\}]
.
Since
H
is connected and has at least two nodes, there exists a neighbor
v
of
u
in
H
. Since
H[\{u,v\}]
is a single edge and thus has no clique separator,
H^{\prime}=H[\{u\}]
is no maximal induced subgraph of
H
without clique separator, which contradicts
H^{\prime}\in D(H)
.
Hence, each
H^{\prime}\in D(H)
is a pattern.

For the first part of the theorem statement, assume that there is some
H^{\prime}\in D(H)
that is not isomorphic to any graph in
\mathcal{P}
. If
\clemb(H^{\prime})\geq 2
, then
\clemb(H)\geq 2
by
Lemma

2.7
, and thus we get the desired quadratic lower bounds by
Lemma

2.4
.
So we can assume
\clemb(H^{\prime})<2
. Note that
H^{\prime}
has no clique separator, as it is an element of
D(H)
. Thus,
Lemma

2.8
implies that
H^{\prime}
is a
P
-graph, i.e,
H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
P(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k})
for some integers
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}\geq 1
.
Suppose that
H^{\prime}
has at least three paths of length at least three. Then
H^{\prime}
(and thus
H
) has
P(3,3,3)
as an induced minor, so
we obtain the desired quadratic lower bounds by
Lemmas

2.10
and
2.6
.
So we can assume that
H^{\prime}
is a
P
-graph with at most two paths of length at least three and without clique separator. In this situation we show that
H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to a graph in
\mathcal{P}
, contradicting the choice of
H^{\prime}
.
Note that
H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
P(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k})
with
\ell_{3},\ldots,\ell_{k}\leq 2
.
If
k=1
, then
H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
P(\ell_{1})
. This graph has a clique separator of size one, unless
\ell_{1}=1
. So
H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
P(1)=P(1,0,0\times 2)\in\mathcal{P}
.
If
k=2
, then
H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
P(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
, and thus to the cycle of length
\ell:=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
. If
\ell=3
then
H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
P(2,1)=P(2,1,0\times 2)\in\mathcal{P}
, and if
\ell\geq 4
then
H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
P(\ell-2,2)=P(\ell-2,2,0\times 2)\in\mathcal{P}
.
If
k\geq 3
, then none of
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}
can be 1, as the path of length 1 would be a size-2 clique separator of
H^{\prime}
. Therefore,
H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
for some
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 2
and
\gamma\geq 1
, which is in
\mathcal{P}
.
This contradicts the choice of
H^{\prime}
.

For the second part of the theorem, assume that every
H^{\prime}\in D(H)
is isomorphic to some pattern in
\mathcal{P}
, i.e., up to isomorphism we have
D(H)=\{P(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1}\times 2),\ldots,P(\alpha_{k},\beta_{k},\gamma_{k}\times 2)\}
for some
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1}),\ldots,(\alpha_{k},\beta_{k},\gamma_{k})\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
Let
c(H):=2-1/\max_{i\in[k]}\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i})
. By
Lemma

2.19
,
P(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}\times 2)
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i})})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)})
for each
i
.
Hence, by
Corollary

2.18
,
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c(H)})
.

Lemma

2.14
now yields the desired upper bounds.
For the corresponding lower bounds, by
Lemma

2.11
we have
\clemb(P(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}\times 2))\geq 2-1/\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i})
for each
i
.
Since each
P(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}\times 2)
is an induced subgraph of
H
, by
Lemma

2.7
we obtain
\clemb(H)\geq\max_{i}\clemb(P(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}\times 2))\geq 2-1/\max_{i}\mathcal{F}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i})=c(H)
.
The desired lower bounds now follow from
Lemma

2.4
.

Organization

At this point it only remains to prove the lemmas stated in the last two sections.
In
Section

3
, we give preliminaries and prove
Lemma

1.4
.
We then prove the lemmas from
Section

2.1
in Sections
4
and
5
, and we prove the lemmas from
Section

2.2
in Sections
6
and
7
. These sections rely on various inequalities on
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
which are proven in
Section

8
.
In
Appendix

A
we present an example of our algorithms and lower bounds, which we recommend to read at this point.
In
Appendix

B
we discuss further related work, in particular the connection to join queries in database theory and submodular width.
In
Appendix

C
we present an efficient algorithm for computing the decomposition
D(H)
.

3
Preliminaries

All graphs in this paper are simple and undirected. In addition to standard notation from graph theory, we use the following notation. We write
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
and use
\mathbb{N}
to denote the set of positive integers.
For a vector
\overline{v}
we denote by
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B}
the projection to the entries with indices in
B
.
Furthermore, for a set
S
of vectors we denote by
\left.S\right|_{B}
the set
\{\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B}\mid\overline{v}\in S\}
.

Let
H
be a pattern and
G=(V,E)
be a host graph with partitioning
V=\bigcup_{a\in V(H)}V_{a}
. Let
H^{\prime}
be an induced subgraph of
H
. We denote by
G[H^{\prime}]
the subgraph of
G
induced by all nodes in
G
that are colored by nodes in
H^{\prime}
. More precisely,
G[H^{\prime}]:=G[\bigcup_{a\in V(H^{\prime})}V_{a}]
.

For
B\subseteq V(H)
, we call a set
S\subseteq\prod_{a\in B}V_{a}
a
materialization
of
B
if for every
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
we have
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B}\in S
.
We say that
B
is materialized if we found some materialization of
B
.

3.1
Fine-Grained Hypotheses

Let us introduce the hypotheses that our conditional lower bounds are based upon.

3SUM Hypothesis

In the 3SUM problem, given sets
A,B,C
of
n
integers the task is to decide whether there exist
a\in A,b\in B,c\in C
with
a+b=c
. This has a classic
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
-time algorithm
[
47
]
.
The 3SUM Hypothesis postulates that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
c\geq 0
such that the 3SUM problem on integers in the range
\{-n^{c},\ldots,n^{c}\}
cannot be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2-\varepsilon})
. This is one of the oldest fine-grained hypotheses, dating back to the 90s
[
47
]
, so lower bounds based on the 3SUM Hypothesis have a long history, see, e.g.,
[
47
,
68
,
59
,
3
,
2
,
52
,
33
]
.

MinConv Hypothesis

In the
(\min,+)
-Convolution problem, or MinConv problem for short, given sequences
A[1..n]
and
B[1..n]
the task is to compute the sequence
C[1..n]
with
C[k]=\min\{A[i]+B[k-i]:i\in[k]\}
, where out-of-bounds entries are interpreted as
\infty
. Naively this can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
.
The MinConv Hypothesis postulates that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
c\geq 0
such that MinConv with integer entries in the range
\{-n^{c},\ldots,n^{c}\}
cannot be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2-\varepsilon})
. This hypothesis has been frequently used to prove fine-grained lower bounds, see, e.g.,
[
61
,
35
,
26
,
50
,
58
,
32
]
.

It is known that the MinConv Hypothesis implies the 3SUM Hypothesis
[
61
,
35
]
. That is, all our lower bounds based on the 3SUM Hypothesis also hold assuming the MinConv Hypothesis (but phrasing a lower bound under the 3SUM Hypothesis gives a stronger result).

MinClique Hypothesis

The MinWeight-
k
-Clique problem is identical to the Min-Weight-
K_{k}
-Subgraph problem. Naively, the problem can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{k})
, where
n
is the number of nodes in the host graph.
For the purpose of fine-grained lower bounds, we restrict the edge weights to polynomially bounded integers, i.e., to integers in the range
\{-n^{c},\ldots,n^{c}\}
for some constant
c\geq 0
.
The MinClique Hypothesis postulates that there exist no
k\geq 3
and
\varepsilon>0
such that for all
c\geq 0
, MinWeight-
k
-Clique with edge weights in
\{-n^{c},\ldots,n^{c}\}
can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\varepsilon})
.

ZeroClique Hypothesis

The Zero-
k
-Clique problem is similar to the MinWeight-
k
-Clique problem, but instead of asking for the minimum total edge weight of a
k
-clique we ask whether there exists a
k
-clique with total edge weight 0. Again the naive running time is
\mathcal{O}(n^{k})
.
The ZeroClique Hypothesis postulates that there exist no
k\geq 3
and
\varepsilon>0
such that for all
c\geq 0
, Zero-
k
-Clique with edge weights in
\{-n^{c},\ldots,n^{c}\}
can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\varepsilon})
.

It is known that the MinClique Hypothesis implies the ZeroClique Hypothesis, see, e.g.,
[
1
]
. That is, all our lower bounds based on the ZeroClique Hypothesis also hold assuming the MinClique Hypothesis (but phrasing a lower bound assuming the ZeroClique Hypothesis gives a stronger result).
It is also known that the special case of the MinClique Hypothesis for
k=3
is implied by the All-Pairs-Shortest-Paths Hypothesis
[
76
]
. Similarly,
the special case of the ZeroClique Hypothesis for
k=3
, also called ZeroTriangle Hypothesis, is implied
by the 3SUM Hypothesis
[
77
]
and the All-Pairs-Shortest-Paths Hypothesis
[
76
]
.
For
k>3
, these hypotheses are natural generalizations, but are not known to be implied by any other fine-grained hypothesis.
Both the MinClique Hypothesis and the ZeroClique Hypothesis have been frequently used to prove fine-grained lower bounds, see, e.g.,
[
6
,
13
,
14
,
1
,
62
,
25
,
24
]
.

In this paper, whenever we talk about algorithms we always implicitly include randomized algorithms. In particular, all hypotheses are assumed to hold also for randomized algorithms.

3.2
Tree Decompositions

Let us define tree decompositions and collect some facts about them.
A
tree decomposition
of a graph
H
is a tree
T=([k],E_{T})
and sets
B(1),\ldots,B(k)\subseteq V(H)
(these sets are called
bags
) with the following properties. (1) Every node
v\in V(H)
is contained in some bag. (2) For every edge
\{u,v\}\in E(H)
some bag contains both
u
and
v
. And (3) for any
i,j\in[k]
and
v\in V(H)
, if both
B(i)
and
B(j)
contain
v
, then
v
is contained in
B(\ell)
for every node
\ell
on the unique path from
i
to
j
in
T
.

Note that if
H
has no isolated nodes then (1) is subsumed by (2). Property (3) implies that for any node
v\in V(H)
the bags containing
v
form a connected subgraph of
T
. We will use the following fact.

Lemma 3.1

(Clique Containment Lemma,
[
20
, Lemma 3.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a tree decomposition of a graph
H
and let
C\subseteq V(H)
be a clique in
H
.
Then for some bag
B
of
\mathcal{T}
we have
C\subseteq B
.

3.3
Series-Parallel Graphs

Any series-parallel graph is a graph
H
with a distinguished source node
s
and a distinguished sink node
t
. The family of all series-parallel graphs is defined as follows.

•

Single Edge.
The graph
H=(\{s,t\},\{\{s,t\}\})
with source
s
and sink
t
is series-parallel.

•

Series Composition.

Let
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
be series-parallel graphs with source nodes
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
and sink nodes
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, respectively. Construct graph
H
by taking the disjoint union of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
and identifying
t_{1}
with
s_{2}
. Then
H
with source
s_{1}
and sink
t_{2}
is series-parallel.

•

Parallel Composition.
Let
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
be series-parallel graphs with source nodes
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
and sink nodes
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, respectively. Construct graph
H
by taking the disjoint union of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
and identifying
s_{1}
with
s_{2}
and identifying
t_{1}
with
t_{2}
. Then
H
with source
s_{1}=s_{2}
and sink
t_{1}=t_{2}
is series-parallel.

3.4
Miscellaneous

In this section we prove statements that did not naturally fit in any other section, including
Lemma

1.4
from
Section

1.4
.

{observation}

Let
G
be a
k
-partite graph for
k\geq 2
with parts
V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{k}
. Let
m\geq|E(G)|
. Then for any
i\in[k]
and any
\Delta\geq 1
the number of nodes in
V_{i}
with degree at least
\Delta
is at most
m/\Delta
.

See
1.4

Proof 3.2
.

First consider the case that
C
is empty.
Hence,
H
is disconnected.
Note that any element of
D(H)
does not have a clique separator, and thus is connected.
Therefore, any
H^{\prime}\in D(H)
lies completely inside some connected component
S_{i}
of
H
.
It is easy to see that
H^{\prime}
is a maximal subgraph without a clique separator in
H[S_{i}]
if and only if it is a maximal subgraph without a clique separator in
H
.
Hence, we get
D(H)=\bigcup_{i\in[k]}D(H[S_{i}])=\bigcup_{i\in[k]}D(H[S_{i}\cup C])
.
It remains to consider the case
|C|\geq 1
.

We first show the inclusion “
\subseteq
”.
Consider any
U\subseteq V(H)
such that
H[U]\in D(H)
.
If
U\cap S_{i}\neq\emptyset
and
U\cap S_{j}\neq\emptyset
for some
i\neq j\in[k]
, then
U\cap C
is a clique separator in
H[U]
which contradicts
H[U]\in D(H)
.
Hence,
U\subseteq S_{i}\cup C
for some
i\in[k]
.
As
H[U]
is a maximal subgraph of
H
without a clique separator, it is also a maximal subgraph of
H[S_{i}\cup C]
without a clique separator, and thus
H[U]\in D(H[S_{i}\cup C])
.
Therefore,
D(H)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[k]}D(H[S_{i}\cup C])
.

We now show the inclusion “
\supseteq
”. Consider any
U\subseteq V(H[S_{i}\cup C])
such that
H[U]\in D(H[S_{i}\cup C])
for some
i\in[k]
.
Assume for the sake of contradiction that
H[U]
is not a maximal subgraph without a clique separator in
H
.
It is still a subgraph without a clique separator, so there is some
U^{\prime}\subseteq V(H)
such that
U\subsetneq U^{\prime}
and
H[U^{\prime}]\in D(H)
does not contain a clique separator.
As shown before, we have
U^{\prime}\subseteq S_{j}\cup C
for some
j\in[k]
.
If
U\not\subseteq C
, then
j=i
, and
U^{\prime}=U
because
H[U]
is a maximal subgraph in
H[S_{i}\cup C]
without a clique separator.
It follows that
H[U]=H[U^{\prime}]\in D(H)
.

Otherwise, we have
U\subseteq C
.
Since
C
is a clique, it does not have a clique separator in
H[S_{i}\cup C]
.
By maximality of
U
we have
U=C
.
From now on we can assume
U=C
and
H[U]\in D(H[S_{i}\cup C])
, and we want to show
H[U]\in D(H)
.
Note that every node
a\in C
is adjacent to at least one node of
S_{i}
as otherwise removal of
C\setminus\{a\}
from
H
would disconnect
a
from
S_{i}
which contradicts the fact that
C
is a minimal clique separator in
H
.

If
|C|=1
, let
a\in V(H)
be such that
C=\{a\}
. We have that
a
is adjacent to some
b\in S_{i}
. Since
\{a,b\}
does not have a clique separator, we get a contradiction as
C=U
is not a maximal subgraph of
H[S_{i}\cup C]
without a clique separator.
Thus, from now on we can assume
|C|\geq 2
.

Let
H^{\prime}\coloneqq(S_{i}\cup C,E(H[S_{i}\cup C])\setminus E(H[C]))
.
That is,
H^{\prime}
is the graph
H[S_{i}\cup C]
without the edges in
H[C]
.
Define
T
as a tree in
H^{\prime}
of minimum cardinality such that
C\subseteq V(T)
and each node of
C
has degree one in
T
.
In other words,
T
is a Steiner tree with
C
as a set of terminals such that all nodes of
C
are leaves in
T
.
Note that such a tree exists as
H[S_{i}]
is connected and each node of
C
is adjacent to some node in
S_{i}
.
We choose
T
as a minimal such tree.
Note that as
|C|\geq 2
and
H^{\prime}
does not have any edges between nodes in
C
, we have
C\subsetneq V(T)
.
We claim that
H[V(T)]
does not have a clique separator.
As
C
is a strict subset of
V(T)
, this leads to a contradiction with the fact that
C
is a maximal subgraph of
H[S_{i}\cup C]
without a clique separator.

It thus remains to prove the claim that
H[V(T)]
does not have a clique separator.
Assume for the sake of contradiction that
H[V(T)]
has a clique separator
C^{\prime}
.
In case
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
, the subtree
T[V(T)\setminus C^{\prime}]
is connected as we removed some leaves from
T
.
Hence,
H[V(T)\setminus C^{\prime}]
is connected which contradicts the fact that
C^{\prime}
is a clique separator in
H[V(T)]
.
It remains to consider the case
C^{\prime}\not\subseteq C
.
Let
a
be some node in
C^{\prime}\setminus C
.
Note that all nodes from
C\setminus C^{\prime}
(if any) are adjacent in
H[V(T)\setminus C^{\prime}]
.
Hence, there is some connected component of
H[V(T)\setminus C^{\prime}]
that contains all nodes from
C\setminus C^{\prime}
.
As
C^{\prime}
is a clique separator in
H[V(T)]
, there is some other connected component
S^{\prime}_{\ell}\subseteq S_{i}
of
H[V(T)\setminus C^{\prime}]
.
We build a new tree
T^{\prime}
in
H^{\prime}
such that
C\subseteq V(T^{\prime})
and each node of
C
has degree one in
T^{\prime}
such that
T^{\prime}
only contain nodes from
V(T)\setminus S^{\prime}_{\ell}
, thus contradicting the fact that
T
is such a tree of minimum cardinality.
To create
T^{\prime}
we first connect
a
with all other nodes from
C^{\prime}
by edges.
Then for each terminal
b\in C\setminus C^{\prime}
we consider the path from
b
to
a
in
T
, go along it and add edges of it to
T^{\prime}
until the current node is already in
T^{\prime}
.

At every point of this process,
T^{\prime}
is a connected tree with
V(T^{\prime})\subseteq V(T)
, and at the end it connects all terminals
C
. Furthermore, all nodes from
C
have degrees one in
T^{\prime}
.
On the other hand, we claim that
T^{\prime}
does not contain any nodes from
S^{\prime}_{\ell}
.
In the initial step we only added nodes from
C^{\prime}
to
T^{\prime}
where
S^{\prime}_{\ell}\cap C^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
For each terminal
b\in C
we do not visit any nodes from
S^{\prime}_{\ell}
on the path, because as
b
lies in a different connected component of
H[V(T)\setminus C^{\prime}]
, any path from
b
to some node in
S^{\prime}_{\ell}
goes through
C^{\prime}
(as it is a clique separator), and as soon as we enter
C^{\prime}
, we stop adding edges (or we stop even earlier).
Hence, indeed have
V(T^{\prime})\subseteq V(T)\setminus S^{\prime}_{\ell}
. This proves the claim and thus finishes the proof.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
H
be a pattern that has a clique separator and
\clemb(H)<2
. Then every minimal clique separator of
H
has cardinality one or two.

Proof 3.4
.

As
H
is a pattern, it is connected, and thus
H
does not have a clique separator of size zero.

If
H
has a clique separator of size at least four,
H
has an (induced)
K_{4}
minor.
The trivial embedding
\psi(v)=\{v\}
of
K_{4}
into
K_{4}
has weak edge depth
2
.
Together with
Lemma

2.7
, we thus arrive at the contradiction
\clemb(H)\geq\clemb(K_{4})\geq 4/2=2
.

It remains to show that no minimal clique separator of
H
has size three.
For the sake of contradiction assume that nodes
a,b
, and
c
form a minimal clique separator of size three in
H
.
Let
S_{1}
be one of the connected components of
H-\{a,b,c\}
.
If
a
is not adjacent to any node of
S_{1}
, then
\{b,c\}
is a clique separator in
H
because its removal disconnects nodes of
S_{1}
from
a
.
This would violate the fact that
\{a,b,c\}
is a minimal clique separator in
H
.
Thus,
a
is adjacent to some node in
S_{1}
.
Analogously,
b
and
c
are adjacent to some nodes in
S_{1}
.
Removing all nodes from
V(H)\setminus S_{1}\setminus\{a,b,c\}
and contracting
S_{1}
into a single node yields that
H
has a
K_{4}
minor, and thus
\clemb(H)\geq\clemb(K_{4})\geq 2
, which is a contradiction.

4
Lower Bounds: from General Case to Family
\mathcal{P}

In this section we prove
Lemmas

2.4
,
2.6
,
2.7
,
2.8
and
2.10
. This covers most of the lemmas from
Section

2.1
, except for
Lemma

2.11
whose proof is postponed to
Section

5
.

We start by proving that the ZeroClique Hypothesis implies a lower bound for the problem of listing cliques.

Hypothesis 4.1

(Clique Listing Hypothesis)
.

For any
k\geq 3
and
\varepsilon>0
, there is no algorithm that lists all
k
-cliques of a given
n
-node host graph
G
in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\varepsilon}+t)
, where
t
is the number of
k
-cliques in
G
.

Lemma 4.2
.

The ZeroClique Hypothesis implies the Clique Listing Hypothesis.

In the case of triangles, i.e,
k=3
, this lemma follows from the work of Vassilevska Williams and Xu
[
78
, Theorem 3.4]
. Here we essentially generalize the proof of
[
78
]
to the case
k>3
. However, their result also shows lower bounds for a related problem (AllEdgesTriangle) and bounds the number of edges in the constructed graph, both of which is not necessary for the result that we need to prove here, which allows to somewhat simplify their proof.

Proof 4.3
.

For the sake of contradiction assume that the Clique Listing Hypothesis does not hold, i.e., there exists an algorithm listing all
k
-cliques in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\varepsilon}+t)
for some
k\geq 3
and
0<\varepsilon<1
.
We use it to design an algorithm that, for a given host graph
G
with edge weights absolutely bounded by
n^{c}
, decides whether
G
has a
k
-clique of total weight zero in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\varepsilon^{\prime}})
for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
. This contradicts the ZeroClique Hypothesis.

For
100n^{k-\varepsilon}
times we pick random nodes from each part of
G
and check whether they form a zero-
k
-clique. If so, we return such a
k
-clique. Otherwise, we proceed to the main algorithm.

We first pick a prime number in
[100k^{2}n^{c},\Gamma k^{2}n^{c}]
for a large enough constant
\Gamma
.
By the prime number theorem, a random integer in this range is prime with probability
\Omega(1/\log n)
, so we can find such a prime number
p
in time
\mathcal{O}(\polylog n)
by checking random numbers from this range for primality.
After picking
p
, we view all edge weights in
G
as numbers in the prime field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
by reducing them modulo
p
.
Since all edges in
G
have weights in
[-n^{c},n^{c}]
, any
k
-clique in
G
has weight in
[-k^{2}n^{c},k^{2}n^{c}]
.
Since
p\geq 100k^{2}n^{c}
, the set of zero-
k
-cliques with respect to the new weights stays the same.

Pick a uniformly random number
x
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Let
G^{\prime}
be a copy of
G
, but all weights
w^{\prime}
of edges are multiplied by
x
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, i.e.,
w^{\prime}(e)=x\cdot w(e)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Note that for any
k
-clique
C
we have
w^{\prime}(C)=x\cdot w(C)
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Let
\rho\coloneqq\frac{\varepsilon}{3k^{2}}
. We split the numbers
\{0,1,\ldots,p-1\}
into
\ell\coloneqq\left\lfloor n^{\rho}\right\rfloor
contiguous ranges
R_{1},R_{2},\ldots,R_{\ell}
, so that each range has size
\left\lfloor p/\ell\right\rfloor
or
\left\lceil p/\ell\right\rceil
.
Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of all tuples
(R_{i_{e}})_{e\in E(K_{k})}
such that
0\in\sum_{e\in E(K_{k})}R_{i_{e}}
, where
i_{e}\in\{1,2,\ldots,\ell\}
for each
e
.
We compute
\mathcal{R}
by enumerating all possible tuples
(R_{i_{e}})_{e\in E(K_{k})}
and checking whether the resulting tuple satisfies
0\in\sum_{e\in E(K_{k})}R_{i_{e}}
. There are
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{|E(K_{k})|})\leq\mathcal{O}(n^{k^{2}\rho})=\mathcal{O}(n^{\varepsilon/3})
such tuples, and they can be enumerated in this time bound as well.

For each tuple
r=(R_{i_{e}})_{e\in E(K_{k})}\in\mathcal{R}
we construct the set of edges

E_{r}\coloneqq\bigcup_{\{a,b\}\in E(K_{k})}\{\{v_{a},v_{b}\}\in E(G)\mid v_{a}\in V_{a},v_{b}\in V_{b},w^{\prime}(\{v_{a},v_{b}\})\in R_{i_{\{a,b\}}}\}.

Let
G_{r}=(V(G),E_{r},w^{\prime})
be the subgraph of
G^{\prime}
with edge set
E_{r}
.
Observe that
G^{\prime}
has a zero-
k
-clique if and only if at least one of the graphs
G_{r}
has a zero-
k
-clique.

We list all
k
-cliques in each graph
G_{r}
for all
r\in\mathcal{R}
. For each listed
k
-clique, we check whether it is a zero-
k
-clique in
G
, and if so, we return it.

Correctness of the presented algorithm is immediate: Since we check the output to be a zero-
k
-clique in
G
, if we return a clique, then it is guaranteed to be a zero-
k
-clique, so we never return a false positive.
Moreover, for any zero-
k
-clique
C
in
G
, we have
w^{\prime}(C)=x\cdot w(C)=0
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, so
C
is also a zero-
k
-clique in
G^{\prime}
, and thus a zero-
k
-clique in some graph
G_{r}
for some
r\in\mathcal{R}
.
Since we list all
k
-cliques in
G_{r}
, in particular we list
C
.
Thus, if
G
has any zero-
k
-cliques, the algorithm will return one.

It remains to analyze the running time of the algorithm.
If the number
t
of zero-
k
-cliques in
G
is at least
n^{2\varepsilon}
, a collection of random nodes picked in the first stage of the algorithm is a zero-
k
-clique with probability at least
n^{2\varepsilon-k}
.
Hence, by repeating the procedure
100n^{k-\varepsilon}
time we find a zero-
k
-clique with probability at least
1-(1-n^{2\varepsilon-k})^{100n^{k-\varepsilon}}=1-\mathcal{O}(e^{-n^{\varepsilon}})
.
Thus, in this case we continue to the main stage of the algorithm with probability
\mathcal{O}(e^{-n^{\varepsilon}})
.

Note that since
|R_{e}|=\mathcal{O}(p/n^{\rho})
, the constraint
0\in\sum_{e\in E(K_{k})}R_{i_{e}}
for a tuple
(R_{i_{e}})_{e\in E(K_{k})}
to be in
\mathcal{R}
implies that
\sum_{e\in E(K_{k})}R_{i_{e}}\subseteq[-\mathcal{O}(p/n^{\rho}),\mathcal{O}(p/n^{\rho})]
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Thus, if a
k
-clique
C
appears as a
k
-clique of one of graphs
G_{s}
, then
w^{\prime}(C)\in[-\mathcal{O}(p/n^{\rho}),\mathcal{O}(p/n^{\rho})]
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
If
C
is not a zero-
k
-clique, then
w^{\prime}(C)=x\cdot w(C)
is uniformly distributed in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, since
p
is prime and
x
a uniformly random number in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Hence,
w^{\prime}(C)\in[-\mathcal{O}(p/n^{\rho}),\mathcal{O}(p/n^{\rho})]
happens with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{-\rho})
.
It follows that if
t\leq n^{2\varepsilon}
, then the expected total number of listed cliques in all graphs
G_{r}
over all
r\in\mathcal{R}
is
\mathcal{O}(n^{2\varepsilon}+n^{k-\rho})=\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\rho})
, where the first summand counts the number of zero-
k
-cliques in
G
and the second counts the number of false positives.
The remaining case
t\geq n^{2\varepsilon}
is handled with high probability by the first step of the algorithm which samples random
k
-tuples of nodes and checks whether they are zero-
k
-cliques. Specifically, with high probability in this case we do not list any cliques (and with probability
\mathcal{O}(e^{-n^{\varepsilon}})
we list
O(n^{k})
cliques).
In both cases, the expected total number of listed cliques is
\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\rho})
.

Since
|\mathcal{R}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{\varepsilon/3})
, we get that the expected time the algorithm takes is
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\varepsilon}+n^{\varepsilon/3}\cdot n^{k-\varepsilon}+n^{k-\rho})\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\rho})
, where we use that the total output size of all calls to
k
-clique listing is
\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\rho})
in expectation.

With standard boosting methods we can turn this algorithm with expected running time into an algorithm that is correct with high probability.
Using Markov’s bound, by aborting this algorithm after time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\rho})
, we obtain a randomized algorithm running in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\rho})
that is correct with probability at least
0.9
.
Repeating this algorithm
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
times and returning a zero-
k
-clique if at least one repetition returned a zero-
k
-clique, we obtain a randomized algorithm running in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\rho})\leq\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\rho/2})
that is correct with high probability.
This contradicts the ZeroClique Hypothesis.

4.1
Lower Bounds Based on Clique Embeddings

The following is a helper lemma to prove lower bounds based on clique embeddings.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
H
be a pattern,
k\geq 3
be an integer, and
\psi:K_{k}\to H
be a clique embedding.
There is an algorithm that given a (weighted) host graph
G
for
K_{k}
-subgraph isomorphism with
|V(G)|=n
computes a (weighted) host graph
G^{\prime}
for
H
-subgraph isomorphism with
|E(G^{\prime})|=\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})
in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})
, such that there is a (weight-preserving) constant-time-computable bijection between
K_{k}
-subgraphs in
G
and
H
-subgraphs in
G^{\prime}
.

Proof 4.5
.

It suffices to prove the weighted case of the lemma, as the unweighted case follows from setting all weights to zero. The proof is a rather standard embedding.

Choose a function
f:E(K_{k})\to E(H)
that maps every edge
\{x,y\}
of
K_{k}
to an arbitrary edge
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
such that
x,y\in\psi^{-1}(a)\cup\psi^{-1}(b)
.
Such a function
f
exists, because for any
\{x,y\}\in E(K_{k})
the subgraphs induced by
\psi(x)
and
\psi(y)
touch. That is, there is an edge
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
with
a\in\psi(x)
and
b\in\psi(y)
, or there is a node
a\in V(H)
with
a\in\psi(x)\cap\psi(y)
. In the former case we can choose
f(\{x,y\})=\{a,b\}
, in the latter case we pick an arbitrary neighbor
b
of
a
in
H
and set
f(\{x,y\})=\{a,b\}
, where we used that
H
is connected and
|V(H)|\geq 2
since
H
is a pattern.

Denote the partitioning of
G
by
V_{x}
for
x\in V(K_{k})
. We assume that the edges of
G
are stored in an adjacency matrix, so given two nodes we can query whether there is an edge between them and, if so, query its weight in constant time. Reading the adjacency matrix of
G
takes time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})\leq\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi})
. Here,
\wed(\psi)\geq 2
follows from the fact that any two embedded subgraphs
\psi(x),\psi(y)
touch, so there is some edge
e\in E(H)
with
x,y\in\psi^{-1}(e)
, and thus the depth of
e
is at least two.

We construct the graph
G^{\prime}
as follows. The nodes of
G^{\prime}
consist of parts
V^{\prime}_{a}
for
a\in V(H)
.
For each
a\in V(H)
, we put a node in
V^{\prime}_{a}
for each tuple of nodes in
\prod_{x\in\psi^{-1}(a)}V_{x}
. That is, each node in
V^{\prime}_{a}
corresponds to a tuple of nodes from the parts of
G
corresponding to nodes of
K_{k}
that are embedded into
a
by
\psi
. For a node
v_{a}\in V^{\prime}_{a}
, denote by
v_{a,x}:=v_{a}|_{x}
the node from
V_{x}
that
v_{a}
encodes for
x\in\psi^{-1}(a)
.
To construct the edges of
G^{\prime}
, for each edge
e=\{a,b\}\in E(H)
and all nodes
v_{a}\in V^{\prime}_{a}
and
v_{b}\in V^{\prime}_{b}
, we add the edge
\{v_{a},v_{b}\}
to
E(G^{\prime})
if and only if
C\coloneqq\bigcup_{x\in\psi^{-1}(a)}\{v_{a,x}\}\cup\bigcup_{x\in\psi^{-1}(b)}\{v_{b,x}\}
forms a clique in
G
. In particular, since there are no edges in
G
within a part, this condition checks that
v_{a,x}=v_{b,x}
for every
x\in\psi^{-1}(a)\cap\psi^{-1}(b)
.
In case we put an edge, we set its weight to
\sum_{\{x,y\}\in f^{-1}(e)}w(\{v_{a,x},v_{b,y}\})
, where
w
is the weight function of edges in
G
.

We now prove that there is a constant-time-computable weight-preserving one-to-one correspondence between
K_{k}
-subgraphs in
G
and
H
-subgraphs in
G^{\prime}
. Fix a
k
-clique
C
in
G
. For each
x\in V(K_{k})
, denote by
C_{x}
the node of
C
from
V_{x}
. Consider nodes
v_{a}=C|_{\psi^{-1}(a)}\in V^{\prime}_{a}
for all
a\in V(H)
. We claim that
I(C)\coloneqq\overline{v}\coloneqq(v_{a})_{a\in V(H)}
forms an
H
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
, and its weight is the same as the weight of
C
. Indeed, for every edge
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
we have
\{v_{a},v_{b}\}\in E(G^{\prime})
since the subset
\bigcup_{x\in\psi^{-1}(a)}\{v_{a,x}\}\cup\bigcup_{x\in\psi^{-1}(b)}\{v_{b,x}\}=\bigcup_{x\in\psi^{-1}(a)\cup\psi^{-1}(b)}\{C_{x}\}\subseteq C
forms a clique in
G
. Furthermore, the weight of the subgraph formed by
\overline{v}
is
\sum_{e\in E(H)}\sum_{\{x,y\}\in f^{-1}(e)}w(\{C_{x},C_{y}\})=\sum_{\{x,y\}\in E(K_{k})}w(\{C_{x},C_{y}\})
, which is the weight of
C
, because the function
f
embeds every edge into exactly one summand. Thus, every
K_{k}
-subgraph in
G
corresponds to an
H
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
of the same weight.
Furthermore, such a correspondence is an injection.
Indeed, let
C^{\prime}
be some other
k
-clique in
G
.
As
C\neq C^{\prime}
, we have
C_{x}\neq C^{\prime}_{x}
for some
x\in V(K_{k})
.
Pick some
a\in\psi(x)
.
It is easy to see that
I(C)
and
I(C^{\prime})
pick different nodes in
V^{\prime}_{a}
.
Hence,
I
is indeed an injection.

We now show the inverse correspondence. Fix an
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
in
G^{\prime}
. For each
a\in V(H)
, denote
v_{a}
the node of
\overline{v}
from
V^{\prime}_{a}
. Consider nodes
C_{x}\coloneqq v_{a}^{x}
for each
x\in V(K_{k})
for some
a\in\psi(x)
. We claim that
C\coloneqq(C_{x})_{x\in V(K_{k})}
forms a
k
-clique in
G
and
I(C)=\overline{v}
.

First, we show that
v_{b}^{x}=C_{x}
for all
b\in\psi(x)
. Let
a
be the node in
\psi(x)
, for which
C_{x}
was defined as
v_{a}^{x}
. Thus,
v_{a}^{x}=C_{x}
. By the definition of a clique embedding,
H[\psi(x)]
is connected, thus there exists a path
a=d_{1}\leftrightline d_{2}\leftrightline\dots\leftrightline d_{i}=b
between
a
and
b
in
H
, such that all nodes of this path are from
\psi(x)
. If we now consider
v_{d_{1}},v_{d_{2}},\ldots,v_{d_{i}}
, such nodes form a path in
G^{\prime}
because
\overline{v}
is an
H
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
. For each
j\in[i-1]
, an edge between
v_{d_{j}}
and
v_{d_{j+1}}
was added to
G^{\prime}
only if
v_{d_{j}}
and
v_{d_{j+1}}
represent the same nodes in parts
V_{y}
for all
y\in\psi^{-1}(d_{j})\cap\psi^{-1}(d_{j+1})
. In particular, they represent the same node in
V_{x}
. Thus, by transitivity
v_{d_{1}}
and
v_{d_{i}}
represent the same node in
V_{x}
, so
v_{b}^{x}=v_{d_{i}}^{x}=v_{d_{1}}^{x}=v_{a}^{x}=C_{x}
, proving the claim.
Thus, indeed
I(C)=\overline{v}
.
Consider some
x\neq y\in V(K_{k})
.
By the definition of a clique embedding, there is an edge
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
such that
x,y\in\psi^{-1}(\{a,b\})
.
As
\overline{v}
forms an
H
