-subgraph in
G
, we have that
v_{a}
and
v_{b}
are adjacent, and thus
C_{x}
and
C_{y}
are adjacent due to the definition of graph
G^{\prime}
.
Hence,
C
forms a
k
-clique in
G
.

Thus,
I
is an injection and a surjection.
Furthermore, computation of
I
and
I^{-1}
can be straightforwardly done in constant time.

It remains to analyze the time complexity of building graph
G^{\prime}
. We first build its nodes. For each
a\in H
, we create
\prod_{x\in\psi^{-1}(a)}|V_{x}|\leq n^{|\psi^{-1}(a)|}
nodes in
V^{\prime}_{a}
. For any edge
\{a,b\}\in H
, weak edge depth of
\{a,b\}
is at least
|\psi^{-1}(a)|
, thus
|\psi^{-1}(a)|\leq\wed(\psi)
.
Thus, nodes of
G^{\prime}
can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})
. Then for each
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
, we create edges between
V^{\prime}_{a}
and
V^{\prime}_{b}
. We fix a node
v_{a}\in V^{\prime}_{a}
, and to generate all
v_{b}\in V^{\prime}_{b}
with
v_{b}^{x}=v_{a}^{x}
for all
x\in\psi^{-1}(a)\cap\psi^{-1}(b)
, we generate all tuples of nodes from
\prod_{x\in\psi^{-1}(b)\setminus\psi^{-1}(a)}V_{x}
and generate a node
v_{b}
that encodes them and
v_{a}^{x}
for all
x\in\psi^{-1}(a)\cap\psi^{-1}(b)
. In total, this process works in time
\mathcal{O}(\prod_{x\in\psi^{-1}(\{a,b\})}|V_{x}|)\leq\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})
. For a fixed pair of nodes
v_{a}\in V^{\prime}_{a}
and
v_{b}\in V^{\prime}_{b}
, it takes constant time to decide whether an edge should be created and what should be its weight. Thus, in total it takes time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})
to build
G^{\prime}
.

Despite the supremum in the definition of clique embeddings, it is known that the clique embedding power is always attained by an embedding from a finite clique
K_{k}
:

Lemma 4.6

(
[
44
, Proposition 16]
)
.

For any pattern
H
, there is some
k
and a clique embedding
\psi:K_{k}\to H
, such that
\clemb(\psi)=\clemb(H)
.

We can now turn clique embeddings into conditional lower bounds for subgraph problems.

See
2.4

Proof 4.7

(Proof of
Lemma

2.4
)
.

We start with the claim on Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph. For the sake of contradiction assume that there is an algorithm that solves Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{\clemb(H)-\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
. According to
Lemma

4.6
, there is some
k
and an embedding
\psi:K_{k}\to H
, such that
\clemb(\psi)=\clemb(H)
. We assume that
\clemb(H)>1
as otherwise the claim is obvious, since Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph is not solvable in sublinear time. We now prove that Min-Weight-
K_{k}
-Subgraph is solvable in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\varepsilon^{\prime}})
for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, contradicting the MinClique Hypothesis.
Given an instance
G
of Min-Weight-
K_{k}
-Subgraph with
|V(G)|=n
, we use
Lemma

4.4
to build an instance
G^{\prime}
of Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph with
|E(G^{\prime})|=\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})
in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})
, such that there is a weight-preserving constant-time-computable bijection between
K_{k}
-subgraphs in
G
and
H
-subgraphs in
G^{\prime}
.
Thus, solving Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph on
G^{\prime}
in time
\mathcal{O}(|E(G^{\prime})|^{\clemb(H)-\varepsilon})=\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)\cdot(\clemb(\psi)-\varepsilon)})=\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\wed(\psi)\cdot\varepsilon})
, we solve Min-Weight-
K_{k}
-Subgraph on
G
in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})+\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\wed(\psi)\cdot\varepsilon})=\mathcal{O}(n^{k-\varepsilon^{\prime}})
for
\varepsilon^{\prime}\coloneqq\min\{k-\wed(\psi),\wed(\psi)\cdot\varepsilon\}
.
Since
\clemb(\psi)>1
we have
\wed(\psi)<k
and thus
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, contradicting the MinClique Hypothesis.

For proving the claim on
H
-Listing, by
Lemma

4.2
we may assume the Clique Listing Hypothesis.
For the sake of contradiction assume that there is an algorithm that solves
H
-Listing in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{\clemb(H)-\varepsilon}+t)
for some
\varepsilon>0
, where
t
is the number of
H
-subgraphs in the host graph. According to
Lemma

4.6
, there exists some
k
and an embedding
\psi:K_{k}\to H
, such that
\clemb(\psi)=\clemb(H)
. We assume that
\clemb(H)>1
as otherwise the lemma claim is obvious, since
H
-Listing is not solvable in sublinear time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{1-\varepsilon}+t)
. We now prove that
K_{k}
-Listing is solvable in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\varepsilon^{\prime}}+t^{\prime})
for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, where
t^{\prime}
is the number of
k
-cliques in the host graph, which contradicts the Clique Listing Hypothesis.
Given an instance
G
of
K_{k}
-Listing with
|V(G)|=n
, we use
Lemma

4.4
(in the unweighted setting) to create an instance
G^{\prime}
of
H
-Listing with
|E(G^{\prime})|=\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})
in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})
, such that there is a constant-time-computable bijection between
K_{k}
-subgraphs in
G
and
H
-subgraphs in
G^{\prime}
.
Thus, solving
H
-Listing on
G^{\prime}
in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(|E(G^{\prime})|^{\clemb(H)-\varepsilon}+t)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{\wed(\psi)\cdot(\clemb(\psi)-\varepsilon)}+t)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\wed(\psi)\cdot\varepsilon}+t)
, we solve
K_{k}
-Listing on
G
in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\wed(\psi)})+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\wed(\psi)\cdot\varepsilon}+t)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{k-\varepsilon^{\prime}}+t)
for
\varepsilon^{\prime}\coloneqq\min\{k-\wed(\psi),\wed(\psi)\cdot\varepsilon\}
, where again
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
. This contradicts the Clique Listing Hypothesis, and thus the ZeroClique Hypothesis.

Now consider the claim on
H
-Enumeration. For the sake of contradiction assume that there is an algorithm that solves
H
-Enumeration in
\mathcal{O}(m^{\clemb(H)-\varepsilon})
preprocessing time and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
delay for some
\varepsilon>0
.
We can then use this algorithm to solve
H
-Listing in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{\clemb(H)-\varepsilon}+t)
, where
t
is the number of
H
-subgraphs in the host graph, by enumerating all
H
-subgraphs in the host graph. By the second claim of the lemma this contradicts the ZeroClique Hypothesis.

4.2
Lower Bounds Based on Induced Minors

For induced minors, we prove a similar helper lemma as for clique embeddings (cf.
Lemma

4.4
).

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
H
and
H^{\prime}
be patterns, where
H^{\prime}
is an induced minor of
H
. Let
G^{\prime}
be a (weighted)
m
-edge host graph for pattern
H^{\prime}
.
There is an algorithm that in time
\mathcal{O}(m)
builds a (weighted) host graph
G
for pattern
H
such that
|V(G)|=\mathcal{O}(m)
,
|E(G)|=\mathcal{O}(m)
, and there is a (weight-preserving) constant-time-computable bijection
I
between
H
-subgraphs in
G
and
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs in
G^{\prime}
.

Proof 4.9
.

Again it suffices to prove the lemma in the weighted setting, as in the unweighted setting we can set all weights to zero. The proof is similar to
Lemma

4.4
.

As
H^{\prime}
is an induced minor of
H
, there exists a function
\varphi:V(H^{\prime})\to 2^{V(H)}
, such that nodes of
H^{\prime}
are mapped to disjoint non-empty connected subsets of nodes of
H
, where
\{x,y\}\in E(H^{\prime})
for
x\neq y
if and only if
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
for some
a\in\varphi(x)
and
b\in\varphi(y)
. Pick an arbitrary such function
\varphi
and a function
f:E(H^{\prime})\to E(H)
that maps each
\{x,y\}\in E(H^{\prime})
to some
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
with
a\in\varphi(x)
and
b\in\varphi(y)
.
Denote by
\varphi^{-1}:V(H)\to V(H^{\prime})\cup\{\bot\}
a function that maps each node
a
of
H
to the only node
x
of
H^{\prime}
, such that
a\in\varphi(x)
, or
\bot
if no such node
x
exists. As sets
\varphi(x)
are disjoint,
\varphi^{-1}
is uniquely defined.

Denote the parts of
G^{\prime}
by
V^{\prime}_{x}
for
x\in V(H^{\prime})
.

We construct the graph
G
as follows. For each
a\in V(H)
we construct a part
V_{a}
in
G
.
If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=x
for some
x\in V(H^{\prime})
, then
V_{a}
consists of
|V^{\prime}_{x}|
nodes that are in a one-to-one correspondence with nodes of
V^{\prime}_{x}
. We denote the nodes in
V^{\prime}_{x}
by
v_{x,i}
for
i\in[|V^{\prime}_{x}|]
, and the nodes in
V_{a}
by
v_{a,i}
for
i\in[|V^{\prime}_{x}|]
.
Otherwise we have
\varphi^{-1}(a)=\bot
, in this case
V_{a}
consists of a single node
v_{a,1}
.
To construct the edges of
G
, for each
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
, we add edges between
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
as follows.
If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=\bot
or
\varphi^{-1}(b)=\bot
, we add edges of weight zero between all pairs of nodes in
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
. If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=x=\varphi^{-1}(b)
for some
x\in V(H^{\prime})
, we add edges of weight zero between
v_{a,i}
and
v_{b,i}
for all
i\in[|V^{\prime}_{x}|]
.
If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=x\neq y=\varphi^{-1}(b)
for some
x,y\in V(H^{\prime})
, for each edge
\{v^{\prime}_{x,i},v^{\prime}_{y,j}\}\in E(G^{\prime})
between nodes in
V^{\prime}_{x}
and
V^{\prime}_{y}
, we add an edge
\{v_{a,i},v_{b,j}\}
to
G
. Furthermore, if
f(\{x,y\})=\{a,b\}
, we set the weight of the edge
\{v_{a,i},v_{b,j}\}
to the weight of the edge
\{v^{\prime}_{x,i},v^{\prime}_{y,j}\}
, otherwise we set it to zero.

We now prove that
|V(G)|,|E(G)|=\mathcal{O}(m)
. In each part
V_{a}
, we have either a single node or
|V^{\prime}_{x}|=\mathcal{O}(m)
nodes for
x=\varphi^{-1}(a)
. Thus, there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
nodes in
G
in total. Regarding edges, if
\varphi^{-1}(a)=\bot
or
\varphi^{-1}(b)=\bot
, either
V_{a}
or
V_{b}
consists of a single node, and we add
\mathcal{O}(|V_{a}|+|V_{b}|)=\mathcal{O}(m)
edges between these parts. If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=x=\varphi^{-1}(b)
, we add
|V^{\prime}_{x}|=\mathcal{O}(m)
edges between these parts. If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=x\neq y=\varphi^{-1}(b)
, the number of edges we add is equal to the number of edges between
V^{\prime}_{x}
and
V^{\prime}_{y}
in
G^{\prime}
, and thus is
\mathcal{O}(m)
. Hence, there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
edges in
G
in total. It is easy to see that
G
can also be constructed in time
\mathcal{O}(m)
.

We now show that there is a weight-preserving constant-time-computable bijection between
H
-subgraphs in
G
and
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs in
G^{\prime}
. Fix an
H^{\prime}
-subgraph
\overline{u}^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
. For each
x\in V(H^{\prime})
, denote by
u^{\prime}_{x}
the node of
\overline{u}^{\prime}
from
V^{\prime}_{x}
. For any
a\in V(H)
, if
x=\phi^{-1}(a)
then we have
u^{\prime}_{x}=v_{x,i}
for some
i
, and we set
u_{a}:=v_{a,i}
. Otherwise we have
\varphi^{-1}(a)=\bot
and we set
u_{a}:=v_{a,1}
. We claim that
I(\overline{u}^{\prime})\coloneqq\overline{u}\coloneqq(u_{a})_{a\in V(H)}
forms an
H
-subgraph in
G
and its weight is the same as the weight of
\overline{u}^{\prime}
.
Indeed, consider some edge
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
.
If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=\bot
or
\varphi^{-1}(b)=\bot
, all pairs of nodes in
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
are connected, in particular,
u_{a}
and
u_{b}
. If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=x=\varphi^{-1}(b)
,
u_{a}=v_{a,i}
and
u_{b}=v_{b,i}
, where
u^{\prime}_{x}=v_{x,i}
, and thus they are connected by an edge. If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=x\neq y=\varphi^{-1}(b)
,
u_{a}
and
u_{b}
are connected by an edge as they are nodes in these parts corresponding to
u^{\prime}_{x}
and
u^{\prime}_{y}
respectively, and they are connected by an edge in
G^{\prime}
due to the definition of
\varphi
and the fact that
\overline{u}^{\prime}
forms an
H^{\prime}
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, for each
\{x,y\}\in E(H)
, the weight of
\{u^{\prime}_{x},u^{\prime}_{y}\}
is embedded into exactly one edge on
\overline{u}
, specifically it is embedded in the weight of
\{u_{a},u_{b}\}
where
\{a,b\}=f(\{x,y\})
. Thus, every
H^{\prime}
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
corresponds to an
H
-subgraph in
G
of the same weight.
Furthermore, the mapping
I
is an injection.
Indeed, let
\overline{u}^{\prime\prime}
be some other
H^{\prime}
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
.
As
\overline{u}^{\prime}\neq\overline{u}^{\prime\prime}
, we have
u^{\prime}_{x}\neq u^{\prime\prime}_{x}
for some
x\in V(H^{\prime})
.
Pick some
a\in\varphi(x)
.
As
u^{\prime}_{x}\neq u^{\prime\prime}_{x}
,
I(\overline{u}^{\prime})
and
I(\overline{u}^{\prime\prime})
pick different nodes in
V_{a}
.
Hence,
I
is indeed an injection.

We now show the inverse correspondence. Fix some
H
-subgraph
\overline{u}
in
G
. For each
a\in V(H)
, denote
u_{a}
the node of
\overline{u}
from
V_{a}
.
For each
x\in V(H^{\prime})
pick an
a\in\varphi(x)
, write
u_{a}=v_{a,i}
for some
i
, and set
u^{\prime}_{x}\coloneqq v^{\prime}_{x,i}
. We claim that
\overline{u}^{\prime}\coloneqq(u^{\prime}_{x})_{x\in V(H^{\prime})}
forms an
H^{\prime}
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
and
I(\overline{u}^{\prime})=\overline{u}
.

First, we prove that
u_{b}=v_{b,i}
for all
b\in\varphi(x)
, where
u^{\prime}_{x}=v^{\prime}_{x,i}
. Let
a
be the node that we picked in
\varphi(x)
to set
u^{\prime}_{x}
. Thus,
u_{a}=v_{a,i}
. By the definition of
\varphi
,
H[\varphi(x)]
is connected, thus there exists a path
a=d_{1}\leftrightline d_{2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline d_{i}=b
between
a
and
b
in
H
, such that all nodes of this path are from
\varphi(x)
. If we now consider
u_{d_{1}},u_{d_{2}},\ldots,u_{d_{i}}
, such nodes form a path in
G
because
\overline{u}
is an
H
-subgraph in
G
. For each
j\in[i-1]
, an edge between
u_{d_{j}}
and
u_{d_{j+1}}
exists if and only if
u_{d_{j}}=v_{d_{j},k}
and
u_{d_{j+1}}=v_{d_{j+1},k}
for some
k
because
\varphi^{-1}(d_{j})=x=\varphi^{-1}(d_{j+1})
. Thus, by transitivity
u_{d_{1}}=u_{a}=v_{a,k}
and
u_{d_{i}}=u_{b}=v_{b,k}
for some
k
. But as
u_{a}=v_{a,i}
, we get that
k=i
, thus proving the claim. Furthermore, for each
a\in V(H)
, such that
\varphi^{-1}(a)=\bot
,
\overline{u}
chooses the only available node of
V_{a}
.
Thus, indeed
I(\overline{u}^{\prime})=\overline{u}
.
Consider some
\{x,y\}\in E(H^{\prime})
.
By the definition of
\varphi
, there is an edge
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
such that
a\in\varphi(x)
and
b\in\varphi(y)
.
As
\overline{u}
forms an
H
-subgraph in
G
, we have that
u_{a}
and
u_{b}
are adjacent, and thus
u^{\prime}_{x}
and
u^{\prime}_{y}
are adjacent due to the definition of graph
G
.
Hence,
\overline{u}^{\prime}
forms a
H^{\prime}
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
.

Thus,
I
is an injection and a surjection.
Furthermore, computation of
I
and
I^{-1}
can be straightforwardly done in constant time.

See
2.6

Proof 4.10

(Proof of
Lemma

2.6
)
.

We first prove the claim for Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph. Given a host graph
G^{\prime}
for Min-Weight-
H^{\prime}
-Subgraph, we apply
Lemma

4.8
to build a host graph
G
for Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph in time
\mathcal{O}(m)
. Given
G
, we solve Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
. From the solution for Min-Weight-
H
-Subgraph on
G
, in constant time we get the solution for Min-Weight-
H^{\prime}
-Subgraph on
G^{\prime}
. The overall time complexity is
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.

For
H
-Listing, we apply
Lemma

4.8
in the unweighted setting. Given a list of all
H
-subgraphs in
G
, we can convert each one of them into a corresponding
H^{\prime}
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
, which solves the problem in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m,t))
.

For
H
-Enumeration, we proceed similarly as for
H
-Listing. Whenever the enumeration algorithm generates an
H
-subgraph in
G
, we convert it into an
H^{\prime}
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
in constant time. The delay thus remains bounded by
\mathcal{O}(D(m))
.

See
2.7

Proof 4.11

(Proof of
Lemma

2.7
)
.

Let
\varphi,\varphi^{-1}
, and
f
be the same functions as in the proof of
Lemma

4.8
.

Fix some clique embedding
\psi^{\prime}:K_{k}\to 2^{V(H^{\prime})}
.
We create a clique embedding
\psi:K_{k}\to 2^{V(H)}
with
\wed(\psi)\leq\wed(\phi^{\prime})
.
Hence, we obtain
\clemb(H^{\prime})\leq\clemb(H)
by taking supremum over all
k
and all clique embeddings
\psi^{\prime}
.

We define
\psi
in the following way:

\psi(v)=\bigcup_{a\in\psi^{\prime}(v)}\varphi(a)

for every
v\in V(K_{k})
.

Denote
\psi^{\prime-1}(a)\coloneqq\{v\in V(K_{k})\mid a\in\psi^{\prime}(v)\}
for every
a\in V(H^{\prime})
.
Analogously,
\psi^{-1}(a)\coloneqq\{v\in V(K_{k})\mid a\in\psi(v)\}
for every
a\in V(H)
.

We first show that it is a valid clique embedding.
Note that
\psi(v)
is an image in
V(H)
of
\varphi
of some connected subset
\psi^{\prime}(v)
of nodes of
V^{\prime}(H)
.
An image of a connected subset of nodes in a minor is a connected subset of nodes in the graph.
Furthermore, as
\psi^{\prime}
is a clique embedding, for every
u,v\in V(K_{k})
with
u\neq v
, there is some edge
\{x,y\}\in E(H^{\prime})
, such that
\psi^{\prime}(v)\cap\{x,y\}\neq\emptyset
and
\psi^{\prime}(u)\cap\{x,y\}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\{a,b\}\coloneqq f(\{x,y\})
.
By the definition of
\psi
, we have
\psi(v)\cap\{a,b\}\neq\emptyset
and
\psi(u)\cap\{a,b\}\neq\emptyset
.
Thus,
\psi
is indeed a clique embedding.

It remains to show that
\wed(\psi)\leq\wed(\psi^{\prime})
.
Consider any edge
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
.
We claim that there is some edge
\{x,y\}\in E(H^{\prime})
such that
\psi^{-1}(a)\cup\psi^{-1}(b)\subseteq\psi^{\prime-1}(x)\cup\psi^{\prime-1}(y)
.
If
\bot\neq\varphi^{-1}(a)\neq\varphi^{-1}(b)\neq\bot
, there is an edge
\{x\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}(a),y\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}(b)\}\in E(H^{\prime})
as
H^{\prime}
is an induced minor of
H
. We have
\psi^{-1}(a)\subseteq\psi^{\prime-1}(x)
and
\psi^{-1}(b)\subseteq\psi^{-1}(y)
.
If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=\varphi^{-1}(b)
, we have
\psi^{-1}(a),\psi^{-1}(b)\subseteq\psi^{\prime-1}(x)
for
x\coloneqq\psi^{-1}(a)
.
As
H^{\prime}
is a pattern, there is some
y\in V(H^{\prime})
with
\{x,y\}\in E(H^{\prime})
.
If
\varphi^{-1}(a)=\bot
, we have
\psi^{-1}(a)=\emptyset
by the definition of
\psi
.
Such a case is similar to the case
\varphi^{-1}(a)=\varphi^{-1}(b)
.
The case of
\varphi^{-1}(b)=\bot
is symmetric.

As
\psi^{-1}(a)\cup\psi^{-1}(b)\subseteq\psi^{\prime-1}(x)\cup\psi^{\prime-1}(y)
, we get that the depth of
\{a,b\}
is at most the depth of
\{x,y\}
.
Hence, by taking maximum over all edges, we obtain
\wed(\psi)\leq\wed(\psi^{\prime})
.

4.3
Lower Bounds: from General Case to
P
-graphs

In this section we prove
Lemma

2.8
, which reduces general patterns to
P
-graphs.

Without defining treewidth, we will use the following two facts.
The first is a characterization of graphs of treewidth at most two in terms of forbidden minors, see also
[
70
]
. The next is a characterization of graphs of treewidth at most 2 in terms of series-parallel graphs.

Lemma 4.12

(
[
11
,
71
]
)
.

Let
H
be a graph. The treewidth of
H
is at most
2
if and only if
H
has no
K_{4}
minor.

Lemma 4.13

(
[
21
]
)
.

Let
H
be a graph. A
block
in
H
is a maximal connected subgraph of
H
that has no cut vertex. The treewidth of
H
is at most
2
if and only if every block of
H
is a series-parallel graph.

See
2.8

Proof 4.14

(Proof of
Lemma

2.8
)
.

Recall
Lemma

2.2
, in which we defined the goggles graph
H_{\textup{gog}}
and showed that
\clemb(H_{\textup{gog}})\geq 2
.
If
H
has an induced
H_{\textup{gog}}
minor, then we have
\clemb(H)\geq\clemb(H_{\textup{gog}})\geq 2
by
Lemma

2.7
.

If
H
has a
K_{4}
minor, it also has an induced
K_{4}
minor, and thus
\clemb(H)\geq\clemb(K_{4})
by
Lemma

2.7
. The trivial embedding
\psi(v)=\{v\}
of
K_{4}
into
K_{4}
has weak edge depth
2
, which shows
\clemb(H)\geq\clemb(K_{4})\geq 4/2=2
.

If
H
has no
K_{4}
minor, by
Lemmas

4.12
and
4.13
every block of
H
is a series-parallel graph.
Note that a cut vertex is a clique separator of size one.
Hence, as
H
has no clique separator,
H
is a series-parallel graph itself.

It remains to show that if
H
is a series-parallel graph with no clique separator and no induced
H_{\textup{gog}}
minor then it is a
P
-graph.

If
H
is a single edge, it is a
P
-graph
P(1)
. Otherwise, if the topmost composition in some series-parallel decomposition of
H
is series, the middle node of this series composition is a cut vertex.
Since
H
has no clique separator and thus no cut vertex, we may assume that in every series-parallel decomposition of
H
the topmost composition is parallel.
Out of all series-parallel decompositions of
H
, choose the one that has the largest number of parallel compositions on the topmost level.
Out of such decompositions choose the one where the distance between the source
s
and the sink
t
is maximized.

Let
H_{1},H_{2},\ldots,H_{k}
for
k\geq 2
be the subgraphs of
H
that constitute the topmost parallel composition, that is, (1)
H
arises by taking the disjoint union of
H_{1},\ldots,H_{k}
and identifying all their source nodes and identifying all their sink nodes, and (2) no
H_{i}
can be written as the parallel composition of series-parallel graphs.
See
Figure

4
.
We consider two cases.

Figure 4
:
Series-parallel graph
H
from
Lemma

2.8
decomposed into series parts.

•

Case 1:
s
and
t
are adjacent in
H
.

The edge between
s
and
t
induces one of the subgraphs
H_{i}
; without loss of generality let it be
H_{1}
.
If
k\geq 3
, then the edge
\{s,t\}
is a clique separator as its removal disconnects
H_{2}\setminus\{s,t\}
and
H_{3}\setminus\{s,t\}
, which are nonempty as there cannot be two edges between
s
and
t
.
Hence, we may assume
k=2
.
Let
H_{2}
be composed as series of two subgraphs
H_{2,1}
and
H_{2,2}
, and denote their common middle node by
q
(see
Figure

5
).
If
H_{2,1}
and
H_{2,2}
are single edges, the whole graph is a triangle, which is a
P
-graph
P(2,1)
.
Otherwise, assume that
H_{2,1}
is not a single edge (this is without loss of generality by symmetry).
In this case
s
and
q
are not adjacent, as otherwise the edge
\{s,q\}
would be a clique separator, as its removal disconnects
t
and
V(H_{2,1})\setminus\{s,q\}
(which is not empty since
H_{2,1}
is not a single edge).
We then build a different series-parallel decomposition of
H
, where
s
is the source,
q
is the sink, and
H_{2,1}
and
(V(H_{1})\cup V(H_{2,2}),E(H_{1})\cup E(H_{2,2}))
are series parts.
See
Figure

5
.
We arrive at a contradiction as we assumed that the series-parallel decomposition we picked maximizes the number of parallel compositions on the topmost level, and out of such maximizes the distance between the source and the sink.
The decomposition we picked has
k-1=1
series compositions on the topmost level, and the distance between the source and the sink is one as
s
and
t
are adjacent.
However, the new series-parallel decomposition has at least one series composition on the topmost level, and the distance between the source and the sink is at least two as
s
and
q
are not adjacent.
Therefore, in this case the only valid graph is
P(2,1)
.

Figure 5
:
Possible transformation of the series-parallel graph
H
from Case 1 of
Lemma

2.8
.

•

Case 2:
s
and
t
are not adjacent.

If
H
is not a
P
-graph with endpoints
s
and
t
, the parts
H_{1},\ldots,H_{k}
cannot all be paths, and thus for some part
H_{i}
its constructions requires a parallel composition;
without loss of generality let this part be
H_{1}
.
Then there are nodes
p,q
in
H_{1}
such that
H
is the series composition of a graph
H_{1,1}
with source
s
and sink
p
, a graph
H_{1,2}
with source
p
and sink
q
, and a graph
H_{1,3}
with source
q
and sink
t
, and the graph
H_{1,2}
is the parallel composition of two graphs
H_{1,2,1}
and
H_{1,2,2}
each with source
p
and sink
q
. See
Figure

6
. Note that here it may happen that
s
coincides with
p
, in which case the graph
H_{1,1}
consists of a single node
s=p
, or that
q
coincides with
t
, in which case the graph
H_{1,3}
consists of a single node
q=t
. However, both cannot happen at the same time, as otherwise
H_{1}
itself arises from a parallel composition, contradicting our choice of
H_{1},\ldots,H_{k}
as the subgraphs that constitute the topmost parallel composition.
Without loss of generality assume that
q\neq t
.
Note that
V(H_{1,2})\setminus\{p,q\}
is not empty as
H_{1,2}
arises from a parallel composition.
It follows that
p
and
q
are not adjacent, as otherwise the edge
\{p,q\}
would be a clique separator, as its removal disconnects
t
and
V(H_{1,2})\setminus\{p,q\}
.
We now build a new series-parallel decomposition of
H
, where
p
is the source,
q
is the sink, and
H
arises as the parallel composition of
H_{1,2}
and everything else (i.e., the second part is formed by
H_{1,1},H_{1,3},H_{2},\ldots,H_{k}
).
See
Figure

6
.
Note that the new decomposition consists of at least 3 parallel parts, as
H_{1,2}
arises by a parallel composition and thus consists of at least two parallel parts, and the remainder of the graph yields at least one additional parallel part.
Since we picked the original series-parallel decomposition to maximize the number of parallel parts, we obtain
k\geq 3
.

Figure 6
:
Possible transformation of the series-parallel graph
H
from Case 2 of
Lemma

2.8
.

We now turn our attention back to the original series-parallel decomposition, now with the additional knowledge that
k\geq 3
.
We claim that
H
has an induced
H_{\textup{gog}}
minor, which yields a contradiction.
See
Figure

7
.
We contract the graph
H_{1,1}
with source
s
and sink
p
into a single node, i.e., we contract the nodes
V(H_{1,1})
.
We contract the graph
H_{1,3}
with source
q
and sink
t
to a single edge, i.e., we contract the nodes
V(H_{1,3})\setminus\{q\}
.
For the parallel parts
H_{1,2,1}
and
H_{1,2,2}
that constitute the graph
H_{1,2}
with source
p
and sink
q
, we contract both of them to paths of length two, i.e., we contract the nodes
V(H_{1,2,1})\setminus\{p,q\}
and the nodes
V(H_{1,2,2})\setminus\{p,q\}
. This is possible because
p
and
q
are not adjacent.
Analogously, we contract
H_{2}
and
H_{3}
into two paths of length two between
s
and
t
; this is possible because
s
and
t
are not adjacent.
We delete all nodes in
V(H_{i})\setminus\{s,t\}
for
i\geq 4
. This yields an induced
H_{\textup{gog}}
minor. We thus arrive at a contradiction.

Figure 7
:
Induced
H_{\textup{gog}}
minor in
H
in Case 2 of
Lemma

2.8
. The graph
H
is shown in black. An induced
H_{\textup{gog}}
minor is shown in red.

In each case we showed that
H
is a
P
-graph or obtained a contradiction, which finishes the proof.

4.4
Lower Bounds for
P(3,3,3)

We now turn to the specific pattern graph
P(3,3,3)
. As discussed in the proof overview, clique embeddings do not suffice to show that this pattern has complexity at least 2. We therefore prove conditional lower bounds assuming the MinConv Hypothesis or the 3SUM Hypothesis, see
Section

3.1
for their definitions.

For MinConv we make use of the standard equivalence with MinConv Verification.

Hypothesis 4.15

(MinConv Verification Hypothesis)
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
c\geq 0
such that there is no algorithm that, given sequences
A[1..n],B[1..n]
, and
C[1..n]
with integer entries in the range
\{-n^{c},\ldots,n^{c}\}
, in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2-\varepsilon})
decides whether
C[k]\leq A[i]+B[j]
holds for all
i+j=k
.

Lemma 4.16

(
[
35
, Theorem 2]
)
.

The MinConv Hypothesis is equivalent to the MinConv Verification Hypothesis.

For our 3SUM-based lower bounds we make use of the following result that shows hardness of 3SUM Listing over small universe.
4
4

4

This result was known earlier, the specific reference
[
45
]
proves this result by a deterministic reduction.

Lemma 4.17

(
[
45
, Lemma 4.6]
)
.

For any
1<\mu<2
and
\varepsilon>0
, there is no algorithm that given a size-
n
3SUM instance
A\subseteq[n^{\mu}]
with
\mathcal{O}(n^{3-\mu})
solutions lists all solutions in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2-\varepsilon})
, unless the 3SUM Hypothesis fails.

See
2.10

Proof 4.18

(Proof of
Lemma

2.10
)
.

For the first claim, by
Lemma

4.16
, we may use the MinConv Verification Hypothesis instead of the MinConv Hypothesis.
We reduce MinConv Verification on sequences of length
n
to Min-Weight-
P(3,3,3)
-Subgraph on a host graph
G
with
\mathcal{O}(n)
nodes and edges in
\mathcal{O}(n)
time.

MinConv Verification asks us to check, given integer sequences
A,B
, and
C
of length
n
, whether there are indices
i,j
, and
k
such that
i+j=k
and
A[i]+B[j]<C[k]
. Define
s\coloneqq\left\lceil\sqrt{n}\right\rceil
. Every index
i
of
A
is uniquely represented as
i_{H}\cdot s+i_{L}
where
i_{L}\in[0,s-1]
. Similarly, every index
j
of
B
is uniquely represented as
j_{H}\cdot s+j_{L}
where
j_{L}\in[0,s-1]
.

Denote the two end-nodes of the paths of
P(3,3,3)
by
u_{H}
and
u_{L}
, the nodes of the first path by
u_{H},a_{H},a_{L},u_{L}
, the nodes of the second path by
u_{H},b_{H},b_{L},u_{L}
, and the nodes of the third path by
u_{H},c_{H},c_{L},u_{L}
. See
Figure

8
.

We create a host graph
G
as follows. The parts of
G
are
V_{a_{L}}=V_{a_{H}}=V_{b_{L}}=V_{b_{H}}=V_{c_{L}}=V_{c_{H}}=[0,2s]
and
V_{u_{L}}=V_{u_{H}}=\{(i,j)\mid i,j\in[0,s]\}
. The edges are constructed in the following way.
Nodes
(i,j)\in V_{u_{L}}
and
i^{\prime}\in V_{a_{L}}
are adjacent if and only if
i=i^{\prime}
.
Nodes
(i,j)\in V_{u_{L}}
and
j^{\prime}\in V_{b_{L}}
are adjacent if and only if
j=j^{\prime}
.
Nodes
(i,j)\in V_{u_{L}}
and
k^{\prime}\in V_{c_{L}}
are adjacent if and only if
i+j=k^{\prime}
.
We add analogous edges between
V_{u_{H}}
and
V_{a_{H}}
, between
V_{u_{H}}
and
V_{b_{H}}
, and between
V_{u_{H}}
and
V_{c_{H}}
.
All these edges are assigned weight zero.
Furthermore,
i_{H}\in V_{a_{H}}
and
i_{L}\in V_{a_{L}}
are adjacent if and only if
i_{L}\in[0,s-1]
and
i_{H}\cdot s+i_{L}\in[n]
, and the weight of this edge is
A[i_{H}\cdot s+i_{L}]
.
Similarly,
j_{H}\in V_{b_{H}}
and
j_{L}\in V_{b_{L}}
are adjacent if and only if
j_{L}\in[0,s-1]
and
j_{H}\cdot s+j_{L}\in[n]
, and the weight of this edge is
B[j_{H}\cdot s+j_{L}]
.
Finally,
k_{H}\in V_{c_{H}}
and
k_{L}\in V_{c_{L}}
are adjacent if and only if
k_{H}\cdot s+k_{L}\in[n]
, and the weight of this edge is
-C[k_{H}\cdot s+k_{L}]
.

Figure 8
:
Illustration of the Min-Weight-
P(3,3,3)
-Subgraph instance constructed in the proof of
Lemma

2.10
. The figure depicts the
P(3,3,3)
-subgraph corresponding to some triple
(A[i],B[j],C[k])
with
i+j=k
.

We claim that there is a constant-time-computable bijection between
P(3,3,3)
-subgraphs of
G
and triples
(i,j,k)\in[n]^{3}
with
i+j=k
. Furthermore, the weight of such a
P(3,3,3)
-subgraph is equal to
A[i]+B[j]-C[k]
. Thus, there is a triple
(i,j,k)
with
A[i]+B[j]<C[k]
if and only if there is a
P(3,3,3)
-subgraph in
G
of negative weight.
Hence, by finding the minimum weight of a
P(3,3,3)
-subgraph in
G
we can decide the given MinConv Verification instance.
It remains to show a bijection.

Let
\overline{v}
be a
P(3,3,3)
-subgraph of
G
. Denote
i_{H}\coloneqq v_{a_{H}},i_{L}\coloneqq v_{a_{L}},j_{H}\coloneqq v_{b_{H}},j_{L}\coloneqq v_{b_{L}},i\coloneqq i_{H}\cdot s+i_{L},j\coloneqq j_{H}\cdot s+j_{L}
, and
k\coloneqq i+j
. We say that
\overline{v}
corresponds to a triple
I(\overline{v})\coloneqq(i,j,k)
. As
i_{H}
and
i_{L}
are adjacent,
j_{H}
and
j_{L}
are adjacent and
k_{H}
and
k_{L}
are adjacent, we get that
(i,j,k)\in[n]^{3}
be the definition of
G
. Furthermore,
i+j=k
by the definition of
k
. The weight of the edge between
i_{H}
and
i_{L}
is
A[i]
, the weight of the edge between
j_{H}
and
j_{L}
is
B[j]
, and the weight of the edge between
k_{H}
and
k_{L}
is
-C[k]
. All other edges have weight zero. Thus, the weight of
\overline{v}
is indeed equal to
A[i]+B[j]-C[k]
.

We show that
I
is injective. For a given
i
and
j
, there is exactly one pair of nodes
(i_{H},i_{L})\in V_{a_{H}}\times V_{a_{L}}
with
i_{H}\cdot s+i_{L}=i
that is connected by an edge, and there is exactly one pair of nodes
(j_{H},j_{L})\in V_{b_{H}}\times V_{b_{L}}
with
j_{H}\cdot s+j_{L}=j
that is connected by an edge. For fixed
i_{H}
and
j_{H}
, there is exactly one node
(i_{H},j_{H})
in
V_{u_{H}}
that is connected to both of them, and
(i_{H},j_{H})
is connected to exactly one node
i_{H}+j_{H}
in
V_{c_{H}}
. Analogously, the nodes in
V_{u_{L}}
and
V_{c_{L}}
are defined uniquely. Thus, for each
(i,j,k)
, at most one
P(3,3,3)
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
has
I(\overline{v})=(i,j,k)
.

Finally,
I
is surjective. Given
(i,j,k)\in[n]^{3}
with
i+j=k
, define
\overline{v}
, where
v_{a_{H}}\coloneqq i_{H},v_{a_{L}}\coloneqq i_{L},v_{b_{H}}\coloneqq j_{H},v_{b_{L}}\coloneqq j_{L},v_{c_{H}}\coloneqq i_{H}+j_{H},v_{c_{L}}\coloneqq i_{L}+j_{L},v_{u_{H}}\coloneqq(i_{H},j_{H})
, and
v_{u_{L}}\coloneqq(i_{L},j_{L})
. It is easy to see that
\overline{v}
forms a
P(3,3,3)
-subgraph in
G
and
I(\overline{v})=(i,j,k)
.

We now analyze the time complexity. There are
\mathcal{O}(s)=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})
nodes in parts
V_{a_{H}}
,
V_{a_{L}}
,
V_{b_{H}}
,
V_{b_{L}}
,
V_{c_{H}}
, and
V_{c_{L}}
. Furthermore, there are
\mathcal{O}(s\cdot s)=\mathcal{O}(n)
nodes in parts
V_{u_{H}}
and
V_{u_{L}}
. Each node in
V_{u_{H}}
and
V_{u_{L}}
is connected to a constant number of nodes in
V_{a_{H}},V_{b_{H}}
, and
V_{c_{H}}
and
V_{a_{L}},V_{b_{L}}
, and
V_{c_{L}}
, respectively. Between
V_{a_{H}}
and
V_{a_{L}}
there are at most
\mathcal{O}(|V_{a_{H}}|\cdot|V_{a_{L}}|)=\mathcal{O}(s^{2})=\mathcal{O}(n)
edges. Analogously, there are
\mathcal{O}(n)
edges between
V_{b_{H}}
and
V_{b_{L}}
and between
V_{c_{H}}
and
V_{c_{L}}
. Thus,
G
has
\mathcal{O}(n)
nodes and
\mathcal{O}(n)
edges, and it can be built in time
\mathcal{O}(n)
.
Hence, if Min-Weight-
P(3,3,3)
-Subgraph can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
, where
m
is the number of edges in the host graph, using this reduction we can solve MinConv Verification in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2-\varepsilon})
, which contradicts the MinConv Verification Hypothesis and thus the MinConv Hypothesis.
This concludes the proof of the first claim of the lemma statement.

For the second claim, assume for the sake of contradiction that
P(3,3,3)
-Listing can be solved in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-\varepsilon}+t)
for some
0<\varepsilon<1
.
We prove that the 3SUM Hypothesis fails.
By
Lemma

4.17
, it suffices to list all solutions of a size-
n
3SUM instance
A\subseteq[n^{\mu}]
with
\mathcal{O}(n^{3-\mu})
solutions in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2-\varepsilon^{\prime}})
for some
1<\mu<2
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
.
We pick
\mu=1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}
.

We claim that, given such a 3SUM listing instance
A
, in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\mu})
we can construct an
\mathcal{O}(n^{\mu})
-edge host graph
G
for
P(3,3,3)
such that there is a constant-time-computable bijection between
P(3,3,3)
-subgraphs in
G
and 3SUM solutions on
A
.
Using the assumed
P(3,3,3)
-Listing algorithm, we then list all
P(3,3,3)
-subgraphs in
G
in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}((n^{\mu})^{2-\varepsilon}+n^{3-\mu})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2})\cdot(2-\varepsilon)}+n^{2-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}}+n^{2-\varepsilon})\leq\mathcal{O}(n^{2-\varepsilon^{\prime}})
.
In time
\mathcal{O}(n^{3-\mu})\leq\mathcal{O}(n^{2-\varepsilon^{\prime}})
we can then convert the listed
P(3,3,3)
-subgraphs in
G
into the solutions of 3SUM on
A
.
It remains to show the construction of
G
.

Define
s\coloneqq\left\lceil\sqrt{n^{\mu}}\right\rceil
. We build a similar graph as in the first claim of the lemma, with the following differences: The edges do not have weights. We add an edge between
i_{H}\in V_{a_{H}}
and
i_{L}\in V_{a_{L}}
if and only if
i_{L}\in[0,s-1]
and
i_{H}\cdot s+i_{L}\in A
. Similarly, we add an edge between
j_{H}\in V_{b_{H}}
and
j_{L}\in V_{b_{L}}
if and only if
j_{L}\in[0,s-1]
and
j_{H}\cdot s+j_{L}\in A
. Finally, we add an edge between
k_{H}\in V_{c_{H}}
and
k_{L}\in V_{c_{L}}
if and only if
k_{H}\cdot s+k_{L}\in A
.
We get the same bijection as in the first claim of the lemma between
P(3,3,3)
-subgraphs in
G
and triples
(i,j,k)
such that
i+j=k
and
i,j,k\in A
.
Finally,
G
has
\mathcal{O}(s^{2})=\mathcal{O}(n^{\mu})
edges, and can be built in time
\mathcal{O}(s^{2})=\mathcal{O}(n^{\mu})
.

For the third claim of the lemma, note that if there is such an algorithm for
P(3,3,3)
-Enumeration, we can use it to solve
P(3,3,3)
-Listing in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-\varepsilon}+t)
, where
t
is the number of
P(3,3,3)
-subgraphs in
G
. This contradicts the 3SUM Hypothesis by the second claim of the lemma.

5
Lower Bounds for Patterns in
\mathcal{P}

In this section we prove the following lemma that was stated in
Section

2
.

See
2.11

Throughout this section,
\alpha,\beta
, and
\gamma
denote integers with
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 3
and
\gamma\geq 1
.
We start by considering three simple graph families for which the clique embedding number is known, see
Lemmas

5.1
,
5.3
and
5.4
. Then in
Section

5.1
we cover all graphs of the form
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
, and in
Section

5.2
we cover all graphs of the form
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
.
Finally, in
Section

5.3
we put the pieces together to prove
Lemma

2.11
.

Lemma 5.1
.

We have
\clemb(K_{2})\geq 1
.

Proof 5.2
.

Pick any node
a
in
K_{2}
. We create a clique embedding
\psi:K_{3}\to K_{2}
by setting
\psi(v)=a
for each node
v\in V(K_{3})
. The depth of the only edge in
K_{2}
is
3
, and thus
\wed(\psi)=3
, which yields
\clemb(K_{2})\geq 3/3=1
.

Lemma 5.3

(
[
44
, Lemma 17]
)
.

For any integer
k\geq 3
, we have
\clemb(C_{k})\geq 2-1/\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil
.

Lemma 5.4

(
[
44
, Proposition 19]
)
.

For any integer
k\geq 2
, we have
\clemb(K_{2,k})\geq 2-\frac{1}{k}
.

5.1
Lower bound for
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)

Lemma 5.5
.

For integers
k\geq 3
and
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in[0,k-1]
such that
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\geq k-3
, any two paths of lengths
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
respectively in a cycle of length
k
touch.

Proof 5.6
.

The first path of length
\ell_{1}
goes through
\ell_{1}+1
nodes.
Therefore, if the second path contains any of these
\ell_{1}+1
nodes or the ones adjacent to the ends of the first path, the two paths touch each other.
There are
\min\{\ell_{1}+3,k\}
such nodes.

The second path goes through
\ell_{2}+1
nodes.
We have
\min\{l_{1}+3,k\}+\ell_{2}+1\geq k+1
as
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\geq k-3
.
Therefore, by the pigeonhole principle, the second path goes through either one of the nodes of the first path or one of the nodes that are adjacent to its ends.
Hence, the two paths touch each other.

For a graph
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
for
\alpha\geq 3
and
\gamma\geq 1
we fix its node numbering as follows (see
Figure

9
). We denote the nodes on the path of length
\alpha
by
a_{0}
,
a_{1}
,
\ldots
,
a_{\alpha-1}
,
a_{\alpha}
, where
a_{0}
and
a_{\alpha}
are the ends of the path. We denote the nodes on the paths of length
2
that are adjacent to both
a_{0}
and
a_{\alpha}
by
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
\ldots
,
c_{\gamma}
.

Figure 9
:
An example of a node numbering for
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
for
\alpha=9
and
\gamma=2
.

Lemma 5.7
.

For any
\alpha\geq 3
and
\gamma\geq 2
we have
\clemb(P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/(2\gamma+\left\lfloor\frac{\alpha-1}{2}\right\rfloor)
.

Proof 5.8
.

We prove the lemma for odd values of
\alpha
.
For an even
\alpha
, the graph
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
has
P(\alpha-1,\gamma\times 2)
as an induced minor, and thus due to
Lemma

2.7
we get
\clemb(P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2))\geq\clemb(P(\alpha-1,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/(2\gamma+\left\lfloor\frac{\alpha-2}{2}\right\rfloor)=2-1/(2\gamma+\left\lfloor\frac{\alpha-1}{2}\right\rfloor)
.

Hence, assume
\alpha
to be odd and let
\ell\coloneqq\frac{\alpha-1}{2}
(so
\alpha=2\ell+1
).
We create a clique embedding
\psi
from
K_{4\gamma+2\ell-1}
to
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
with
\wed(\psi)=2\gamma+\ell
, which yields
\clemb(P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2))\geq\frac{4\gamma+2\ell-1}{2\gamma+\ell}=2-\frac{1}{2\gamma+\ell}
.

We treat the path of length
\alpha
and node
c_{1}
as a cycle
\mathcal{C}
of length
2\ell+3
.
We use three different types of subgraphs that we embed into.
See
Figure

27
for an example.

A.

We embed one node into each path of length
\ell
in
\mathcal{C}
.

B.

For the paths
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell}
and
a_{\ell+1}\leftrightline a_{\ell+2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}
we embed additional
\gamma-1
nodes (making their total embedded values
\gamma
).

C.

For each
i\in[2,\gamma]
, we embed one node into paths
c_{i}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell-1}
and
c_{i}\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline a_{\alpha-1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell+2}
each.

In total, we embed one node into each one of
2\ell+3
subgraphs of type A, additional
\gamma-1
node for two of subgraphs of type B, and one node for each of the
2\gamma-2
subgraphs of type C.
Hence, we embed
(2\ell+3)+2\cdot(\gamma-1)+(2\gamma-2)=4\gamma+2\ell-1
nodes, which is exactly the number of nodes in
K_{4\gamma+2\ell-1}
.

It is clear that all subgraphs chosen in our embedding are connected. Let us argue that all pairs of embedded subgraphs touch.
All pairs of subgraphs of type A and B touch due to
Lemma

5.5
.
All pairs of subgraphs of type C touch either on the edge
\{a_{0},c_{i}\}
or on the edge
\{a_{\alpha},c_{i}\}
for some
i\in[\gamma]
.
Furthermore, we claim that every path
p_{1}
of type C touches every path
p_{2}
of type A or B.
Pick
i
such that
p_{1}
goes through
c_{i}
.
If
a_{0}\in p_{2}
, the two paths touch on the edge
\{a_{0},c_{i}\}
.
If
a_{\alpha}\in p_{2}
, they touch on the edge
\{a_{\alpha},c_{i}\}
.
Otherwise,
p_{2}
lies inside the path
a_{1}\leftrightline a_{2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha-1}
.
Hence,
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
lie inside the cycle consisting of nodes
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{\alpha},c_{i}
.
Such a cycle has length
2\ell+3
, and thus
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
touch due to
Lemma

5.5
.
Hence
\psi
is a valid clique-embedding function.

It remains to show
\wed(\psi)=2\gamma+\ell
.
In isolation, the subgraphs of type A give weak edge depth of
\ell+2
to each edge in
\mathcal{C}
because every edge intersects exactly
\ell+2
paths of length
\ell
.

We claim that the subgraphs of types B and C increase weak edge depths of all edges in
\mathcal{C}
by
2\gamma-2
.
Edges on the path
c_{1}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell}
intersect
\gamma-1
subgraphs of type C and the subgraph
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell}
of type B with weight
\gamma-1
.
Edges on the path
c_{1}\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline a_{\alpha-1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell+1}
touch the symmetric subgraphs.
The remaining edge
\{a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1}\}
touches both subgraphs of type B with weight
\gamma-1
each and no subgraphs of type C.
Hence, every edge in
\mathcal{C}
has weak edge depth
\ell+2+2\gamma-2=2\gamma+\ell
.

It remains to argue that edges outside of
\mathcal{C}
have weak edge depth
2\gamma+\ell
.
Such edges have form
\{a_{0},c_{i}\}
or
\{a_{\alpha},c_{i}\}
for some
i\in[2,\gamma]
.
We already know that weak edge depth of
\{a_{0},c_{1}\}
is
2\gamma+\ell
.
If we switch from this edge to
\{a_{0},c_{i}\}
, we lose a single path of type A that ends in
c_{1}
and gain a single path of form
c_{i}\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline a_{\alpha-1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell+2}
of type C.
Therefore, weak edge depth of
\{a_{0},c_{i}\}
is the same as for
\{a_{0},c_{1}\}
.
Edges of the form
\{a_{\alpha},c_{i}\}
can be checked symmetrically.

5.2
Lower bounds for
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)

For a graph
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
for
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 3
and
\gamma\geq 1
we fix its node numbering as follows (see
Figure

10
). We denote the nodes on the path of length
\alpha
by
a_{0}
,
a_{1}
,
\ldots
,
a_{\alpha-1}
,
a_{\alpha}
, where
a_{0}
and
a_{\alpha}
are the ends of the path. We denote the nodes on the path of length
\beta
by
b_{0}
,
b_{1}
,
\ldots
,
b_{\beta-1}
,
b_{\beta}
. Note that
a_{0}=b_{0}
and
a_{\alpha}=b_{\beta}
. We denote the nodes in the paths of length
2
that are adjacent to both
a_{0}
and
a_{\alpha}
by
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
\ldots
,
c_{\gamma}
.

Figure 10
:
An example of a node numbering for
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
for
\alpha=7
,
\beta=4
, and
\gamma=2
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For any
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 5
,
\alpha+\beta
odd, and
\gamma\geq 1
, we have
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/(2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+2\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-4\gamma-\frac{3}{2})
.

Proof 5.10
.

As
\alpha+\beta
is odd and
\alpha\geq\beta
, we have
\alpha\geq\beta+1
.

Define
X\coloneqq 2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+2\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-4\gamma-\frac{3}{2}=(\beta-1)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})-2\gamma
.
We create a clique embedding
\psi
from
K_{2X-1}
to
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
with
\wed(\psi)=X
, which yields
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq\frac{2X-1}{X}=2-\frac{1}{X}
, as desired.
We treat the paths
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}
and
b_{0}\leftrightline b_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{\beta}
as a cycle
\mathcal{C}
of length
\alpha+\beta
.
We use
6
different types of subgraphs that we embed into. See
Figure

11
.

A.

We embed one node into each path of the form
a_{\ell}\leftrightline a_{\ell+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell+\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}-1}
for
\ell\in[\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}]
.

B.

We embed
\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
nodes into the path
b_{\beta-2}\leftrightline b_{\beta-3}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-1}
.
Furthermore, we embed
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
nodes into each path of the form
b_{\beta-3-\ell}\leftrightline b_{\beta-4-\ell}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}+\ell}
for
\ell\in[0,\beta-4]
(note that there is at least one such
\ell
as
\beta\geq 5
).

C.

Symmetrically, we embed
\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
nodes into the path
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+1}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline b_{\beta-1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{2}
.
Furthermore, we embed
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
nodes into each path of the form
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+\ell}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+\ell+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline b_{\beta-1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{\ell+3}
for
\ell\in[0,\beta-4]
(note that there is at least one such
\ell
as
\beta\geq 4
).

D.

We embed one node into each path of the form
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+\ell}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+\ell+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline c_{1}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell-2}
for
\ell\in[2,\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}]
(note that there is at least one such
\ell
as
\alpha-\beta\geq 1
).

E.

We embed one node into each path of the form
c_{\ell}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-1}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
.

F.

We embed one node into each path of the form
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+1}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline c_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
.

Figure 11
:
Illustration of the proof of
Lemma

5.9
. In addition to the abstract situation of
Lemma

5.9
, the figure also shows the clique embedding for
P(12,7,2,2)
.

We now analyze this construction.
In total, we embed

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}\cdot 1

(A)

\displaystyle+(\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})+(\beta-3)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})

(B)

\displaystyle+(\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})+(\beta-3)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})

(C)

\displaystyle+(\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-2+1)\cdot 1

(D)

\displaystyle+\gamma\cdot 1

(E)

\displaystyle+\gamma\cdot 1

(F)

\displaystyle=2\cdot((\beta-1)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})-2\gamma)-1

nodes, which is exactly the number of nodes in
K_{2X-1}
.
It is clear that all subgraphs into which we embed are connected.
Next, we prove that all pairs of these subgraphs touch.
For every type from A to F we show that the paths of this type touch each other and all later types.

A.

All paths of type A start not later than node
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}}
and end not earlier than node
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}}
.
Note that
\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}<\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}
as
\beta\geq 5
.
Thus, all these paths contain all nodes
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}},\ldots,a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}}
, and all pairs of them touch.
All paths of type B end not earlier than node
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-1}
, so they touch all paths of type A on the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-1},a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}}\}
.
Symmetrically, all paths of type C start not later than
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+1}
, so they all touch all paths of type A on the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}},a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+1}\}
.
For any fixed
\ell
, paths of types D (for
\ell=1
), E (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
), and F (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
) lie on the cycle
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline c_{\ell}
of length
\alpha+2
, which also contains all paths of type A.
The paths of types D, E, and F have length
\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
, while the paths of type A have length
\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}-1
.
Thus,
Lemma

5.5
implies that all pairs of them touch.

B.

All paths of type B go through node
a_{0}
, so all pairs of them touch.
All paths of type B lie on the cycle
\mathcal{C}
of length
\alpha+\beta
together with all paths of type C.
All of these paths have length
\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}
.
Thus,
Lemma

5.5
implies that each path of type B and each path of type C touch.
For any fixed
\ell
, paths of types D (for
\ell=1
), E (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
), and F (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
) go through node
c_{\ell}
, so they touch paths of type B on the edge
\{a_{0},c_{\ell}\}
.

C.

All paths of type C go through node
a_{\alpha}
, so all pairs of them touch.
For any fixed
\ell
, paths of types D (for
\ell=1
), E (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
), and F (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
) go through node
c_{\ell}
, so they touch paths of type C on the edge
\{a_{\alpha},c_{\ell}\}
.

D-F.

Any path of type D, E, or F goes either through node
a_{0}
or through node
a_{\alpha}
.
Any other such path goes through node
c_{\ell}
for some
\ell\in[\gamma]
.
Hence, they touch either on the edge
\{a_{0},c_{\ell}\}
or on the edge
\{a_{\alpha},c_{\ell}\}
.

We showed that all pairs of subgraphs we embed touch, so
\psi
is a valid clique embedding. It remains to show that all edges have weak edge depth at most
X
.
There are

\displaystyle(\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})+(\beta-3)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})

(B)

\displaystyle+(\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-2+1)\cdot 1

(D)

\displaystyle+\gamma\cdot 1

(E)

\displaystyle=(\beta-1)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})-2\gamma-1

\displaystyle=X-1

embedded subgraphs that go through node
a_{0}
.
Node
b_{1}
is not contained in any paths that
a_{0}
is not contained in.
Hence, weak edge depth of
\{a_{0},b_{1}\}
is equal to
X-1
.
For all edges of the form
\{a_{0},c_{\ell}\}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
, the only new path that
c_{\ell}
adds to the subgraphs that are already embedded into
a_{0}
are paths of type F, contributing one to weak edge depth of these edges.
So each edge
\{a_{0},c_{\ell}\}
has weak edge depth
(X-1)+1=X
.
The same holds for the edge
\{a_{0},a_{1}\}
as the only new path that
a_{1}
adds to the subgraphs that are already embedded into
a_{0}
is the path of type A that starts in
a_{1}
, which contributes one to weak edge depth of this edge.
Whenever we now move from an edge
\{a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1}\}
to an edge
\{a_{\ell+1},a_{\ell+2}\}
for
\ell\in[0,\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-2]
(note that there is at least one such
\ell
as
\alpha\geq\beta+1
), we lose a single path of type D that ends in
a_{\ell}
, and we gain a single path of type A that starts in
a_{\ell+2}
.
Both have weight one, so weak edge depth remains at
X
.
When we move from the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-1},a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}}\}
to the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}},a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}+1}\}
, we lose a single path of type B with weight
\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
that ends in
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-1}
and all paths of type E with total weight
\gamma
.
At the same time we gain a single path of type C with weight
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
that starts in node
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}+1}
.
In total, weak edge depth of
\{a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}},a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}+1}\}
is again
X
.
Next, whenever we move from an edge
\{a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1}\}
to an edge
\{a_{\ell+1},a_{\ell+2}\}
for
\ell\in[\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2},\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}-2]
(there is at least one such
\ell
because
\beta\geq 5
), we lose a single path of type B with weight
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
that ends in node
a_{\ell}
, and we gain a single path of type C with weight
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
that starts in node
a_{\ell+2}
, so weak edge depth remains at
X
.
When we move from the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}-1},a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}}\}
to the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}},a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+1}\}
, we lose a single path of type B with weight
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
that ends in node
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}-1}
.
We gain a path of type C with weight
\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
that starts in node
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+1}
and all paths of type F with total weight
\gamma
.
Hence, weak edge depth of
\{a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}},a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2}+1}\}
is again
X
.
Whenever we move from an edge
\{a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1}\}
to an edge
\{a_{\ell+1},a_{\ell+2}\}
for
\ell\in[\frac{\alpha+\beta-3}{2},\alpha-2]
(there is at least one such
\ell
as
\alpha\geq\beta+1
), we lose a single path of type A that ends in node
a_{\ell}
and gain a single path of type D that starts in node
a_{\ell+2}
.
Both of them have weight one, so weak edge depth of the current edge remains at
X
.
If we move from the edge
\{a_{\alpha-1},a_{\alpha}\}
to an edge
\{a_{\alpha},c_{\ell}\}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
, we lose a single path of type A that ends in
a_{\alpha-1}
and gain a single path of type E that starts in
c_{\ell}
, so each such edge has weak edge depth
X
.
When we move from the edge
\{a_{\alpha-1},a_{\alpha}\}
to the edge
\{b_{\beta-1},b_{\beta}\}
, we do not gain any new subgraphs, but we lose a single path of type A that ends in
a_{\alpha-1}
, so weak edge depth of
\{b_{\beta-1},b_{\beta}\}
is
X-1
.
When we move from the edge
\{b_{\beta-1},b_{\beta}\}
to the edge
\{b_{\beta-2},b_{\beta-1}\}
, we lose all paths of types D and F of total weight
\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-1+\gamma
, and we gain a single path of type B with weight
\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
that starts in
b_{\beta-2}
.
In total, weak edge depth of
\{b_{\beta-2},b_{\beta-1}\}
is
(X-1)-(\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}-1+\gamma)+(\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2})=X
.
Whenever we now move from an edge
\{b_{\ell+1},b_{\ell+2}\}
to an edge
\{b_{\ell},b_{\ell+1}\}
for
\ell\in[\beta-3]
(note that there is at least one such
\ell
as
\beta\geq 5
), we lose a single path of type C with weight
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta+3}{2}
that ends in node
b_{\ell+2}
, and we gain a single path of type B with the same weight that starts in node
b_{\ell}
, so weak edge depth of the current edge remains at
X
.
This way we checked that weak edge depth of every edge is at most
X
, which concludes the proof of the fact that
\wed(\psi)=X
.

The proofs of the following two lemmas are very similar to the previous one.

Lemma 5.11
.

For any
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 3
,
\alpha+\beta
odd, and
\gamma\geq 1
, we have
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/(2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+\frac{3}{2})
.

Proof 5.12
.

As
\alpha+\beta
is odd and
\alpha\geq\beta
, we have
\alpha\geq\beta+1
.

Define
X\coloneqq 2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+\frac{3}{2}=(\beta-1)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1)+2
.
We create a clique embedding
\psi
from
K_{2X-1}
to
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
with
\wed(\psi)=X
, which yields
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq\frac{2X-1}{X}=2-\frac{1}{X}
, as desired.
We treat the paths
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}
and
b_{0}\leftrightline b_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{\beta}
as a cycle
\mathcal{C}
of length
\alpha+\beta
.
We use
6
different types of subgraphs that we embed into. See
Figure

12
.

A.

We embed one node into each path of the form
a_{\ell}\leftrightline a_{\ell+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell+\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-2}
for
\ell\in[\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1]
.

B.

We embed one node into the path
b_{\beta-1}\leftrightline b_{\beta-2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{1}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}}
.
Furthermore, we embed
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1
nodes into each path of the form
b_{\beta-\ell-1}\leftrightline b_{\beta-\ell-2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{1}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+\ell}
for
\ell\in[\beta-2]
.
Moreover, we embed
\gamma+1
nodes into the path
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-2}
.

C.

Symmetrically, we embed one node into the path
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha-1}\leftrightline b_{\beta}\leftrightline b_{\beta-1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{1}
.
Furthermore, we embed
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1
nodes into each path of the form
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+\ell}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+\ell+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha-1}\leftrightline b_{\beta}\leftrightline b_{\beta-1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{\beta-\ell+2}
for
\ell\in[3,\beta]
.
Moreover, we embed
\gamma+1
nodes into the path
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+2}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+3}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha-1}\leftrightline a_{\alpha}
.

D.

If
\alpha\geq\beta+3
, we embed one node into each path of the form
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}+\ell}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}+\ell+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline c_{1}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell-1}
for
\ell\in[\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}]
.

E.

We embed one node into each path of the form
c_{\ell}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
.

F.

We embed one node into each path of the form
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline c_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
.

Figure 12
:
Illustration of the proof of
Lemma

5.11
. In addition to the abstract situation of
Lemma

5.11
, the figure also shows the clique embedding for
P(13,6,2,2)
.

We now analyze this construction.
In total, we embed

\displaystyle(\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1)\cdot 1

(A)

\displaystyle+1+(\beta-2)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1)+(\gamma+1)

(B)

\displaystyle+1+(\beta-3+1)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1)+(\gamma+1)

(C)

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}\cdot 1

(D)

\displaystyle+\gamma\cdot 1

(E)

\displaystyle+\gamma\cdot 1

(F)

\displaystyle=2\cdot((\beta-1)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1)+2)-1

nodes, which is exactly the number of nodes in
K_{2X-1}
.
It is clear that all subgraphs into which we embed are connected.
Next, we prove that all pairs of these subgraphs touch.
For every type from A to F we show that the paths of this type touch each other and all later types.

A.

All paths of type A start not later than node
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1}
and end not earlier than node
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-1}
.
Note that
\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1<\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-1
because
\beta\geq 3
.
Thus, all these paths contain all nodes
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1},\ldots,a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-1}
, and all pairs of them touch.
All paths of type B end not earlier than node
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}}
, so they touch all paths of type A on the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}},a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1}\}
.
Symmetrically, all paths of type C start not later than
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}}
, so they touch all paths of type A on the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-1},a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}}\}
.
For any fixed
\ell
, paths of types D (for
\ell=1
), E (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
), and F (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
) lie on the cycle
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline c_{\ell}
of length
\alpha+2
, which also contains all paths of type A.
The paths of types D, E, and F have length
\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1
, while the paths of type A have length
\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-2
.
Thus,
Lemma

5.5
implies that all pairs of them touch.

B.

All paths of type B go through node
a_{0}
, so all pairs of them touch.
All paths of type B lie on the cycle
\mathcal{C}
of length
\alpha+\beta
together with all paths of type C.
All of these paths have length
\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-2
.
Thus,
Lemma

5.5
implies that each path of type B and each path of type C touch.
For any fixed
\ell
, paths of types D (for
\ell=1
), E (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
), and F (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
) go through node
c_{\ell}
, so they touch all paths of type B on the edge
\{a_{0},c_{\ell}\}
.

C.

All paths of type C go through node
a_{\alpha}
, so all pairs of them touch.
For any fixed
\ell
, paths of types D (for
\ell=1
), E (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
), and F (for
\ell\in[\gamma]
) go through node
c_{\ell}
, so they touch paths of type C on the edge
\{a_{\alpha},c_{\ell}\}
.

D-F.

Any path of type D, E, or F goes either through node
a_{0}
or through node
a_{\alpha}
.
Any other such path goes through node
c_{\ell}
for some
\ell\in[\gamma]
.
Hence, they touch either on the edge
\{a_{0},c_{\ell}\}
or on the edge
\{a_{\alpha},c_{\ell}\}
.

We showed that all pairs of subgraphs we embed touch, so
\psi
is a valid clique embedding. It remains to show that all edges have weak edge depth at most
X
.
There are

\displaystyle 1+(\beta-2)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1)+(\gamma+1)

(B)

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}\cdot 1

(D)

\displaystyle+\gamma\cdot 1

(E)

\displaystyle=X-1

embedded subgraphs that go through node
a_{0}
.
The only subgraph that node
b_{1}
is contained in that
a_{0}
is not contained in is the path of type C with weight one that ends in
b_{1}
.
Hence, weak edge depth of
\{a_{0},b_{1}\}
is equal to
X
.
For all edges of the form
\{a_{0},c_{\ell}\}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
, the only new path that
c_{\ell}
adds to the subgraphs that are already embedded into
a_{0}
are paths of type F, contributing one to weak edge depth of these edges.
So each edge
\{a_{0},c_{\ell}\}
has weak edge depth
(X-1)+1=X
.
The same holds for the edge
\{a_{0},a_{1}\}
as the only new path that
a_{1}
adds to the subgraphs that are already embedded into
a_{0}
is the path of type A that starts in
a_{1}
, which contributes one to weak edge depth of this edge.
If
\alpha\geq\beta+3
, whenever we now move from an edge
\{a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1}\}
to an edge
\{a_{\ell+1},a_{\ell+2}\}
for
\ell\in[0,\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}-1]
, we lose a single path of type D that ends in
a_{\ell}
, and we gain a single path of type A that starts in
a_{\ell+2}
.
Both have weight one, so weak edge depth remains at
X
.
When we move from the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}},a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1}\}
to the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1},a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+2}\}
, we lose a single path of type B with weight one that ends in
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}}
and all paths of type E with total weight
\gamma
.
At the same time we gain a single path of type C with weight
\gamma+1
that starts in node
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+2}
.
In total, weak edge depth of
\{a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1},a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+2}\}
is again
X
.
Next, whenever we move from an edge
\{a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1}\}
to an edge
\{a_{\ell+1},a_{\ell+2}\}
for
\ell\in[\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1,\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-3]
(there is at least one such
\ell
as
\beta\geq 3
), we lose a single path of type B with weight
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1
that ends in node
a_{\ell}
, and we gain a single path of type C with weight
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1
that starts in node
a_{\ell+2}
so weak edge depth remains at
X
.
When we move from the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-2},a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-1}\}
to the edge
\{a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-1},a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}}\}
, we lose a single path of type B with weight
\gamma+1
that ends in node
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-2}
.
We gain a path of type C with weight one that starts in node
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}}
and all paths of type F with total weight
\gamma
.
Hence, weak edge depth of
\{a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-1},a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}}\}
is again
X
.
Now if
\alpha\geq\beta+3
, whenever we move from an edge
\{a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1}\}
to an edge
\{a_{\ell+1},a_{\ell+2}\}
for
\ell\in[\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2}-1,\alpha-2]
, we lose a single path of type A that ends in node
a_{\ell}
, and we gain a single path of type D that starts in node
a_{\ell+2}
.
Both of them have weight one, so weak edge depth of the current edge remains at
X
.
If we move from the edge
\{a_{\alpha-1},a_{\alpha}\}
to an edge
\{a_{\alpha},c_{\ell}\}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
, we lose a single path of type A that ends in
a_{\alpha-1}
, and we gain a single path of type E that starts in
c_{\ell}
, so each such edge has weak edge depth
X
.
When we move from the edge
\{a_{\alpha-1},a_{\alpha}\}
to the edge
\{b_{\beta},b_{\beta-1}\}
, we similarly lose a single path of type A with weight one that ends in
a_{\alpha-1}
, and we gain a single path of type B with weight one that starts in
b_{\beta-1}
, so weak edge depth of
\{b_{\beta},b_{\beta-1}\}
is
X
.
Now when we move from the edge
\{b_{\beta},b_{\beta-1}\}
to the edge
\{b_{\beta-1},b_{\beta-2}\}
, we lose a single path of type C with weight
\gamma+1
that ends in node
b_{\beta}
and all paths of types D and F of total weight
\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+\gamma
.
At the same time we gain a single path of type B with weight
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1
that starts in node
b_{\beta-2}
.
In total, weak depth is again
X
.
Now if
\beta\geq 4
, whenever we move from an edge
\{b_{\ell+2},b_{\ell+1}\}
to an edge
\{b_{\ell+1},b_{\ell}\}
for
\ell\in[\beta-3]
, we lose a single path of type C with weight
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta-1}{2}+1
that ends in
b_{\ell+2}
, and we gain a single path of type B with the same weight that starts in node
b_{\ell}
, so weak edge depth of the current edge remains at
X
.
This way we checked that weak edge depth of every edge is at most
X
, which concludes the proof of the fact that
\wed(\psi)=X
.

Lemma 5.13
.

For any
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 3
,
\alpha+\beta
even, and
\gamma\geq 1
, we have
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/(2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-3\gamma)
.

Proof 5.14
.

Define
X\coloneqq 2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-3\gamma=(\beta-2)\cdot(2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+1)+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+\gamma+2
.
We create a clique embedding
\psi
from
K_{2X-1}
to
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
with
\wed(\psi)=X
, which yields
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq\frac{2X-1}{X}=2-\frac{1}{X}
, as desired.
We treat the paths
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}
and
b_{0}\leftrightline b_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{\beta}
as a cycle
\mathcal{C}
of length
\alpha+\beta
.
We use
6
different types of subgraphs that we embed into. See
Figure

13
.

A.

We embed one node into each path of the form
a_{\ell}\leftrightline a_{\ell+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell+\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}-2}
for
\ell\in[\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+1]
.

B.

We embed one node into the path
b_{\beta-1}\leftrightline b_{\beta-2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{1}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}}
.
Furthermore, we embed
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+1
nodes into each path of the form
b_{\beta-\ell-1}\leftrightline b_{\beta-\ell-2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{1}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+\ell}
for
\ell\in[\beta-2]
.

C.

We embed
\gamma+1
nodes into the path
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+2}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+3}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}
.
Furthermore, if
\beta\geq 4
, we embed
2\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+1
nodes into each path of the form
a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+\ell}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+\ell+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline b_{\beta-1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{\beta-\ell+2}
for
\ell\in[3,\beta-1]
.
Moreover, we embed
\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}+1
nodes into the path
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline b_{\beta-1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{2}
.

D.

If
\alpha>\beta
, we embed one node into each path of the form
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}+\ell}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}+\ell+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline c_{1}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\ell-1}
for
\ell\in[\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}]
.

E.

We embed one node into each path of the form
c_{\ell}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
.

F.

We embed one node into each path of the form
a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}}\leftrightline a_{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}+1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}\leftrightline c_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[\gamma]
.

Lemma 5.15
.

For
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 3
and
\gamma\geq 1
, we have
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
.

Proof 5.16
.

We consider all the cases from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
.

1.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+2
,
\alpha+\beta
is even,
\alpha>\beta
, and
\beta<\gamma+2
.
As
\alpha>\beta
in this case, we have
\alpha-1\geq\beta
.
Furthermore, as
\alpha+\beta
is even,
(\alpha-1)+\beta
is odd.
Finally,
P(\alpha-1,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
is an induced minor of
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
.
Hence, due to
Lemma

2.7
and
Lemma

5.11
, we obtain

\displaystyle\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))

\displaystyle\geq\clemb(P(\alpha-1,\beta,\gamma\times 2))

\displaystyle\geq 2-1/(2\beta\gamma+\frac{(\alpha-1)\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\beta-\frac{\alpha-1}{2}-2\gamma+\frac{3}{2})

\displaystyle=2-1/(2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+2)

\displaystyle=2-1/\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma).

2.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-3\gamma
,
\alpha+\beta
is even,
3\beta<\alpha+6\gamma+8
, and (
\alpha=\beta
or
\beta\geq\gamma+2
).
Follows from
Lemma

5.13
.

3.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+3\beta-\alpha-6\gamma-4
,
\alpha+\beta
is even,
2\beta\leq\alpha+4\gamma+6
, and
3\beta\geq\alpha+6\gamma+8
.
In this case
3\beta\geq\alpha+6\gamma+8\geq\beta+6\gamma+8
, so
\alpha>\beta-1\geq(3\gamma+4)-1\geq 5
.
Furthermore, as
\alpha+\beta
is even,
\alpha+(\beta-1)
is odd.
Finally,
P(\alpha,\beta-1,\gamma\times 2)
is an induced minor of
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
.
Hence, due to
Lemma

2.7
and
Lemma

5.9
, we obtain

\displaystyle\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))

\displaystyle\geq\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta-1,\gamma\times 2))

\displaystyle\geq 2-1/(2(\beta-1)\gamma+\frac{\alpha(\beta-1)}{2}-\frac{(\beta-1)^{2}}{2}+2(\beta-1)-\frac{\alpha}{2}-4\gamma-\frac{3}{2})

\displaystyle=2-1/(2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+3\beta-\alpha-6\gamma-4)

\displaystyle=2-1/\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma).

4.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+\frac{3}{2}
,
\alpha+\beta
is odd, and
\beta<2\gamma+3
.
Follows from
Lemma

5.11
.

5.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+2\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-4\gamma-\frac{3}{2}
,
\alpha+\beta
is odd,
2\beta\leq\alpha+4\gamma+6
, and
\beta\geq 2\gamma+3
.
This case follows from
Lemma

5.9
as
\beta\geq 2\gamma+3\geq 5
.

6.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2}
,
\alpha=0\bmod 4
, and
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6
.
Here we consider all cases
6
through
8
together.
Define
x\coloneqq(((\alpha-1)\bmod 4)+3)/2
.
Denote
\beta^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{\alpha}{2}+2\gamma+x
.
It is easy to see that
\beta^{\prime}
is an integer.
Furthermore,
2\alpha+2\beta^{\prime}=2\alpha+\alpha+4\gamma+(\alpha-1)+3=2\bmod 4
, hence
\alpha+\beta^{\prime}
is odd.
Moreover, we have
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{\alpha}{2}+2\gamma+x\geq\frac{3}{2}+2+\frac{3}{2}=5
.
Furthermore,
\beta^{\prime}<\beta\leq\alpha
because
x\leq 3
and
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6
by case assumption, and thus
P(\alpha,\beta^{\prime},\gamma\times 2)
is an induced minor of
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
.
Hence, due to
Lemma

2.7
and
Lemma

5.9
, we obtain

\displaystyle\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))

\displaystyle\geq\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta^{\prime},\gamma\times 2))

\displaystyle\geq 2-1/(2\beta^{\prime}\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}{2}-\frac{\beta^{\prime 2}}{2}+2\beta^{\prime}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-4\gamma-\frac{3}{2})

\displaystyle=2-1/(2(\frac{\alpha}{2}+2\gamma+x)\gamma+\frac{\alpha(\frac{\alpha}{2}+2\gamma+x)}{2}-\frac{(\frac{\alpha}{2}+2\gamma+x)^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle+2(\frac{\alpha}{2}+2\gamma+x)-\frac{\alpha}{2}-4\gamma-\frac{3}{2})

\displaystyle=2-1/(2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2}+(2x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}-\frac{3}{2}))

\displaystyle=2-1/\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma),

where the last inequality can be checked for each value of
\alpha\bmod 4
.

(a)

\alpha=0\bmod 4
. In this case
x=(3+3)/2=3
. Hence,
2x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}-\frac{3}{2}=0
.

(b)

\alpha=1\bmod 4
. In this case
x=(0+3)/2=3/2
. Hence,
2x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}-\frac{3}{2}=3/8
.

(c)

\alpha=2\bmod 4
. In this case
x=(1+3)/2=2
. Hence,
2x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}-\frac{3}{2}=1/2
.

(d)

\alpha=3\bmod 4
. In this case
x=(2+3)/2=5/2
. Hence,
2x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}-\frac{3}{2}=3/8
.

7.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{3}{8}
,
\alpha
is odd, and
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6
.
See case
6
.

8.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{1}{2}
,
\alpha=2\bmod 4
, and
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6
.
See case
6
.

5.3
Proof of
Lemma

2.11

Now we can put the pieces together to prove
Lemma

2.11
.

See
2.11

Proof 5.17

(Proof of
Lemma

2.11
)
.

Let
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.

For
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1,0,0)
, we have
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq 1=2-1/1
by
Lemma

5.1
and
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=1
by
Lemma

8.3
.
For
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(2,1,0)
, we have
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/2
by
Lemma

5.3
and
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2
by
Lemma

8.5
.
For
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(k-2,2,0)
with
k\geq 4
, we have
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/\left\lceil k/2\right\rceil
by
Lemma

5.3
and
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\left\lceil k/2\right\rceil
by
Lemma

8.15
.

Otherwise, we have
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 2
and
\gamma\geq 1
.
If
\alpha=\beta=2
, then we have
\clemb(P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2))\geq 2-1/(\gamma+2)
by
Lemma

5.4
and
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\gamma+2
by
Lemma

8.17
.
If
\alpha>\beta=2
, then we have
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\gamma+2+\left\lfloor\frac{\alpha-1}{2}\right\rfloor
by
Lemma

8.19
, and
Lemma

5.7
proves the desired inequality.
Finally, if
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 3
, then
Lemma

5.15
implies the desired inequality. This covers all
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.

6
Algorithms: from General Case to Family
\mathcal{P}

In this section we prove
Lemmas

2.14
,
2.15
,
2.16
and
2.18
. This covers most of the claims from
Section

2.2
, except for
Lemma

2.19
whose proof is postponed to
Section

7
.

We start by presenting a reduction to tree patterns.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
H
be a pattern.
There is an algorithm that, given a (weighted)
m
-edge host graph
G
and a partial
H
-encoding
\mathcal{E}
of size
s
, in time
\mathcal{O}(m+s)
computes a tree
T^{\prime}
and a (weighted) host graph
G^{\prime}
for pattern
T^{\prime}
such that there is a (weight-preserving) bijection between
H
-subgraphs of
G
encoded by
\mathcal{E}
and
T^{\prime}
-subgraphs of
G^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, given any
T^{\prime}
-subgraph of
G^{\prime}
, the corresponding
H
-subgraph of
G
can be computed in constant time.

Proof 6.2
.

It suffices to proof the weighted setting, as the unweighted setting follows by setting all weights to zero.
Recall that a partial encoding
\mathcal{E}
consists of a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
, which consists of a tree
T
together with bags
B(v)
for each node
v
of
T
, as well as a submaterialization
S_{B(v)}
for every bag
B(v)
of
\mathcal{T}
.

We start by filtering submaterializations in time
\mathcal{O}(s)
.
For each bag
B
of
\mathcal{T}
, we remove all
\overline{v}\in S_{B}
such that
\overline{v}
does not form an
H[B]
-subgraph in
G[H[B]]
.
Note that the filtered partial
H
-encoding encodes exactly the same
H
-subgraphs as before the filtering. Indeed, if
\overline{v}\in S_{B}
does not form an
H[B]
-subgraph in
G[H[B]]
, it cannot be a projection of any
H
-subgraph in
G
onto
B
.

We construct the new pattern
T^{\prime}
by subdividing every edge of
T
, i.e., we let
T^{\prime}\coloneqq(V(T)\cup E(T),\{\{a,e\}\mid e\in E(T),a\in e\})
. Clearly,
T^{\prime}
is a tree and a pattern.

For any edge
e=\{a,b\}\in E(T)
, let
B(e)\coloneqq B(a)\cap B(b)
and
S_{B(e)}\coloneqq\left.S_{B(a)}\right|_{B(e)}\cup\left.S_{B(b)}\right|_{B(e)}
.
With this notation, we construct the nodes of the new host graph
G^{\prime}
as follows.
For every
a\in V(T)
, we let
V^{\prime}_{a}\coloneqq S_{B(a)}
be the part of
G^{\prime}
corresponding to
a
.
For every
e\in E(T)
, we let
V^{\prime}_{e}\coloneqq S_{B(e)}
be the part of
G^{\prime}
corresponding to
e
.

The edges of
G^{\prime}
simply encode projections:
For every
\{a,e\}\in E(T^{\prime})
, we let the edges between
V^{\prime}_{a}
and
V^{\prime}_{e}
be
\{\{v_{a},\left.v_{a}\right|_{B(e)}\}\mid v_{a}\in S_{B(a)}\}
.

We next define the edge weights. Note that, by the definition of a tree decomposition, for every edge
e^{\prime}=\{x,y\}\in E(H)
there is some node
a\in T
with
x,y\in B(a)
.
We pick any such node
a
and an arbitrary edge
e\in E(T)
incident to
a
and denote
\varphi(e^{\prime})\coloneqq\{a,e\}
.
We define the edge weights in
G^{\prime}
by

w^{\prime}(\{v_{a},\left.v_{a}\right|_{B(e)}\})\coloneqq\sum_{\{x,y\}\in E(H):\,\varphi(\{x,y\})=\{a,e\}}w(\{\left.v_{a}\right|_{x},\left.v_{a}\right|_{y}\}).

We now show how to build
G^{\prime}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m+s)
.
We can build
V^{\prime}_{a}
for each
a\in V(T)
trivially in time
\mathcal{O}(|S_{B(a)}|)=\mathcal{O}(s)
.
To build
V^{\prime}_{e}
for each
e=\{a,b\}\in E(T)
, we first project
S_{B(a)}
and
S_{B(b)}
onto
B(e)
in time
\mathcal{O}(s)
and unite the two sequences.
The resulting sequence is
S_{B(e)}
but with repetitions.
To delete repetitions, we sort the sequence lexicographically in time
\mathcal{O}(m+s)
using radix sort.
By remembering throughout this process for each element of
S_{B(e)}
from which elements of
S_{B(a)}
and
S_{B(b)}
it was created through projection, we can build edges between
V^{\prime}_{a}
and
V^{\prime}_{e}
and between
V^{\prime}_{b}
and
V^{\prime}_{e}
in time
\mathcal{O}(s)
.
To compute the edge weights
w^{\prime}
of edges between
V^{\prime}_{a}
and
V^{\prime}_{e}
for each
