\{a,e\}\in E(T^{\prime})
, we iterate over
\{x,y\}\in E(H)
, and if
\varphi(\{x,y\})=\{a,e\}
, we add the corresponding weights from
G
to
G^{\prime}
.
For a fixed
\{x,y\}\in E(H)
with
\varphi(\{x,y\})=\{a,e\}
, we first sort edges between
V_{x}
and
V_{y}
in
G
in time
\mathcal{O}(m)
using radix sort.
Note that
x,y\in B(a)
holds by the definition of
\varphi
.
We then use radix sort to lexicographically sort
V^{\prime}_{a}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m+s)
where
x
and
y
come first in the ordering of
B(a)
according to which we sort lexicographically.
We then use two pointers to go through all
v_{a}\in V^{\prime}_{a}
and corresponding edges
\{\left.v_{a}\right|_{x},\left.v_{a}\right|_{y}\}
.
We then add
w(\{\left.v_{a}\right|_{x},\left.v_{a}\right|_{y}\})
to the weight
w^{\prime}
of the edge
\{v_{a},\left.v_{a}\right|_{B(e)}\}
, which is the single outgoing edge from
v_{a}
to
V^{\prime}_{e}
.
Thus, in time
\mathcal{O}(m+s)
we build
G^{\prime}
.

We now prove that there is a weight-preserving bijection between
H
-subgraphs of
G
encoded by
\mathcal{E}
and
T^{\prime}
-subgraphs of
G^{\prime}
.
Fix some
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
encoded by
\mathcal{E}
.
Let
I(\overline{v})\coloneqq\overline{v}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B(a)})_{a\in V(T^{\prime})}
.
We claim that
\overline{v}^{\prime}
is a
T^{\prime}
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
with the same weight as the
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
in
G
.
The fact that
\overline{v}^{\prime}
is a
T^{\prime}
-subgraph in
G^{\prime}
follows from the definition of
G^{\prime}
.
The weight is preserved as for every edge
\{x,y\}\in E(H)
, there is exactly one edge
\{a,e\}\in E(T^{\prime})
, such that the weights of edges between
V_{x}
and
V_{y}
are encoded on the edges between
V^{\prime}_{a}
and
V^{\prime}_{e}
.

Furthermore, if
\overline{u}
is some other
H
-subgraph of
G
encoded by
\mathcal{E}
, there is some
x\in V(H)
such that
v_{x}\neq u_{x}
.
There is some node
a\in V(T)
such that
x\in B(a)
, and thus
I(\overline{v})_{x}\neq I(\overline{u})_{x}
.
Hence,
I
is an injection.

It remains to show that
I
is a surjection and how to compute
I^{-1}
in constant time.
Fix some
T^{\prime}
-subgraph
\overline{v}^{\prime}
of
G^{\prime}
.
For each
x\in V(H)
, fix some node
\nu(x)\in V(T)
such that
x\in B(\nu(x))
.
Let
\overline{v}\coloneqq(\left.v^{\prime}_{\nu(x)}\right|_{x})_{x\in V(H)}
.
It is clear that
\overline{v}
can be computed from
\overline{v}^{\prime}
in constant time.
We claim that
\overline{v}
is an
H
-subgraph of
G
encoded by
\mathcal{E}
and
I(\overline{v})=\overline{v}^{\prime}
.
First, we claim that
v_{x}=\left.v^{\prime}_{a}\right|_{x}
for any
a\in V(T^{\prime})
with
x\in B(a)
.
That is, the definition of
\overline{v}
does not depend on the specific choice of nodes
\nu(x)
.
Note that as
T
corresponds to some tree decomposition of
H
, nodes
a\in V(T)
with
x\in B(a)
form a connected subgraph of
T
.
After subdivision this fact still holds.
That is, nodes
a\in V(T^{\prime})
with
x\in B(a)
form a connected subgraph of
T^{\prime}
.
For any two such adjacent nodes
a,b\in V(T^{\prime})
, by the definition of
G^{\prime}
, we have
\left.v^{\prime}_{a}\right|_{x}=\left.v^{\prime}_{b}\right|_{x}
as
v^{\prime}_{a}
and
v^{\prime}_{b}
are adjacent in
G^{\prime}
and edges in
G^{\prime}
are projections.
Hence, by transitivity we get
\left.v^{\prime}_{a}\right|_{x}=\left.v^{\prime}_{\nu(x)}\right|_{x}=v_{x}
.
From this, it follows that
I(\overline{v})=\overline{v}^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, we claim that
\overline{v}
is an
H
-subgraph in
G
.
For each
\{x,y\}\in E(H)
, by the definition of a tree decomposition there is some
a\in V(T)
with
x,y\in B(a)
.
As
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B(a)}=v^{\prime}_{a}\in S_{B(a)}
, we have that
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B(a)}
forms an
H[B(a)]
-subgraph in
G[H[B(a)]]
as we initially have filtered out all elements of
S_{B(a)}
that do not form
H[B(a)]
-subgraphs in
G[H[B(a)]]
.
In particular, we have
\{v_{x},v_{y}\}\in E(G)
as
x,y\in B(a)
.
As this holds for each
\{x,y\}\in E(H)
, we get that
\overline{v}
is indeed an
H
-subgraph in
G
.
Finally, for each
a\in V(T)
, we have
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B(a)}=v^{\prime}_{a}\in S_{B(a)}
, and thus
\overline{v}
is encoded by
\mathcal{E}
.
This concludes the proof of the fact that
\overline{v}
is an
H
-subgraph of
G
encoded by
\mathcal{E}
and
I(\overline{v})=\overline{v}^{\prime}
, thus proving that
I
is a surjection.

See
2.14

Proof 6.3

(Proof of
Lemma

2.14
)
.

For the first claim, we first solve
H
-Encoding on
G
in time
T(m)
, which returns an
H
-encoding consisting of partial
H
-encodings
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{k}
. Recall that
k
must be constant, i.e., it may depend on
H
but not on
G
.
Since the running time is
T(m)
, each partial
H
-encoding has size
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.
For each partial encoding
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, we apply
Lemma

6.1
to build a tree
H^{\prime}_{i}
and a host graph
G^{\prime}_{i}
for pattern
H^{\prime}_{i}
.
We solve Min-Weight-
H^{\prime}_{i}
-Subgraph on
G^{\prime}_{i}
in linear time as
H^{\prime}_{i}
is a tree.
This yields a weight for each
i
, and we return the minimum of all these weights.

Since
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{k}
is a full
H
-encoding, the minimum-weight
H
-subgraph of
G
is encoded by some partial encoding
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, and by the weight-preserving property of
Lemma

6.1
we obtain the same minimum weight by solving Min-Weight-
H^{\prime}_{i}
-Subgraph on
G^{\prime}_{i}
. This proves correctness.
The running time of this algorithm is
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
as
k
is constant.

The second claim of the lemma follows similarly.
We apply
Lemma

6.1
(in the unweighted setting) to each
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.
Since
H^{\prime}_{i}
is a tree, we can then solve
H^{\prime}_{i}
-Listing on
G^{\prime}_{i}
in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m)+t_{i})
where
t_{i}
is the number of
H
-subgraph of
G
encoded by
\mathcal{E}_{i}
(see, e.g.,
[
15
]
).
By the definition of a full
H
-encoding, we have
\sum_{i}t_{i}=t
, and thus in total we work in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m)+t)
.
Each
H^{\prime}_{i}
-subgraph of
G^{\prime}_{i}
can be converted into an
H
-subgraph of
G
in constant time.

It remains to show the third claim of the lemma.
In the preprocessing phase we solve
H
-Encoding on
G
in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
and apply
Lemma

6.1
to each
\mathcal{E}_{i}
to build corresponding instances
G^{\prime}_{i}
of
H^{\prime}_{i}
-Enumeration.
Since
H^{\prime}_{i}
is a tree,
H^{\prime}_{i}
-Enumeration can be solved in preprocessing time
\mathcal{O}(|E(G^{\prime}_{i})|)=\mathcal{O}(T(m))
and constant delay for each
i
(see, e.g.,
[
15
]
).
Thus, the preprocessing takes time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
in total.

To enumerate the next
H
-subgraph of
G
, we pick any
i
such that we did not yet enumerate all
H^{\prime}_{i}
-subgraphs in
G^{\prime}_{i}
and ask
H^{\prime}_{i}
-Enumeration to generate a new
H^{\prime}_{i}
-subgraph of
G^{\prime}_{i}
. This takes constant delay.
Then, in constant time we can convert the computed
H^{\prime}_{i}
-subgraph of
G^{\prime}_{i}
into an
H
-subgraph of
G
.
As
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{k}
are a full
H
-encoding, every
H
-subgraph of
G
is enumerated exactly once.
Hence, we obtain constant-time delay.

See
2.15

Proof 6.4

(Proof of
Lemma

2.15
)
.

Given a host graph
G^{\prime}
for
H^{\prime}
-Encoding, we apply
Lemma

4.8
(in the unweighted setting) to build a host graph
G
for
H
-Encoding in time
\mathcal{O}(m)
. Given
G
, we solve
H
-Encoding in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
. As a result, we get a full
H
-encoding of
G
consisting of partial
H
-encodings
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{k}
of
G
. There is a bijection
I
between
H
-subgraphs in
G
and
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs in
G^{\prime}
.
We will create partial
H^{\prime}
-encodings
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{k}
of
G^{\prime}
, such that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{i}
encodes exactly the
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs of
G^{\prime}
corresponding to the
H
-subgraphs of
G
that are encoded by
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, which solves the problem.

Fix some partial encoding
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.
Given its underlying tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
H
, we can create a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
H^{\prime}
by preserving the tree structure of
\mathcal{T}
, and for each bag
B
of
\mathcal{T}
creating a bag
\varphi^{-1}(B)\coloneqq\{x\in V(H^{\prime})\mid\varphi(x)\cap B\neq\emptyset\}
in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, where
\varphi
is taken from the proof of
Lemma

4.8
.
Consider the bijection
I
(and its inverse
I^{-1}
) between
H
-subgraphs of
G
and
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs of
G^{\prime}
from
Lemma

4.8
, and denote the submaterializations of
\mathcal{E}_{i}
by
S_{B}
, for each bag
B
of
\mathcal{T}
.
Fix some
\overline{u}\in S_{B}
.
For each
x\in\varphi^{-1}(B)
, pick some
a\in\varphi(x)\cap B
.
Let
j
be such that
u_{a}=v_{a,j}
.
Denote
u^{\prime}_{x}=v^{\prime}_{x,j}
.
Similarly to
Lemma

4.8
, one can see that the definition of
u^{\prime}_{x}
does not depend on the choice of
a
.
Define
I^{-1}(\overline{u})\coloneqq(u^{\prime}_{x})_{x\in\varphi^{-1}(B)}
.
We define the submaterialization of bag
\varphi^{-1}(B)
by setting
S^{\prime}_{\varphi^{-1}(B)}\coloneqq\{I^{-1}(\overline{u})\mid\overline{u}\in S_{B}\}
.
As the algorithm for
H
-Encoding works in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
, all submaterializations of
\mathcal{E}_{i}
have size at most
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.
Thus, creating
S^{\prime}_{\varphi^{-1}(B)}
takes time
\mathcal{O}(|S_{B}|)=\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.

We now prove that the constructed
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{i}
is a partial
H^{\prime}
-encoding.
First, we prove that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a tree decomposition of
H^{\prime}
.
Consider any edge
\{x,y\}\in E(H^{\prime})
.
As
H^{\prime}
is an induced minor of
H
, there is an edge
\{a,b\}\in E(H)
such that
a\in\varphi(x)
and
b\in\varphi(y)
.
There is a bag
B
of
\mathcal{T}
that contains
a
and
b
.
Hence,
\varphi^{-1}(B)
contains
x
and
y
.

Now consider some
x\in V(H^{\prime})
.
Note that
x
is contained in exactly the bags
\varphi^{-1}(B)
of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, for which
\varphi(x)\cap B\neq\emptyset
.
Note that
\varphi(x)
is a connected subset of nodes of
H
.
Nodes from a connected subset of nodes of
H
lie in the bags of a connected subtree of the tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
H
.
Therefore,
x
lies in the bags of a connected subtree of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
Hence,
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a tree decomposition of
H^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, for each bag
B
of
\mathcal{T}
, it is clear that
S^{\prime}_{\varphi^{-1}(B)}
is a submaterialization of
\varphi^{-1}(B)
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{i}
is a partial
H^{\prime}
-encoding.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{i}
encodes exactly the
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs of
G^{\prime}
that correspond to the
H
-subgraphs of
G
encoded by
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.
Consider some
H
-subgraph
\overline{u}
in
G
encoded by
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.
We have
\left.\overline{u}\right|_{B}\in S_{B}
for all bags
B
of
\mathcal{T}
.
By the definition of
S^{\prime}_{\varphi^{-1}(B)}
, we have that
I^{-1}(\left.\overline{u}\right|_{B})\in S^{\prime}_{\varphi{-1}(B)}
.
As
I^{-1}(\left.\overline{u}\right|_{B})=\left.I^{-1}(\overline{u})\right|_{\varphi^{-1}(B)}
, we have that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{i}
encodes
I^{-1}(\overline{u})
.
Furthermore, if
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{i}
encodes some
H^{\prime}
-subgraph
\overline{u}^{\prime}
of
G^{\prime}
, we have
I(\left.\overline{u}^{\prime}\right|_{\varphi^{-1}(B)})\in S_{B}
for all bags
B
of
\mathcal{T}
, and as
I(\left.\overline{u}^{\prime}\right|_{\varphi^{-1}(B)})=\left.I(\overline{u}^{\prime})\right|_{B}
, we have that
I(\overline{u}^{\prime})
is encoded by
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

See
2.16

Proof 6.5
.

For the given host graph
G
, for each
i\in[k]
solve
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-Encoding on
G[H[S_{i}\cup C]]
to obtain an encoding
\mathcal{E}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{i,k_{i}}
.
We now consider all combinations of partial
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-encodings over all
i\in[k]
, i.e., we consider all tuples
(\mathcal{E}_{1,i_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{k,i_{k}})
with
i_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{j}\}
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Note that there is a constant number of such combinations.
By
Lemma

3.1
and since
C
is a clique, each tree decomposition of each partial
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-encodings has a bag that contains
C
.
We connect such a bag from the tree decomposition of
\mathcal{E}_{1,i_{1}}
with the corresponding bags from the tree decompositions of
\mathcal{E}_{2,i_{2}},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{k,i_{k}}
.
Observe that this yields a tree decomposition of
H
.
We preserve the already created submaterializations of all bags, thus creating a partial
H
-encoding of
G
.
Note that any
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
is encoded exactly once, as for each of
H[S_{i}\cup C]
we have that
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{S_{i}\cup C}
is encoded exactly once in the solution of
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-Encoding, thus only the combination corresponding to all such partial
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-encodings encodes
\overline{v}
.

We now analyze the time complexity. Running the algorithm of
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-Encoding for all
i\in[k]
takes time
\mathcal{O}(T(m)\cdot k)=\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.
As these algorithms work in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
, all partial encodings have size
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.
Hence, combining the partial encodings takes time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.

See
2.18

Proof 6.6

(Proof of
Corollary

2.18
)
.

We prove the lemma by induction on
|V(H)|
.
If
H
does not have a clique separator, then
D(H)=\{H\}
, and the claim is obvious.
Otherwise, there is some minimal clique separator
C
in
H
.
Let
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k}
be the connected components of
H-C
.
Note that by
Lemma

1.4
, we have
D(H[S_{i}\cup C])\subseteq D(H)
for each
i
.
Hence, for each
H^{\prime}\in D(H[S_{i}\cup C])
,
H^{\prime}
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
.
Consequently, by the induction hypothesis,
H[S_{i}\cup C]
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
for each
i
.
Applying
Lemma

2.16
, we get that
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(T(m))
. Since
H
has constant size, the number of induction steps is constant, so the overhead remains constant.

7
Algorithms for Patterns in
\mathcal{P}

In this section we prove the following lemma that was stated in
Section

2
.

See
2.19

Throughout this section,
\alpha,\beta
, and
\gamma
denote integers with
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 3
and
\gamma\geq 1
.
We start by considering three simple graph families, see
Lemmas

7.1
,
7.3
and
7.5
. Then in
Section

7.1
we cover all graphs of the form
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
, and in
Section

7.2
we cover all graphs of the form
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
.
Finally, in
Section

7.3
we put the pieces together to prove
Lemma

2.11
.

Lemma 7.1
.

For
H=K_{2}
,
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m)
.

Proof 7.2
.

We create a single tree decomposition consisting of a single bag
B\coloneqq V(K_{2})
. It takes
\mathcal{O}(m)
time to materialize
B
by picking adjacent pairs of nodes.

Lemma 7.3
.

For
H=C_{k}
,
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil})
for any
k\geq 3
.

Proof 7.4
.

We prove the lemma for even values of
k
because for odd values of
k
, the desired time complexity for
C_{k}
is the same as for
C_{k+1}
, and thus by
Lemma

2.15
, the claim follows from the even case.

Denote the nodes of
C_{k}
by
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
,
\ldots
,
a_{k}
in counterclockwise order. Define
\ell\coloneqq\frac{k}{2}
(so
k=2\ell
). We want to solve the problem in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/\ell})
.

Consider some
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
. We create several partial
H
-encodings of
G
and claim that exactly one of them encodes
\overline{v}
.

We split the nodes in
V_{a_{1}},V_{a_{2}},\ldots,V_{a_{k}}
into the ones that have degrees smaller than
m^{1/\ell}
(low-degree nodes, sets
V_{a_{i}}^{\textup{lo}}
) and the ones that have degrees at least
m^{1/\ell}
(high-degree nodes, sets
V_{a_{i}}^{\textup{hi}}
).
For each of the
2^{k}
choices of high or low degrees in all parts, we construct a partial
H
-encoding that encodes exactly the
H
-subgraphs that satisfy these degree constraints.
As
\overline{v}
satisfies exactly one of these
2^{k}
cases, it will be encoded exactly once. Once we fix for each part of
G
whether we consider a low-degree or a high-degree node in that part, we filter out all other nodes.
We distinguish two cases.

•

Case 1:
v_{a_{i}}
has high degree for some
i
.

In this case
Section

3.4
implies
|V_{a_{i}}^{\textup{hi}}|\leq m^{1-1/\ell}
.
Since
H-\{a_{i}\}
is a tree, it has a tree decomposition where each bag consists of two adjacent nodes.
Adding
a_{i}
to every bag, we obtain a tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

14
).
All of these bags can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/\ell})
because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes and
\mathcal{O}(m^{1-1/\ell})
choices for a node from
V_{a_{i}}^{\textup{hi}}
.

Figure 14
:
An example of a tree decomposition of
C_{k}
for Case 1 of
Lemma

7.3
for
k=8
and
i=4
. Here and later solid black lines inside a bag represent edges used to create a submaterialization of the bag, and dotted grey edges represent all other edges that this bag covers.

•

Case 2:
v_{a_{i}}
has low degree for all
i
.

In this case we create a tree decomposition of
H
consisting of two bags:
B_{1}\coloneqq\{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell+1}\}
and
B_{2}\coloneqq\{a_{\ell+1},a_{\ell+2},\ldots,a_{k},a_{1}\}
.
It is easy to see that it is indeed a valid tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

15
).
To materialize
B_{1}
, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for a pairs of adjacent nodes from
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{lo}}
and
V_{a_{2}}^{\textup{lo}}
, and then for each
i\in[3,\ell+1]
there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{1/\ell})
choices for a neighbor in
V_{a_{i}}^{\textup{lo}}
of the chosen node in
V_{a_{i-1}}^{\textup{lo}}
, because all these nodes have degree at most
m^{1/\ell}
. Thus, we materialize
B_{1}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\ell-1)\cdot 1/\ell})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/\ell})
in total. Bag
B_{2}
is materialized analogously.

Figure 15
:
An example of a tree decomposition of
C_{k}
for Case 2 of
Lemma

7.3
for
k=8
.

The cases are mutually exclusive and cover all
H
-subgraphs of
G
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
H=K_{2,k}
for
k\geq 2
.
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\frac{1}{k}})
.

Proof 7.6
.

Denote the nodes in the first part of the bipartite graph as
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
, and the nodes in the second part as
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
,
\ldots
,
b_{k}
.

Consider some
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
. We create several partial
H
-encodings of
G
and claim that exactly one of them encodes
\overline{v}
.

We split the nodes in
V_{a_{1}}
and
V_{a_{2}}
into the ones that have degrees smaller than
m^{1/k}
(low-degree nodes, sets
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{lo}}
and
V_{a_{2}}^{\textup{lo}}
) and the ones that have degrees at least
m^{1/k}
(high-degree nodes, sets
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{hi}}
and
V_{a_{2}}^{\textup{hi}}
).
For each of the four choices of high or low degrees in parts
V_{a_{1}}
and
V_{a_{2}}
, we construct a partial
H
-encoding that encodes exactly the
H
-subgraphs that satisfy these degree constraints.
As
\overline{v}
satisfies exactly one of these four cases, it will be encoded exactly once.
Once we fix for
V_{a_{1}}
and
V_{a_{2}}
whether we consider a low-degree or a high-degree node in that part, we filter out all other nodes. We distinguish three cases.

•

Case 1:
v_{a_{1}}
has high degree.

In this case Observation
3.4
implies
|V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{hi}}|\leq m^{1-1/k}
.
Since
H-\{a_{1}\}
is a tree, it has a tree decomposition where each bag consists of two adjacent nodes.
Adding
a_{1}
to every bag, we obtain a tree decomposition of
H
.
All of these bags can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/k}
because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes and
\mathcal{O}(m^{1-1/k})
choices for a node from
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{hi}}
.

•

Case 1’:
v_{a_{1}}
has low degree and
v_{a_{2}}
has high degree.
This case is symmetric to the previous one.

•

Case 2:
v_{a_{1}}
and
v_{a_{2}}
have low degrees.

In this case we create a tree decomposition of
H
consisting of two adjacent bags:
B_{1}\coloneqq\{a_{1},b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{k}\}
and
B_{2}\coloneqq\{a_{2},b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{k}\}
.
It is easy to see that it is indeed a valid tree decomposition of
H
.
To materialize
B_{1}
, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for a pairs of adjacent nodes from
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{lo}}
and
V_{b_{1}}
, and then for each
i\in[2,k]
there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{1/k})
choices for a neighbor in
V_{b_{i}}
of the chosen node in
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{lo}}
, because all nodes in
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{lo}}
have degree at most
m^{1/k}
. Thus, we materialize
B_{1}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(k-1)\cdot 1/k})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/k})
. Bag
B_{2}
is materialized analogously.

The cases are mutually exclusive and cover all
H
-subgraphs of
G
.

7.1
Algorithm for
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)

Lemma 7.7

(Biased Cycle)
.

Let
H
be a
(k+1)
-cycle on nodes
d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}
. Let
H
be a host graph, such that between all adjacent parts except
V_{d_{0}}
and
V_{d_{k}}
there are at most
m
blue edges, and between
V_{d_{0}}
and
V_{d_{k}}
there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\ell f})
red edges for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in(0,1)
. Say
\delta_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for
i\in[0,k]
are such that all nodes in part
V_{d_{i}}
have at most
m^{\delta_{i}f}
blue adjacent edges. Furthermore, if
\delta_{i}>1
, then
|V_{d_{i}}|\leq m^{1-f}
. We call such a graph
G

an instance of Biased Cycle
. Then there is an algorithm that solves
H
-Encoding on
G
in the following time with a single partial
H
-encoding.

•

\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\delta_{\ell.\,.k-\ell}+\ell-1)f})
if
\ell\leq\frac{k+1}{2}
, where
\delta_{x.\,.y}\coloneqq\sum_{x\leq i\leq y}\delta_{i}
for any
x,y\in[0,k]
.
5
5

5

Note that if
\ell=\frac{k+1}{2}
, we have
\delta_{\ell.\,.k-\ell}=\delta_{\ell.\,.\ell-1}=0
.

•

\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
if
\ell>\frac{k+1}{2}
.

Furthermore, if
\ell\leq\frac{k+1}{2}
and
\delta_{r}>1
for some
r\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1,k-\ell+1,\ldots,k\}
, the algorithm works in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.
We call such an instance of Biased Cycle
\ell
-reachable
.

Proof 7.8
.

We consider four cases depending on given values
\delta
, and for each case present a single partial
H
-encoding of
G
that encodes all
H
-subgraphs in
G
.

•

Case 1:
\delta_{r}>1
for some
r\in[0,\min\{\ell-1,k-1\}]
.

Choose minimal such
r
. We create the following tree decomposition of
H
.
Bag
B_{0}
consists of nodes
d_{k},d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{r}
.
Furthermore, we create bags
B_{i}\coloneqq\{d_{r},d_{i},d_{i+1}\}
for
i\in[r+1,k-1]
.
We connect them in a line:
B_{0}\leftrightline B_{k-1}\leftrightline B_{k-2}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline B_{r+1}
.
It is clear that this is a tree decomposition of
H
.
Note that as
\delta_{r}>1
, the statement of the lemma implies
|V_{d_{r}}|\leq m^{1-f}
.
Hence, each bag
B_{i}
for
i\in[r+1,k-1]
can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes from
V_{d_{i}}
and
V_{d_{i+1}}
and
\mathcal{O}(m^{1-f})
choices for a node from
V_{d_{r}}
.
To materialize
B_{0}
, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\ell f})
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{d_{0}}
and
V_{d_{k}}
, and for each
i\in[r]
we pick a node from
V_{d_{i}}
as one of the
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
neighbors of the chosen node from
V_{d_{i-1}}
.
Thus, we materialize
B_{0}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\ell f}\cdot m^{rf})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
as
r\leq\ell-1
.

•

Case 1’:
\delta_{r}=1
for all
r\in[0,\min\{\ell-1,k-1\}]
and
\delta_{r}>1
for some
r\in[\max\{k-\ell+1,1\},k]
.
This case is symmetric to the previous one.

•

Case 2:
\delta_{r}=1
for all
r\in[0,\min\{\ell-1,k-1\}]\cup[\max\{k-\ell+1,1\},k]
and
\ell>\frac{k+1}{2}
.

In this case
k\leq 2\ell-2
. Hence,
\delta_{r}=1
for all
r\in[0,k]
.
We create a tree decomposition of
H
consisting of two bags:
B_{1}\coloneqq\{d_{k},d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{\min\{\ell-1,k-1\}\}}
and
B_{2}\coloneqq\{d_{0},d_{k},d_{k-1},\ldots,d_{\max\{k-\ell+1,1\}\}}
.
It is easy to see that it is a tree decomposition of
H
.
To materialize
B_{1}
, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\ell f})
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{d_{0}}
and
V_{d_{k}}
, and then for each
i\in[\min\{\ell-1,k-1\}]
there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node from
V_{d_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node from
V_{d_{i-1}}
.
Hence, we materialize
B_{1}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\ell f}\cdot m^{\min\{\ell-1,k-1\}\cdot f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.
Bag
B_{2}
can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
analogously.

•

Case 3:
\delta_{r}=1
for all
r\in[0,\min\{\ell-1,k-1\}]\cup[\max\{k-\ell+1,1\},k]
and
\ell\leq\frac{k+1}{2}
.

In this case
\min\{\ell-1,k-1\}=\ell-1
and
\max\{k-\ell+1,1\}=k-\ell+1
.
Furthermore,
\ell-1<k-\ell+1
.
We create a tree decomposition of
H
consisting of two bags:
B_{1}\coloneqq\{d_{k},d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell-1}\}
and
B_{2}\coloneqq\{d_{\ell-1},d_{\ell},\ldots,d_{k}\}
.
It is easy to see that it is a tree decomposition of
H
.
We materialize
B_{1}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
the same way it was materialized in the previous case.
To materialize
B_{2}
, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{d_{k}}
and
V_{d_{k-1}}
, and then for each
i\in\{k-2,k-3,\ldots,\ell-1\}
there are at most
m^{\delta_{i+1}f}
choices to pick a node from
V_{d_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node from
V_{d_{i+1}}
.
Hence, we materialize
B_{2}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m\cdot m^{\delta_{\ell.\,.k-1}f})
.
As we know that
\delta_{k-\ell+1}=\delta_{k-\ell+2}=\cdots=\delta_{k-1}=1
, we get that
\mathcal{O}(m\cdot m^{\delta_{\ell.\,.k-1}f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\delta_{\ell.\,.k-\ell}+\ell-1)f})
.
Hence, we materialize both
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\delta_{\ell.\,.k-\ell}+\ell-1)f})
.

Note that if
\ell>\frac{k+1}{2}
, only Cases 1 and 2 appear, and if
G
is an
\ell
-reachable instance of Biased Cycle, only Case 1 appears, and thus the time complexity is
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

Lemma 7.9
.

For
\alpha\geq 3
,
\gamma\geq 2
, and
H=P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
,
H
-subgraph isomorphism encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/(2\gamma+\left\lfloor\frac{\alpha-1}{2}\right\rfloor)})
.

Proof 7.10
.

We prove the lemma for even values of
\alpha
because for odd values of
\alpha
, the desired time complexity for
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
is the same as for
P(\alpha+1,\gamma\times 2)
, and thus by
Lemma

2.15
, the claim follows from the even case.

Define
\ell\coloneqq\frac{\alpha-2}{2}
(so
\alpha=2\ell+2
) and
f\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\gamma+\ell}
.
We need to solve the problem in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(2\gamma+\ell-1)f})
.

Consider some
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
.
We create several partial
H
-encodings of
G
and claim that exactly one of them encodes
\overline{v}
.

We split the nodes in
V_{a_{0}},V_{a_{1}},V_{a_{2}},\ldots,V_{a_{\alpha}}
of
G
into the ones that have degrees smaller than
m^{f}
(low-degree nodes, sets
V_{a_{i}}^{\textup{lo}}
) and the ones that have degrees at least
m^{f}
(high-degree nodes, sets
V_{a_{i}}^{\textup{hi}}
).
For each of the
2^{\alpha+1}=\mathcal{O}(1)
choices of high and low degrees, we construct several partial
H
-encodings that encode exactly the
H
-subgraphs that satisfy these degree constraints.
As
\overline{v}
satisfies exactly one of these
2^{\alpha+1}
cases, it will be encoded exactly once.
Once we fix for each of the parts
V_{a_{0}}\ldots,V_{a_{\alpha}}
whether we consider a low-degree or a high-degree node in that part, we filter out all other nodes.
By a slight abuse of notation we still call the new filtered graph
G
.

We consider four cases.

•

Case 1:
v_{a_{0}}
has high degree.
In this case
|V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{hi}}|\leq m^{1-f}
due to
Section

3.4
.
Since
H-\{a_{0}\}
is a tree, it has a tree decomposition where every bag consists of exactly two adjacent nodes.
Adding
a_{0}
to all these bags, we obtain a tree decomposition of
H
.
Each one of these bags can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes and
|V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{hi}}|=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-f})
choices for
v_{a_{0}}
.

•

Case 1’:
v_{a_{0}}
has low degree, and
v_{a_{\alpha}}
has high degree.

This case is symmetric to the previous one.

•

Case 2:
v_{a_{0}},v_{a_{1}},\ldots,v_{a_{\ell}},v_{a_{\alpha-\ell}},\ldots,v_{a_{\alpha-1}}
, and
v_{a_{\alpha}}
have low degrees.

As
\alpha-\ell=\ell+2
, all nodes
v_{a_{i}}
except for
v_{a_{\ell+1}}
have low degrees.
We create a tree decomposition consisting of two adjacent bags:
B_{1}\coloneqq\{a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell+1},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}
and
B_{2}\coloneqq\{a_{\ell+1},a_{\ell+2},\ldots,a_{\alpha},c_{1},c_{2},\allowbreak\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}
.
It is easy to see that it is a tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

16
).
To materialize
B_{1}
, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
and
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{lo}}
. Then, for each
c_{i}
, for
i\in[\gamma]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
.
Finally, for each
i\in[2,\ell]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{a_{i}}^{\textup{lo}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{i-1}}^{\textup{lo}}
, and there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{a_{\ell+1}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{\ell}}^{\textup{lo}}
.
In total, to materialize
B_{1}
, we use
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\gamma f+\ell f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(2\gamma+\ell-1)f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
time.
We materialize
B_{2}
analogously.

Figure 16
:
An example of a tree decomposition of
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
for Case 2 of
Lemma

7.9
for
\alpha=4
and
\gamma=2
.

•

Case 3:
v_{a_{0}}
and
v_{a_{\alpha}}
have low degrees, and at least one of
v_{a_{1}},\ldots,v_{a_{\ell}},v_{a_{\alpha-\ell}},\ldots,v_{a_{\alpha-1}}
has a high degree.

As the degrees of nodes in
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
are smaller than
m^{f}
, in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma-1)f})
we can materialize a bag
B_{a_{0}\to c}\coloneqq\{a_{0},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}
: there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
and
V_{c_{1}}
, and there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
, for each
i\in[2,\gamma]
.
By using a binary search tree as a dictionary we can store for every tuple of nodes from parts
V_{c_{1}},V_{c_{2}},\ldots,V_{c_{\gamma}}
all their common neighbors in
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{1+(\gamma-1)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(2\gamma+\ell-1)f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.
Note that
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is one of the listed tuples as they have a common neighbor
v_{a_{0}}\in V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
.
Now we split the listed tuples of nodes into the ones that have less than
m^{\gamma f}
common neighbors in
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
(low-degree tuples) and the ones that have at least
m^{\gamma f}
common neighbors (high-degree tuples).
Similarly to nodes from parts
V_{a_{i}}
, we deal with low- and high-degree tuples separately.
We distinguish two cases.

–

Case 3.1:
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a high-degree tuple.

The total number of copies of
B_{a_{0}\to c}
that we generated is at most
m^{1+(\gamma-1)f}
.
Hence, there are at most
m^{1-f}
high-degree tuples of nodes from parts
V_{c_{1}},V_{c_{2}},\ldots,V_{c_{\gamma}}
.
Consider the graph
H-\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}
.
Since it is a tree, it has a tree decomposition where each bag consists of two adjacent nodes.
Adding
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}
to every bag, we obtain a tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

17
).
We can materialize each bag in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes and at most
m^{1-f}
choices for a high-degree tuple from
V_{c_{1}}\times V_{c_{2}}\times\cdots\times V_{c_{\gamma}}
.
Note that this partial
H
-encoding encodes exactly such
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
, for which
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a high-degree tuple because for all bags, only high-degree tuples
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
are listed.

Figure 17
:
An example of a tree decomposition of
P(\alpha,\gamma\times 2)
for Case 3.1 of
Lemma

7.9
for
\alpha=4
and
\gamma=2
.

–

Case 3.2:
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a low-degree tuple.

In time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma-1)f})
we materialize a bag
B_{a_{\alpha}\to c}\coloneqq\{a_{\alpha},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}
analogously to the materialization of
B_{a_{0}\to c}
.
If the chosen nodes from parts
V_{c_{1}},V_{c_{2}},\ldots,V_{c_{\gamma}}
form a low-degree tuple, we spend time
\mathcal{O}(m^{\gamma f})
to materialize all their common neighbors in
V_{a_{0}}
by accessing the dictionary.
This way we create a submaterialization
S_{\textup{down}}
of
B_{\textup{down}}\coloneqq B_{a_{\alpha}\to c}\cup\{a_{0}\}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma-1)f}\cdot(\log m+m^{\gamma f}))=\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(2\gamma-1)f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-(\ell+1)f})
.
Note that for an
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
, we have that
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a low-degree tuple if and only if
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B_{\textup{down}}}\in S_{\textup{down}}
.
Thus, it remains to attach a decomposition of the path
a
to
B_{\textup{down}}
.
We attach a bag
B_{\textup{ends}}\coloneqq\{a_{0},a_{\alpha}\}
to
B_{\textup{down}}
and create its submaterialization
S_{\textup{ends}}
as a set of projections of the elements of
S_{\textup{down}}
onto
B_{\textup{ends}}
.
This takes time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(|S_{\textup{down}}|)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-(\ell+1)f})\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-2f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, and we have
|S_{\textup{ends}}|\leq|S_{\textup{down}}|=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-(\ell+1)f})
.
We consider
V_{a_{0}},\ldots,V_{a_{\alpha}}
as an instance of Biased Cycle, where we think of edges of
G
between
V_{a_{i-1}}
and
V_{a_{i}}
for
i\in[\alpha]
as the blue edges, and we think of
S_{\textup{ends}}
as the red edges between
V_{a_{0}}
and
V_{a_{\alpha}}
.
The values
\delta_{i}
are defined as 1 if we pick low-degree nodes in
V_{a_{i}}
, and as
1/f
if we pick high-degree nodes in
V_{a_{i}}
.
Since at least one of
v_{a_{1}},\ldots,v_{a_{\ell}},v_{a_{\alpha-\ell}},\ldots,v_{a_{\alpha-1}}
has high degree in this case and
\ell+1\leq\frac{\alpha+1}{2}
, such an instance of Biased Cycle is
(\ell+1)
-reachable.
Thus, we can apply
Lemma

7.7
to create a full encoding of this Biased Cycle in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.
As there is an edge
\{a_{0},a_{\alpha}\}
in this instance of Biased Cycle, we can attach the resulting tree decomposition with a partial encoding to
B_{\textup{ends}}
by a bag containing both
a_{0}
and
a_{\alpha}
.
Hence, we create a partial
H
-encoding of
G
.
Note that this partial
H
-encoding encodes exactly such
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
, for which
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a low-degree tuple, because for
B_{\textup{ends}}
only low-degree tuples
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
are listed.

We note that the cases are mutually exclusive and cover all
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
in
G
.
Hence, we create a full
H
-encoding of
G
.

7.2
Algorithm for
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)

Lemma 7.11
.

For
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 3
,
\gamma\geq 1
, and
H=P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
,
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}})
.

Proof 7.12
.

Let
f\coloneqq\frac{1}{\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}
. We want to solve the problem in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-1)f})
.

Consider some
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
.
We create several partial
H
-encodings of
G
and claim that exactly one of them encodes
\overline{v}
.

We split the nodes in
V_{a_{0}},V_{a_{1}},V_{a_{2}},\ldots,V_{a_{\alpha}},V_{b_{1}},V_{b_{2}},\ldots,V_{b_{\beta-1}}
of
G
into groups called subparts by their degrees: degrees in
[1,m^{f})
(sets
V_{a_{i}}^{1}
and
V_{b_{i}}^{1}
), in
[m^{f},m^{2f})
(sets
V_{a_{i}}^{2}
and
V_{b_{i}}^{2}
),
\ldots
, in
[m^{(\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-1)\cdot f},m]
(sets
V_{a_{i}}^{\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}
and
V_{b_{i}}^{\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}
).
For each of the
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)^{\alpha+\beta}=\mathcal{O}(1)
choices of subparts in parts
V_{a_{i}}
and
V_{b_{i}}
, we construct several partial
H
-encodings that encode exactly the
H
-subgraphs that satisfy these degree constraints.
As
\overline{v}
satisfies exactly one of these
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)^{\alpha+\beta}
cases, it will be encoded exactly once.
Let
\delta_{d}
be such that
v_{d}
belongs to
V_{d}^{\delta_{d}}
for any
d\in\{a_{i}\mid i\in[0,\alpha]\}\cup\{b_{i}\mid i\in[\beta-1]\}
.
Note that all nodes in
V_{d}^{\delta_{d}}
have degrees at most
m^{\delta_{d}f}
, and
|V_{d}^{\delta_{d}}|\leq m^{1-(\delta_{d}-1)f}
due to
Section

3.4
as all nodes in
V_{d}^{\delta_{d}}
have degrees at least
m^{(\delta_{d}-1)f}
in
G
.
Denote
\delta_{\max}\coloneqq\max\{\delta_{d}\mid d\in\{a_{i}\mid i\in[0,\alpha]\}\cup\{b_{i}\mid i\in[\beta-1]\}\}
and
d_{\max}\coloneqq\argmax\{\delta_{d}\mid d\in\{a_{i}\mid i\in[0,\alpha]\}\cup\{b_{i}\mid i\in[\beta-1]\}\}
.
Furthermore, we denote
\delta_{a_{x.\,.y}}\coloneqq\sum_{x\leq i\leq y}\delta_{a_{i}}
for any
x,y\in[0,\alpha]
and
\delta_{b_{x.\,.y}}\coloneqq\sum_{x\leq i\leq y}\delta_{b_{i}}
for any
x,y\in[0,\beta]
.
Once we fix for each of the parts
V_{a_{i}}
and
V_{b_{i}}
to which subpart belongs the corresponding node of
\overline{v}
, we filter out all other nodes.
By a slight abuse of notation we still call the new filtered graph
G
.

After the degree splitting, in some cases simple algorithms are already applicable. We first deal with some such cases.

7.2.1

High-degree-low-degree Algorithms

We consider seven special cases. The exact condition of each case includes negations of all previous cases, but we omit them for conciseness.

Special Case 1:
\delta_{a_{0}}\geq 2
.

In this case
|V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}|\leq m^{1-f}
.
Since
H-\{a_{0}\}
is a tree, it has a tree decomposition where every bag consists of exactly two adjacent nodes.
Adding
a_{0}
to all these bags, we obtain a tree decomposition of
H
.
Each one of these bags can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes and
|V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}|\leq m^{1-f}
choices for a node in
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
.

Special Case 1’:
\delta_{a_{\alpha}}\geq 2
.

This case is symmetric to the previous one.

Hence, from now on we may assume
\delta_{a_{0}}=\delta_{a_{\alpha}}=1
.

Special Case 2:
\delta_{\max}\leq 2\gamma+1
and
\alpha=\beta
.

We create a tree decomposition of
H
consisting of two adjacent bags:

B_{1}\coloneqq\{a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{\alpha-1},b_{1},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}

and

B_{2}\coloneqq\{a_{\alpha-1},b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{\beta-1},b_{\beta},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}.

It is easy to see that it is indeed a valid tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

18
).

To materialize
B_{1}
, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
and
V_{b_{1}}^{\delta_{b_{1}}}
.
Then, for each
i\in[\gamma]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
.
Furthermore, for each
i\in[\alpha-1]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{\delta_{a_{i-1}}f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{a_{i}}^{\delta_{a_{i}}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{i-1}}^{\delta_{a_{i-1}}}
.
In total, this takes time

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma+\delta_{a_{0.\,.\alpha-2}})f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma+1+(\alpha-2)\cdot\delta_{\max})f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma+1+(\alpha-2)\cdot(2\gamma+1))f})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m^{1+((2\alpha-3)\gamma+\alpha-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}),

where the last inequality holds due to
Lemma

8.21
.

We materialize
B_{2}
analogously.
Hence, we get a full
H
-encoding of
G
.

Figure 18
:
An example of a tree decomposition of
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
for Special Case 2 of
Lemma

7.11
for
\alpha=\beta=6
and
\gamma=3
.

Special Case 3:
\delta_{\max}\leq 2\gamma+1
and
\alpha>\beta
.

We create a tree decomposition of
H
. See
Figure

19
.
Let
B_{b}\coloneqq\{b_{0},b_{1},\ldots,b_{\beta}\}
.
To create a submaterialization
S_{b}
of
B_{b}
,
note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
and
V_{b_{1}}^{\delta_{b_{1}}}
. Then, for each
i\in[2,\beta]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{\delta_{b_{i-1}}f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{\delta_{\max}f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{b_{i}}^{\delta_{b_{i}}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{b_{i-1}}^{\delta_{b_{i-1}}}
.
Hence, we materialize
B_{b}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\beta-1)\delta_{\max}f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\beta-1)(2\gamma+1)f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\ell f})
, where
\ell\coloneqq\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(\beta-1)\cdot(2\gamma+1)
.
Note that
\ell\geq 2
due to
Lemma

8.23
.

Furthermore, we create bags
B_{c_{i}}\coloneqq B_{b}\cup\{c_{i}\}
for all
i\in[\gamma]
. We materialize
B_{c_{i}}
by starting from the submaterialization
S_{b}
of
B_{b}
and extending each tuple
u\in S_{b}
by picking a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as one of the
\mathcal{O}(m^{\delta_{b_{0}}f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
neighbors of the node in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
that is picked by
u
.
Hence, each
B_{c_{i}}
is materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{(2-\ell f)+f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.
Furthermore, we create a bag
B_{\textup{ends}}\coloneqq\{a_{0},a_{\alpha}\}
and create its materialization
S_{\textup{ends}}
as a set of projections of the elements of
S_{b}
onto
B_{\textup{ends}}
.
This takes time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(|S_{b}|)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-\ell f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, and we have
|S_{\textup{ends}}|\leq|S_{b}|=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\ell f})
.

We connect the created bags in a line:
B_{b}\leftrightline B_{c_{1}}\leftrightline B_{c_{2}}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline B_{c_{\gamma}}\leftrightline B_{\textup{ends}}
.
To create a tree decomposition of
H
, it remains to attach a decomposition of the path
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline a_{\alpha}
to
B_{\textup{ends}}
.

We consider
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}},\ldots,V_{a_{\alpha}}^{\delta_{a_{\alpha}}}
as an instance of Biased Cycle, where we think of edges of
G
between
V_{a_{i-1}}^{\delta_{a_{i-1}}}
and
V_{a_{i}}^{\delta_{a_{i}}}
for
i\in[\alpha]
as the blue edges, and we think of
S_{\textup{ends}}
as the red edges between
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
and
V_{a_{\alpha}}^{\delta_{a_{\alpha}}}
.
We apply
Lemma

7.7
to create a full encoding of this Biased Cycle.
If
\ell>\frac{\alpha+1}{2}
, it works in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, and if
\ell\leq\frac{\alpha+1}{2}
, it works in time

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\delta_{a_{\ell.\,.\alpha-\ell}}+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+((\alpha-2\ell+1)\cdot\delta_{\max}+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+((\alpha-2\ell+1)\cdot(2\gamma+1)+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}),

where the last inequality holds due to
Lemma

8.25
.

As there is an edge
\{a_{0},a_{\alpha}\}
in this instance of Biased Cycle, we can attach the resulting tree decomposition with a partial encoding to
B_{\textup{ends}}
by a bag containing both
a_{0}
and
a_{\alpha}
.
Therefore, we create a full
H
-encoding of
G
.

Figure 19
:
An example of a tree decomposition of
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
for Special Case 3 of
Lemma

7.11
for
\alpha=6
,
\beta=4
, and
\gamma=2
.

Hence, from now on we may assume
\delta_{\max}\geq 2\gamma+2
.

Special Case 4: for some positive integer
\ell\leq\alpha-1
, we have
\delta_{a_{1}}=\delta_{a_{2}}=\cdots=\delta_{a_{\ell-1}}=1
,
\delta_{a_{\ell}}\geq\gamma+2
, and
\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}\geq\gamma+\ell+1
.

We create the following tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

20
).
We define a bag

B_{1}\coloneqq\{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell},b_{\beta-1},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\},

a bag

B_{2}\coloneqq\{a_{\ell},b_{\beta-1},b_{\beta},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\},

bags
B_{b_{i}}\coloneqq\{b_{i-1},b_{i},b_{\beta-1}\}
for
i\in[\beta-2]
, and bags
B_{a_{i}}\coloneqq\{a_{\ell},a_{i},a_{i+1}\}
for
i\in[\ell+1,\alpha-1]
.
We connect these bags in a line:
B_{b_{\beta-2}}\leftrightline B_{b_{\beta-3}}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline B_{b_{1}}\leftrightline B_{1}\leftrightline B_{2}\leftrightline B_{a_{\alpha-1}}\leftrightline B_{a_{\alpha-2}}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline B_{a_{\ell+1}}
.
It is easy to see that it is indeed a valid tree decomposition of
H
.

To materialize
B_{1}
, note that the number of choices to pick a node from
V_{b_{\beta-1}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}}
is
\mathcal{O}(|V_{b_{\beta-1}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}}|)=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-(\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}-1)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1-(\gamma+\ell)f})
.
There are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
and
V_{a_{1}}^{\delta_{a_{1}}}
.
Then, for each
i\in[\gamma]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
of the chosen node in
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
.
Finally, for each
i\in[2,\ell]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{\delta_{a_{i-1}}f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{a_{i}}^{\delta_{a_{i}}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{i-1}}^{\delta_{a_{i-1}}}
.
In total, this takes time
\mathcal{O}(m^{(1-(\gamma+\ell)f)+1+(\gamma+(\ell-1))f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

To materialize
B_{2}
, note that the number of choices to pick a node from
V_{a_{\ell}}^{\delta_{a_{\ell}}}
is
\mathcal{O}(|V_{a_{\ell}}^{\delta_{a_{\ell}}}|)=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-(\delta_{a_{\ell}}-1)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1-(\gamma+1)f})
.
There are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices to pick an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{b_{\beta-1}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}}
and
V_{b_{\beta}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta}}}
.
Then, for each
i\in[\gamma]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{b_{\beta}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta}}}
.
In total, this takes time
\mathcal{O}(m^{(1-(\gamma+1)f)+1+\gamma f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

Each bag
B_{b_{i}}
, for
i\in[\beta-2]
, can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for a pair of adjacent nodes from
V_{b_{i-1}}^{\delta_{b_{i-1}}}
and
V_{b_{i}}^{\delta_{b_{i}}}
, and
\mathcal{O}(|V_{b_{\beta-1}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}}|)\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1-(\gamma+\ell)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1-f})
choices for a node from
V_{b_{\beta-1}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}}
.

Analogously, bags
B_{a_{i}}
for
i\in[\ell+1,\alpha-1]
can also be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.
Hence, we get a full
H
-encoding of
G
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

Figure 20
:
An example of a tree decomposition of
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
for Special Case 4 of
Lemma

7.11
for
\alpha=6
,
\beta=5
,
\gamma=2
, and
\ell=2
.

Special Case 4’: for some positive integer
\ell\leq\alpha-1
, we have
\delta_{a_{\alpha-\ell+1}}=\cdots=\delta_{a_{\alpha-2}}=\delta_{\alpha-1}=1
,
\delta_{a_{\alpha-\ell}}\geq\gamma+2
, and
\delta_{b_{1}}\geq\gamma+\ell+1
.

This case is symmetric to the previous one.

Special Case 5: for some positive integer
\ell\leq\alpha/2
, we have
\delta_{a_{r}}=1
for all
r\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1,\alpha-\ell+1,\ldots,\alpha\}
,
\delta_{a_{\ell}}\geq\gamma+2
,
\delta_{b_{1}}\leq\gamma+\ell
, and
\beta=3
.

We create the following tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

21
).
We define a bag

B_{1}\coloneqq\{a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell},b_{1},b_{2},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\},

a bag

B_{2}\coloneqq\{a_{\ell},b_{2},b_{3},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\},

and bags
B_{a_{i}}\coloneqq\{a_{\ell},a_{i},a_{i+1}\}
for
i\in[\ell+1,\alpha-1]
.
We connect these bags in a line:
B_{1}\leftrightline B_{2}\leftrightline B_{a_{\alpha-1}}\leftrightline B_{a_{\alpha-2}}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline B_{a_{\ell+1}}
.
It is easy to see that it is indeed a valid tree decomposition of
H
.

To materialize
B_{1}
, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
and
V_{b_{1}}^{\delta_{b_{1}}}
.
Then there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{\delta_{b_{1}}f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{(\gamma+\ell)f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{b_{2}}^{\delta_{b_{2}}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
.
Then, for each
i\in[\gamma]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
.
Finally, for each
i\in[\ell]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{\delta_{a_{i-1}}f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{a_{i}}^{\delta_{a_{i}}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{a_{i-1}}^{\delta_{a_{i-1}}}
.
In total, this takes time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+((\gamma+\ell)+\gamma+\ell)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(2\gamma+\alpha)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
due to the fact that
\ell\leq\alpha/2
and
Lemma

8.27
.

To materialize
B_{2}
, note that the number of choices to pick a node in
V_{a_{\ell}}^{\delta_{a_{\ell}}}
is
\mathcal{O}(|V_{a_{\ell}}^{\delta_{a_{\ell}}}|)=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-(\delta_{a_{\ell}}-1)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1-(\gamma+1)f})
.
There are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices to pick an adjacent pair of nodes
V_{b_{2}}^{\delta_{b_{2}}}
and
V_{b_{3}}^{\delta_{b_{3}}}
.
Then, for each
i\in[\gamma]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{b_{3}}^{\delta_{b_{3}}}
.
In total, this takes time
\mathcal{O}(m^{(1-(\gamma+1)f)+1+\gamma f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

Each bag
B_{a_{i}}
, for
i\in[\ell+1,\alpha-1]
, can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for a pair of adjacent nodes from
V_{a_{i}}^{\delta_{a_{i}}}
and
V_{a_{i+1}}^{\delta_{a_{i+1}}}
, and the number of choices for a node from
V_{a_{\ell}}^{\delta_{a_{\ell}}}
is
\mathcal{O}(|V_{a_{\ell}}^{\delta_{a_{\ell}}}|)\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1-(\gamma+1)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{1-f})
.
Hence, we get a full
H
-encoding of
G
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

Figure 21
:
An example of a tree decomposition of
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
for Special Case 5 of
Lemma

7.11
for
\alpha=7
,
\beta=3
,
\gamma=2
, and
\ell=3
.

This concludes the list of high-degree-low-degree algorithms we use. We now turn to the general case.

7.2.2

General Case: Algorithms Involving “Hyperdegree” Splitting

Let us recall which assumptions we already have. We have
\delta_{a_{0}}=\delta_{a_{\alpha}}=1
,
\delta_{\max}\geq 2\gamma+2
, and none of the cases above are applicable.

We first build a partial
H
-encodings
\mathcal{E}_{1}
of
G
and a partial
H^{\prime}
-encoding
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
, where
H^{\prime}\coloneqq H-\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\alpha-1}\}
, such that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
has
B_{\textup{ends}}\coloneqq\{b_{0},b_{\beta}\}
as one of the bags of its underlying tree decomposition.
We will later extend
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
to a partial
H
-encoding
\mathcal{E}_{2}
of
G
, such that
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
together constitute a full
H
-encoding of
G
.
That is, each
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
is encoded by exactly one of them.

We have two options of how we will accomplish that.

Option 1

In the first option in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
we create
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
, where the submaterialization
S_{\textup{ends}}
of
B_{\textup{ends}}
in
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
has size
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-kf})
for
k\coloneqq\delta_{\max}-2\gamma\geq 2
.

As the degrees of nodes in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
are smaller than
m^{f}
, in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma-1)f})
we can materialize a bag
B_{b_{0}\to c}\coloneqq\{b_{0},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}
: there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
and
V_{c_{1}}
, and there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
, for each
i\in[2,\gamma]
.
By using a binary search tree as a dictionary we can store for every tuple of nodes from parts
V_{c_{1}},V_{c_{2}},\ldots,V_{c_{\gamma}}
all their common neighbors in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{1+(\gamma-1)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
(due to
Lemma

8.11
).
Note that
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is one of the listed tuples as they have a common neighbor
v_{b_{0}}\in V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
.
Now we split the listed tuples of nodes into the ones that have less than
m^{1-(\delta_{\max}-\gamma-1)f}
common neighbors in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
(low-degree tuples
C^{\textup{lo}}
) and the ones that have at least
m^{1-(\delta_{\max}-\gamma-1)f}
common neighbors (high-degree tuples
C^{\textup{hi}}
).
Similarly to nodes from parts
V_{a_{i}}
and
V_{b_{i}}
, we deal with low- and high-degree tuples separately.
We distinguish two cases, in one of which we create
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, and in the other one we create
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Option 1: Case 1:
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a high-degree tuple.

The total number of copies of
B_{b_{0}\to c}
that we generated is at most
m^{1+(\gamma-1)f}
.
Hence, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma-1)f}/m^{1-(\delta_{\max}-\gamma-1)f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{(\delta_{\max}-2)f})
high-degree tuples of nodes from parts
V_{c_{1}},V_{c_{2}},\ldots,V_{c_{\gamma}}
.
Furthermore, due to
Section

3.4
, there are at most
m^{1-(\delta_{\max}-1)f}
nodes in
V_{d_{\max}}^{\delta_{d_{\max}}}
.
Consider the graph
H-\{d_{\max},c_{1},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}
.
Since it is a tree, it has a tree decomposition where each bag consists of two adjacent nodes.
Adding
d_{\max},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}
to every bag, we obtain a tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

22
).

We materialize each bag from this tree decomposition as follows.
There are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes.
There are
\mathcal{O}(m^{(\delta_{\max}-2)f})
choices for a high-degree tuple from
V_{c_{1}}\times V_{c_{2}}\times\cdots\times V_{c_{\gamma}}
.
Finally, there are at most
m^{1-(\delta_{\max}-1)f}
choices for a node from
V_{d_{\max}}^{\delta_{d_{\max}}}
.
Hence, we can materialize these bags in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\delta_{\max}-2)f+(1-(\delta_{\max}-1)f)})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

Note that this partial
H
-encoding
\mathcal{E}_{1}
encodes exactly such
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
of
G
, for which
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a high-degree tuple because for all bags, only high-degree tuples
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
are listed.

Figure 22
:
An example of a tree decomposition of
P(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\times 2)
for Case 1 of Option 1 of
Lemma

7.11
for
\alpha=6
,
\beta=4
,
\gamma=2
, and
d_{\max}=a_{4}
.

Option 1: Case 2:
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a low-degree tuple.

In time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma-1)f})
we materialize a bag
B_{b_{\beta}\to c}\coloneqq\{b_{\beta},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}
analogously to the materialization of
B_{b_{0}\to c}
.
Then for each low-degree tuple of nodes from
V_{c_{1}},V_{c_{2}},\ldots,V_{c_{\gamma}}
, we spend time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1-(\delta_{\max}-\gamma-1)f})
to materialize all their common neighbors in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
by accessing the dictionary.
This way we create a submaterialization
S_{\textup{down}}
of
B_{\textup{down}}\coloneqq B_{b_{\beta}\to c}\cup\{b_{0}\}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma-1)f}\cdot(\log m+m^{1-(\delta_{\max}-\gamma-1)f}))=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-(\delta_{\max}-2\gamma)f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-kf})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-2f})
.
Note that for an
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
, we have that
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a low-degree tuple if and only if
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B_{\textup{down}}}\in S_{\textup{down}}
.
We attach a bag
B_{\textup{ends}}\coloneqq\{b_{0},b_{\beta}\}
to
B_{\textup{down}}
and create its submaterialization
S_{\textup{ends}}
as a set of projections of the elements of
S_{\textup{down}}
onto
B_{\textup{ends}}
.
This takes time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(|S_{\textup{down}}|)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-kf})\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-2f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, and we have
|S_{\textup{ends}}|\leq|S_{\textup{down}}|=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-kf})
as promised.
It remains to attach the decomposition of the path
b_{0}\leftrightline b_{1}\leftrightline\cdots\leftrightline b_{\beta}
to
B_{\textup{ends}}
to complete the partial
H^{\prime}
-encoding
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
.

We consider
V_{b_{0}},\ldots,V_{b_{\beta}}
as an instance of Biased Cycle for
\ell=k
, where we think of edges of
G
between
V_{b_{i-1}}
and
V_{b_{i}}
for
i\in[\beta]
as the blue edges, and we think of
S_{\textup{ends}}
as the red edges between
V_{b_{0}}
and
V_{b_{\beta}}
.
We apply
Lemma

7.7
to create a full encoding of this Biased Cycle.
If
k>\frac{\beta+1}{2}
, it works in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, and if
k\leq\frac{\beta+1}{2}
, it works in time

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\delta_{b_{k.\,.\beta-k}}+k-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+((\beta-2k+1)\cdot\delta_{\max}+k-1)f})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+((\beta-2k+1)\cdot(k+2\gamma)+k-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}),

where the last inequality holds due to
Lemma

8.29
.

As there is an edge
\{b_{0},b_{\beta}\}
in this instance of Biased Cycle, we can attach the resulting tree decomposition with a partial encoding to
B_{\textup{ends}}
by a bag containing both
b_{0}
and
b_{\beta}
.
Hence, we create a partial
H^{\prime}
-encoding
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
.
Note that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
encodes exactly such
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
, for which
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a low-degree tuple because for
B_{\textup{ends}}
, only low-degree tuples
(v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
are listed.

Combining the two cases together, we get that
\mathcal{E}_{1}
encodes only such
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
of
G
, for which
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}}
is a high-degree tuple, and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
encodes only such
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs
\overline{v}^{\prime}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
, for which
\left.\overline{v}^{\prime}\right|_{\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}}
is a low-degree tuple.
Hence, when we extend
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
to
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, no
H
-subgraph of
G
can be encoded by both
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
.
And on the other hand, for each
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
, we have that
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}}
is either a high-degree tuple or a low-degree tuple.
Hence, either
\overline{v}
is encoded by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
or
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{V(H^{\prime})}
is encoded by
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
, and thus we later will encode
\overline{v}
by
\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

Option 2

In the second option in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f})
we create
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
, where the submaterialization
S_{\textup{ends}}
of
B_{\textup{ends}}
in
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
has size
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f})
for
k^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+\delta_{b_{1}}+\delta_{b_{3.\,.\beta-1}})
.

We materialize the bag

B_{1}\coloneqq\{b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}.

To this end, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for a pair of adjacent nodes from
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
and
V_{b_{1}}^{\delta_{b_{1}}}
.
Then there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{\delta_{b_{1}}f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{b_{2}}^{\delta_{b_{2}}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{b_{1}}^{\delta_{b_{1}}}
.
Finally, for each
i\in[\gamma]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
.
In total, this takes time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\delta_{b_{1}}+\gamma)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f})
.
By using a binary search tree as a dictionary we can store for every tuple of nodes from parts
V_{b_{2}}^{\delta_{b_{2}}},V_{c_{1}},V_{c_{2}},\ldots,V_{c_{\gamma}}
all their common pairs of neighbors in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
and
V_{b_{1}}^{\delta_{b_{1}}}
in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{1+(\delta_{b_{1}}+\gamma)f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f})
.
Note that
(v_{b_{2}},v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is one of the listed tuples as they have a common pair of neighbors
(v_{b_{0}},v_{b_{1}})\in V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}\times V_{b_{1}}^{\delta_{b_{1}}}
.
Now we split the listed tuples of nodes into the ones that have less than
m^{(\gamma+\delta_{b_{1}}+1)f}
common pairs of neighbors in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
and
V_{b_{1}}^{\delta_{b_{1}}}
(low-degree tuples
D^{\textup{lo}}
) and the ones that have at least
m^{(\gamma+\delta_{b_{1}}+1)f}
common pairs of neighbors (high-degree tuples
D^{\textup{hi}}
).
Similarly to nodes from parts
V_{a_{i}}
and
V_{b_{i}}
, we deal with low- and high-degree tuples separately.
We distinguish two cases, in one of which we create
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, and in the other one we create
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Option 2: Case 1:
(v_{b_{1}},v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a high-degree tuple.

The total number of copies of
B_{1}
that we generated is
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\delta_{b_{1}}+\gamma)f})
.
Hence, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\delta_{b_{1}}+\gamma)f}/m^{(\gamma+\delta_{b_{1}}+1)f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-f})
high-degree tuples of nodes from parts
V_{b_{2}}^{\delta_{b_{2}}},V_{c_{1}},\ldots,V_{c_{\gamma}}
.
Consider the graph
H-\{b_{2},c_{1},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}
.
Since it is a tree, it has a tree decomposition, where each bag consists of two adjacent nodes.
Adding
b_{2},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}
to every bag, we obtain a tree decomposition of
H
.
For each bag of this tree decomposition, we create its submaterialization in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, as there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes and
\mathcal{O}(m^{1-f})
choices for a high-degree tuple.

Note that this partial
H
-encoding
\mathcal{E}_{1}
encodes exactly such
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
, for which
(v_{b_{2}},v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a high-degree tuple as for all bags, only high-degree tuples
(v_{b_{2}},v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
are listed.

Option 2: Case 2:
(v_{b_{2}},v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a low-degree tuple.

We materialize the bag

B_{2}\coloneqq\{b_{2},b_{3},\ldots,b_{\beta},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}.

To this end, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for a pair of adjacent nodes from
V_{b_{\beta}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta}}}
and
V_{b_{\beta-1}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}}
.
Then, for each
i\in[\gamma]
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{f})
choices to pick a node in
V_{c_{i}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{b_{\beta}}^{\delta_{b_{\beta}}}
.
Finally, for each
i\in\{\beta-2,\beta-3,\ldots,2\}
, there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{\delta_{b_{i+1}}})
choices to pick a node in
V_{b_{i}}^{\delta_{b_{i}}}
as a neighbor of the chosen node in
V_{b_{i+1}}^{\delta_{b_{i+1}}}
.
In total, this takes time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma+\delta_{b_{3.\,.\beta-1}})f})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f})
.

If the chosen nodes from parts
V_{b_{2}},V_{c_{1}},V_{c_{2}},\ldots,V_{c_{\gamma}}
form a low-degree tuple, we spend time
\mathcal{O}(m^{(\gamma+\delta_{b_{1}}+1)f})
to materialize all their common pairs of neighbors in
V_{b_{0}}^{\delta_{b_{0}}}
and
V_{b_{1}}^{\delta_{b_{1}}}
by accessing the dictionary.
This way we create a submaterialization
S_{\textup{down}}
of
B_{\textup{down}}\coloneqq B_{2}\cup\{b_{0},b_{1}\}
in time

\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(\gamma+\delta_{b_{3.\,.\beta-1}})f}\cdot(\log m+m^{(\gamma+\delta_{b_{1}}+1)f}))=\mathcal{O}(m^{1+(2\gamma+\delta_{b_{1}}+\delta_{b_{3.\,.\beta-1}}+1)f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f}).

Note that for an
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
, we have that
(v_{b_{2}},v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a low-degree tuple if and only if
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{B_{\textup{down}}}\in S_{\textup{down}}
.
We attach a bag
B_{\textup{ends}}\coloneqq\{b_{0},b_{\beta}\}
to
B_{\textup{down}}
and create its submaterialization
S_{\textup{ends}}
as a set of projections of the elements of
S_{\textup{down}}
onto
B_{\textup{ends}}
.
It takes time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(|S_{\textup{down}}|)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f})
, and we have
|S_{\textup{ends}}|\leq|S_{\textup{down}}|=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f})
as promised.
Note that
B_{\textup{down}}\leftrightline B_{\textup{ends}}
is a tree decomposition of
H^{\prime}
, hence we get the partial
H^{\prime}
-encoding
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
.
Note that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
encodes exactly such
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
, for which
(v_{b_{2}},v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
is a low-degree tuple because for
B_{\textup{ends}}
, only low-degree tuples
(v_{b_{2}},v_{c_{1}},\ldots,v_{c_{\gamma}})
are listed.

Combining the two cases together, we get that
\mathcal{E}_{1}
encodes only such
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
of
G
, for which
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{\{b_{2},c_{1},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}}
is a high-degree tuple, and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
encodes only such
H^{\prime}
-subgraphs
\overline{v}^{\prime}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
, for which
\left.\overline{v}^{\prime}\right|_{\{b_{2},c_{1},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}}
is a low-degree tuple.
Hence, when we extend
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
to
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, no
H
-subgraph of
G
can be encoded by both
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
.
And on the other hand, for each
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
of
G
, we have that
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{\{b_{2},c_{1},\ldots,c_{\gamma}\}}
is either a high-degree tuple or a low-degree tuple.
Hence, either
\overline{v}
is encoded by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
or
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{V(H^{\prime})}
is encoded by
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
, and thus we later will encode
\overline{v}
by
\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

We now decide on which of the two options we use.
Let
\ell\coloneqq\max\{k,k^{\prime}\}
, where we recall that
k=\delta_{\max}-2\gamma\geq 2
and
k^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+\delta_{b_{1}}+\delta_{b_{3.\,.\beta-1}})
.
We claim that in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
we can create a partial
H
-encoding
\mathcal{E}_{1}
of
G
and a partial
H^{\prime}
-encoding
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
, such that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
has
B_{\textup{ends}}\coloneqq\{b_{0},b_{\beta}\}
as one of its bags with submaterialization
S_{\textup{ends}}
of size
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\ell f})
. To do that, we use Option 1 if
k\geq k^{\prime}
and Option 2 if
k^{\prime}\geq k
.
Hence, indeed
|S_{\textup{ends}}|=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\ell f})
.
Furthermore, in Option 1 we work in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
, and in the second option we work in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f})
.
We use the second option only if
k^{\prime}\geq k\geq 2
, and hence we have
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{2-k^{\prime}f})=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

We have a partial
H^{\prime}
-encoding
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
of
G[H^{\prime}]
, and now we need to extend it to a partial
H
-encoding
\mathcal{E}_{2}
of
G
, such that
\mathcal{E}_{2}
encodes some
H
-subgraph
\overline{v}
if and only if
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{2}
encodes
\left.\overline{v}\right|_{V(H^{\prime})}
.

We consider
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}},\ldots,V_{a_{\alpha}}^{\delta_{a_{\alpha}}}
as an instance of Biased Cycle, where we think of edges of
G
between
V_{a_{i-1}}^{\delta_{a_{i-1}}}
and
V_{a_{i}}^{\delta_{a_{i}}}
for
i\in[\alpha]
as the blue edges, and we think of
S_{\textup{ends}}
as the red edges between
V_{a_{0}}^{\delta_{a_{0}}}
and
V_{a_{\alpha}}^{\delta_{a_{\alpha}}}
.
We apply the algorithm from
Lemma

7.7
to create a full encoding of this Biased Cycle. As we prove later, it works in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.
As there is an edge
\{a_{0},a_{\alpha}\}
in this instance of Biased Cycle, we can attach the resulting tree decomposition with a partial encoding to
B_{\textup{ends}}
by a bag containing both
a_{0}
and
a_{\alpha}
.
Hence, we create the desired partial
H
-encoding
\mathcal{E}_{2}
of
G
.

It remains to show that when we use the algorithm from
Lemma

7.7
, it runs in
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
time.
To prove it, we consider eight cases.
The exact condition of each case includes negations of all previous cases, but we sometimes omit them for conciseness.
One can verify that it is a complete set of cases.

Case 1:
\alpha\leq 2\ell-2
.

In this case
\ell>\frac{\alpha+1}{2}
, and the algorithm from
Lemma

7.7
works in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

Case 2:
\alpha=2\ell-1
.

In this case the algorithm from
Lemma

7.7
works in time

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\delta_{a_{\ell.\,.\alpha-\ell}}+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\alpha-1)/2\cdot f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}),

where the last inequality holds due to
Lemma

8.11
.

Case 3:
\alpha\geq 2\ell
and
\delta_{a_{r}}\geq 2
for some
r\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1,\alpha-\ell+1,\ldots,\alpha\}
.

In this case the instance of Biased Cycle is
\ell
-reachable, and the algorithm from
Lemma

7.7
works in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f})
.

Case 4:
\alpha\geq 2\ell
,
\delta_{a_{r}}=1
for all
r\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1,\alpha-\ell+1,\ldots,\alpha\}
,
\delta_{a_{\ell}}\geq\gamma+2
, and
\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}\geq\gamma+\ell+1
.

This cannot happen as Special Case 4 (see
7.2.1
) would be applicable.

Case 4’:
\alpha\geq 2\ell
,
\delta_{a_{r}}=1
for all
r\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1,\alpha-\ell+1,\ldots,\alpha\}
,
\delta_{a_{\alpha-\ell}}\geq\gamma+2
, and
\delta_{b_{1}}\geq\gamma+\ell+1
.

This cannot happen as Special Case 4’ (see
7.2.1
) would be applicable.

Case 5:
\alpha\geq 2\ell
and
\delta_{a_{\ell}}\leq\gamma+1
.

In this case the algorithm from
Lemma

7.7
runs time

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\delta_{a_{\ell.\,.\alpha-\ell}}+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\delta_{a_{\ell}}+(\alpha-2\ell)\cdot\delta_{\max}+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\gamma+1+(\alpha-2\ell)\cdot(2\gamma+k)+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\gamma+(\alpha-2\ell)\cdot(2\gamma+\ell)+\ell)f})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}).

The last equality holds as
\ell\geq k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle\geq k^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+\delta_{b_{1}}+\delta_{b_{3.\,.\beta-1}})

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+(\beta-2)\cdot\delta_{\max})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+(\beta-2)\cdot(2\gamma+k))

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+(\beta-2)\cdot(2\gamma+\ell)),

and because of
Lemma

8.35
.

Case 5’:
\alpha\geq 2\ell
and
\delta_{a_{\alpha-\ell}}\leq\gamma+1
.

This case is symmetric to the previous one.

Case 6:
\alpha\geq 2\ell
,
\delta_{b_{1}}\leq\gamma+\ell
,
\delta_{b_{\beta-1}}\leq\gamma+\ell
, and
\beta\geq 4
.

In this case the algorithm from
Lemma

7.7
runs in time

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+(\delta_{a_{\ell.\,.\alpha-\ell}}+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+((\alpha-2\ell+1)\cdot\delta_{\max}+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+((\alpha-2\ell+1)\cdot(2\gamma+k)+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}+m^{1+((\alpha-2\ell+1)\cdot(2\gamma+\ell)+\ell-1)f})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-f}).

The last equality holds as
\ell\geq k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle\geq k^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+\delta_{b_{1}}+\delta_{b_{3.\,.\beta-1}})

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+(\gamma+\ell)+(\beta-4)\cdot\delta_{\max}+(\gamma+\ell))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+(\gamma+\ell)+(\beta-4)\cdot(2\gamma+k)+(\gamma+\ell))

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)-(2\gamma+1+(\beta-4)\cdot(2\gamma+\ell)+2\cdot(\gamma+\ell)),

and because of
Lemma

8.37
.

Case 7:
\alpha\geq 2\ell
,
\delta_{a_{r}}=1
for all
r\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1,\alpha-\ell+1,\ldots,\alpha\}
,
\delta_{a_{\ell}}\geq\gamma+2
,
\delta_{b_{1}}\leq\gamma+\ell
, and
\beta=3
.

This cannot happen as Special Case 5 (see
7.2.1
) would be applicable.

This concludes the proof of
Lemma

7.11
.

7.3
Proof of
Lemma

2.19

Now we can put the pieces together to prove
Lemma

2.19
.

See
2.19

Proof 7.13

(Proof of
Lemma

2.19
)
.

Let
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.

For
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1,0,0)
,
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m)=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/1})
by
Lemma

7.1
and
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=1
by
Lemma

8.3
.
For
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(2,1,0)
,
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/2})
by
Lemma

7.3
and
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2
by
Lemma

8.5
.
For
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(k-2,2,0)
with
k\geq 4
,
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\left\lceil k/2\right\rceil})
by
Lemma

7.3
and
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\left\lceil k/2\right\rceil
by
Lemma

8.15
.

Otherwise, we have
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 2
and
\gamma\geq 1
.
If
\alpha=\beta=2
, then
H
-Encoding can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-1/(\gamma+2)})
by
Lemma

7.5
and
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\gamma+2
by
Lemma

8.17
.
If
\alpha>\beta=2
, then
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\gamma+2+\left\lfloor\frac{\alpha-1}{2}\right\rfloor
by
Lemma

8.19
, and
Lemma

7.9
shows an algorithms with the desired time complexity.
Finally, if
\alpha\geq\beta>2
,
Lemma

7.11
shows an algorithm with the desired time complexity.

8
Algebraic Lemmas

Here we prove inequalities on
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
that we used in the previous sections.

In this section numbers
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
,
k
, and
\ell
are always assumed to be integers.

Let us recall that
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=

\begin{cases}2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+2,&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ even, $\alpha>\beta$, and $\beta<\gamma+2$;}\\
2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-3\gamma,&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ even, $3\beta<\alpha+6\gamma+8$, and ($\alpha=\beta$ or $\beta\geq\gamma+2$);}\\
2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+3\beta-\alpha-6\gamma-4,&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ even, $2\beta\leq\alpha+4\gamma+6$, and $3\beta\geq\alpha+6\gamma+8$;}\\
2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+\frac{3}{2},&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ odd and $\beta<2\gamma+3$;}\\
2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+2\beta-\frac{\alpha}{2}-4\gamma-\frac{3}{2},&\text{if $\alpha+\beta$ odd, $2\beta\leq\alpha+4\gamma+6$, and $\beta\geq 2\gamma+3$;}\\
2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2},&\text{if $\alpha=0\bmod 4$ and $2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6$;}\\
2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{3}{8},&\text{if $\alpha$ is odd and $2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6$;}\\
2\gamma^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}+\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{1}{2},&\text{if $\alpha=2\bmod 4$ and $2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6$.}\\
\end{cases}

Lemma 8.1
.

The function
\mathcal{F}
is well defined. That is, the eight cases from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
are disjoint and cover all
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{\geq 0}
.

Proof 8.2
.

Fix some
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{\geq 0}
. We prove that there exists exactly one case from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
that
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
satisfies.

If
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6
, then the last three cases from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
disjointly cover all triples
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{\geq 0}
depending on the reminder of
\alpha
modulo four.
On the other hand, if
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6
, none of the first five cases are applicable.
Case one fails as
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6>2\gamma+4
contradicts
\beta<\gamma+2
.
Case two fails as
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6
implies
3\beta=3/2\cdot(2\beta)\geq 3/2\alpha+3/2\cdot(4\gamma)+3/2\cdot 6\geq\alpha+6\gamma+9
, contradicting
3\beta<\alpha+6\gamma+8
.
Conditions of case three directly contradict
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6
.
Case four fails as
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6\geq 4\gamma+6
contradicts
\beta<2\gamma+3
.
And finally conditions of case five directly contradict
2\beta>\alpha+4\gamma+6
.

Therefore, from now on we may assume
2\beta\leq\alpha+4\gamma+6
.
Depending on the parity of
\alpha+\beta
, either one of the first three cases can be applicable, or one of the fourth and the fifth. We consider these cases separately.

First, assume that
\alpha+\beta
is even. If
\alpha>\beta
and
\beta<\gamma+2
, then case one is applicable. Otherwise, cases two and three cover all triples
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{\geq 0}
depending on whether
3\beta<\alpha+6\gamma+8
or not.
Furthermore, cases one, two, and three are disjoint. Indeed, case two requires one of the conditions from case one to fail. Conditions of case three directly contradicts conditions of case two. And if case three holds, we have
3\beta\geq\alpha+6\gamma+8\geq 3\gamma+6
contradicting one of the conditions of case one.

Now assume that
\alpha+\beta
is odd. In this case assuming
2\beta\leq\alpha+4\gamma+6
, cases four and five disjointly cover all triples
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{\geq 0}
depending on whether
\beta<2\gamma+3
holds or not.

Therefore, indeed for every
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{\geq 0}
, there exists exactly one case from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
that is applicable.

Lemma 8.3
.

For
\alpha=1,\beta=0
, and
\gamma=0
, we have
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=1
.

Proof 8.4
.

In this case
\alpha+\beta
is odd and
\beta<\gamma+3
.
Hence, the fourth case from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
is applicable.
We substitute and get
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=1
.

Lemma 8.5
.

For
\alpha=2,\beta=1
, and
\gamma=0
, we have
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2
.

Proof 8.6
.

In this case
\alpha+\beta
is odd and
\beta<\gamma+3
.
Hence, the fourth case from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
is applicable.
We substitute and get
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2
.

Lemma 8.7
.

For any
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
.

Proof 8.8
.

If
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1,0,0)
,
Lemma

8.3
implies that
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=1
.
If
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(2,1,0)
,
Lemma

8.5
implies that
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2
.
Otherwise, for all
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, it holds that
\alpha\geq\beta\geq 2
and
\gamma\geq 0
.
We consider all the cases from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
.

1.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+2
,
\alpha+\beta
is even,
\alpha>\beta
, and
\beta<\gamma+2
.
In this case
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-2\gamma+2=2(\beta-1)\gamma+2+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}\cdot(\beta-1)
. As
\alpha+\beta
is required to be even in this case, all the summands are integers. Furthermore, all of them are non-negative, and the summand
2
is positive. Hence,
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
.

2.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-3\gamma
,
\alpha+\beta
is even,
3\beta<\alpha+6\gamma+8
, and (
\alpha=\beta
or
\beta\geq\gamma+2
).
In this case
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}-3\gamma=(2\beta-3)\cdot\gamma+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2}\cdot(\beta-1)+\beta
. As
\alpha+\beta
is required to be even in this case, all the summands are integers. Furthermore, all of them are non-negative, and the summand
\beta
is positive. Hence,
\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
.

3.

\mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=2\beta\gamma+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+3\beta-\alpha-6\gamma-4
,
\alpha+\beta
is even,
2\beta\leq\alpha+4\gamma+6
, and
3\beta\geq\alpha+6\gamma+8
.
In this case
