cases, it will be encoded exactly once. Once we fix for each of the parts
V_{a_{0}},V_{a_{1}},\ldots,V_{a_{9}}
whether we consider a low-degree or a high-degree node in that part, we filter out all other nodes. We now distinguish three cases.

•

Case 1:
v_{a_{0}}
has high degree.
The sum of degrees of nodes from
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{hi}}
in the original host graph (i.e., before filtering) is at least
m^{\frac{1}{8}}\cdot|V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{hi}}|
. As the sum of all degrees in a graph is equal to
2m
, we obtain
|V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{hi}}|=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-{\frac{1}{8}}})
. Since
H-\{a_{0}\}
is a tree, it has a tree decomposition where every bag consists of exactly two adjacent nodes. Adding
a_{0}
to all these bags, we obtain a valid tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

23
). Each one of these bags can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\frac{1}{8}})
because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes and
\mathcal{O}(|V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{hi}}|)=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-\frac{1}{8}})
choices for
v_{a_{0}}
.

Figure 23
:
Tree decomposition of
P(9,2,2)
from Case 1 of
Lemma

A.1
.

•

Case 1’:
v_{a_{0}}
has low degree, and
v_{a_{9}}
has high degree.
This case is symmetric to the previous one.

•

Case 2:
v_{a_{0}}
and
v_{a_{9}}
have low degrees.
As the degrees of nodes in
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
are less than
m^{\frac{1}{8}}
, in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\frac{1}{8}})
we can materialize a bag
B_{a_{0}\to c}\coloneqq\{a_{0},c_{1},c_{2}\}
: There are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices to pick an adjacent pair of nodes from
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
and
V_{c_{1}}
, and there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{\frac{1}{8}})
choices to pick a neighbor in
V_{c_{2}}
of the chosen node in
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
. By materializing all such triples, we ensure that we list the triple
(v_{a_{0}},v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
of the nodes in the fixed solution. By using a binary search tree as a dictionary, we can store for every pair of nodes from parts
V_{c_{1}}
and
V_{c_{2}}
all their common neighbors in
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
in time
\tilde{O}(m^{1+\frac{1}{8}})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\frac{1}{8}})
.
Now we split the listed pairs of nodes from
V_{c_{1}}\times V_{c_{2}}
into the pairs that have less than
m^{\frac{1}{4}}
common neighbors in
V_{a_{0}}
(low-degree pairs) and the ones that have at least
m^{\frac{1}{4}}
common neighbors (high-degree pairs). Similarly to nodes from parts
V_{a_{i}}
, we deal with low- and high-degree pairs separately. We now distinguish four cases.

–

Case 2.1:
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
is a high-degree pair.
The total number of triples in
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{hi}}\times V_{c_{1}}\times V_{c_{2}}
that we generated is
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\frac{1}{8}})
. Every high-degree pair in
V_{c_{1}}\times V_{c_{2}}
has at least
m^{\frac{1}{4}}
common neighbors in
V_{a_{0}}
which means that there are
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\frac{1}{8}}/m^{\frac{1}{4}})=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-\frac{1}{8}})
such pairs. Consider the graph
H-\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
. It is a tree, so it has a tree decomposition where each bag consists of two adjacent nodes. Adding
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
to every bag, we obtain a valid tree decomposition of
H
(see Figure
24
). Submaterializations of all of these bags can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\frac{1}{8}})
because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes and
\mathcal{O}(m^{1-\frac{1}{8}})
choices for a high-degree pair from
V_{c_{1}}\times V_{c_{2}}
.
Note that this tree decomposition encodes exactly such
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
for which
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
is a high-degree pair, because for all bags only high-degree pairs
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
are listed.

Figure 24
:
Tree decomposition of
P(9,2,2)
from Case 2.1 of
Lemma

A.1
.

–

Case 2.2:
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
is a low-degree pair, and at least one of
v_{a_{1}},v_{a_{2}},v_{a_{3}}
, and
v_{a_{4}}
has high degree.
Let
r
be the smallest number among
\{1,2,3,4\}
such that in
v_{a_{r}}
has high degree.
We create the following tree decomposition of
H
(see
Figure

25
). We define the bags
B_{0}\coloneqq\{a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},a_{9},c_{1},c_{2}\}
and
B_{r+1},B_{r+2},\ldots,B_{8}
, where for each
i\in[r+1,8]
we let
B_{i}\coloneqq\{a_{r},a_{i},a_{i+1}\}
. We connect these bags in a path graph:
B_{0}\leftrightline B_{8}\leftrightline B_{7}\leftrightline\dots\leftrightline B_{r+2}\leftrightline B_{r+1}
.
It is easy to see that it is indeed a valid tree decomposition of
H
. As we chose high-degree nodes in
V_{a_{r}}
, we have
|V_{a_{r}}^{\textup{hi}}|=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-\frac{1}{8}})
.
All bags
B_{r+1},\ldots,B_{8}
can be materialized in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\frac{1}{8}})
because there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for the two adjacent nodes and
\mathcal{O}(|V_{a^{r}}^{\textup{hi}}|)=\mathcal{O}(m^{1-\frac{1}{8}})
choices for a node from
V_{a^{r}}^{\textup{hi}}
.
It remains to materialize
B_{0}
. In time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\frac{1}{8}})
we can materialize
\{a_{9},c_{1},c_{2}\}
: there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes
(u_{a_{9}},u_{c_{1}})
from
V_{a_{9}}^{\textup{lo}}
and
V_{c_{1}}
, and
\mathcal{O}(m^{\frac{1}{8}})
choices for a neighbor
u_{c_{2}}
of
u_{a_{9}}
in
V_{c_{2}}
.
We filter the generated triples by removing every triple
(u_{a_{9}},u_{c_{1}},u_{c_{2}})
where
u_{c_{1}}
and
u_{c_{2}}
do not form a low-degree pair. For each remaining triple, we use our binary search tree to list all possible choices of a node from
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
such that it is connected to both
u_{c_{1}}
and
u_{c_{2}}
.
This can be done in time
\tilde{O}(m^{\frac{1}{4}})
for each low-degree pair, giving a total time complexity
\tilde{O}(m^{1+\frac{3}{8}})
to list all tuples of nodes
(u_{a_{0}},u_{a_{9}},u_{c_{1}},u_{c_{2}})
such that
u_{c_{1}}
and
u_{c_{2}}
form a low-degree pair and
u_{a_{0}}
and
u_{a_{9}}
are low-degree nodes. There are
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\frac{3}{8}})
such tuples. For each one of them, we materialize nodes in
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{lo}},V_{a_{2}}^{\textup{lo}},\ldots,V_{a_{r-1}}^{\textup{lo}}
, and
V_{a_{r}}^{\textup{hi}}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{\frac{r}{8}})\leq\mathcal{O}(m^{\frac{4}{8}})
by choosing the node in
V_{a_{i}}
as one of the
\mathcal{O}(m^{\frac{1}{8}})
neighbors of the chosen node in
V_{a_{i-1}}^{\textup{lo}}
for
i=1,2,\ldots,r
, where we are using that
r
is the smallest index for which
v_{a_{r}}
has high degree.
In total, we create a submaterialization of
B_{0}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\frac{3}{8}}\cdot(\log m+m^{\frac{4}{8}}))=\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\frac{1}{8}})
.
Note that this tree decomposition encodes exactly such
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
for which
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
is a low-degree pair, because for
B_{0}
we materialize only high-degree pairs
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
.

Figure 25
:
Example of a tree decomposition of
P(9,2,2)
from Case 2.2 of
Lemma

A.1
for
r=3
.

–

Case 2.2’:
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
is a low-degree pair,
v_{a_{1}},v_{a_{2}},v_{a_{3}}
, and
v_{a_{4}}
all have low degree, and at least one of
v_{a_{5}},v_{a_{6}},v_{a_{7}}
, and
v_{a_{8}}
has high degree.
This case is symmetric to the previous one.

–

Case 2.3:
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
is a low-degree pair, and
v_{a_{1}},v_{a_{2}},\ldots,v_{a_{8}}
all have low degree.

We create a tree decomposition consisting of two adjacent bags:
B_{1}\coloneqq\{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4},a_{5},c_{1},c_{2}\}
and
B_{2}\coloneqq\{a_{5},a_{6},a_{7},a_{8},a_{9},c_{1},c_{2}\}
(see Figure
26
).
It is easy to see that this is indeed a valid tree decomposition of
H
. To materialize
B_{1}
, note that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
choices for an adjacent pair of nodes
(u_{a_{0}},u_{c_{1}})
from
V_{a_{0}}^{\textup{lo}}
and
V_{c_{1}}
, and
\mathcal{O}(m^{\frac{1}{8}})
choices for a neighbor
u_{c_{2}}\in V_{c_{2}}
of
u_{a_{0}}
. We filter out triples
(u_{a_{0}},u_{c_{1}},u_{c_{2}})
where
u_{c_{1}},u_{c_{2}}
do not form a low-degree pair. For each remaining triple, we materialize nodes in
V_{a_{1}}^{\textup{lo}},V_{a_{2}}^{\textup{lo}},V_{a_{3}}^{\textup{lo}},V_{a_{4}}^{\textup{lo}}
, and
V_{a_{5}}^{\textup{lo}}
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{\frac{5}{8}})
by choosing the node in
V_{a_{i}}
as one of the
\mathcal{O}(m^{\frac{1}{8}})
neighbors of the chosen node in
V_{a_{i-1}}^{\textup{lo}}
for
i=1,2,3,4,5
. The materialization of
B_{1}
takes time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\frac{1}{8}}\cdot(\log m+m^{\frac{5}{8}}))=\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\frac{6}{8}})
. The second bag
B_{2}
can be materialized similarly in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{1+\frac{5}{8}})
.
Note that this tree decomposition encodes exactly such
H
-subgraphs
\overline{v}
for which
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
is a low-degree pair, because while we materialize
B_{1}
we discard the options for which
(v_{c_{1}},v_{c_{2}})
is a high-degree pair.

Figure 26
:
Tree decomposition of
P(9,2,2)
from Case 2.3 of
Lemma

A.1
.

The cases are mutually exclusive and cover all
H
-subgraphs. Thus, we indeed create a full
H
-encoding of
G
. In each case, we computed a partial
H
-encoding of
G
in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\frac{1}{8}})
. Hence, we solved
H
-Encoding in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{2-\frac{1}{8}})
.

Lemma A.3
.

\clemb(P(9,2,2))\geq 2-\frac{1}{8}
.

Proof A.4
.

We create a clique embedding
\psi
from
K_{15}
to
P(9,2,2)
with
\wed(\psi)=8
which yields
\clemb(P(9,2,2))\geq\frac{15}{8}=2-\frac{1}{8}
. See Figure
27
.
We first treat
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline a_{2}\leftrightline\dots\leftrightline a_{8}\leftrightline a_{9}\leftrightline c_{1}
as a cycle of length
11
and embed one node into each of the paths of length four on this cycle.
For the paths
a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline a_{2}\leftrightline a_{3}\leftrightline a_{4}
and
a_{5}\leftrightline a_{6}\leftrightline a_{7}\leftrightline a_{8}\leftrightline a_{9}
we embed one additional node, making their total embedded number of nodes two. Furthermore, we embed one node into the path
c_{2}\leftrightline a_{0}\leftrightline a_{1}\leftrightline a_{2}\leftrightline a_{3}
and one node into the path
a_{6}\leftrightline a_{7}\leftrightline a_{8}\leftrightline a_{9}\leftrightline c_{2}
.
In total, we embed one node into every one of
11
paths of length
4
in the cycle, additional one node for two of these paths, and one node for each of the two paths going through
c_{2}
. Thus, we used all
11+2+2=15
nodes of
K_{15}
.

Figure 27
:
Clique embedding for
P(9,2,2)
. Each arc represents an embedding of one node into the subgraph covered by the arc.

We omit the details, but it can be checked by inspecting
Figure

27
that each embedded subgraphs is connected and any two embedded subgraphs touch, so
\psi
is a valid clique embedding. Furthermore, every edge has weak edge depth equal to
8
, thus proving the claim.

Appendix B
Further Related Work

Standard variations of the problems studied in this paper arise from the following changes:

•

Parameter
m
vs
n
:
Measuring running time in terms of
m
is natural, as it describes the input size. Nevertheless, the majority of work on subgraph finding problems studies the parameter
n
. We leave it as an open problem whether similar results as in this paper can be shown for parameter
n
.

•

Colored vs uncolored:
We study the colored version of subgraph isomorphism, where each node of the host graph
G
is colored with a node of the pattern graph
H
, and we are only interested in
H
-subgraphs that respect this coloring. This problem variant arises e.g. in database problems. From a graph theory standpoint, the uncolored variant is somewhat more natural and has been widely studied (see, e.g.
[
79
,
36
]
), also for listing (see, e.g.,
[
5
,
52
,
51
]
). The seminal color coding technique transfers any algorithm from the colored setting to the uncolored setting at the cost of only a logarithmic factor in the running time
[
8
]
. However, conditional lower bounds are significantly harder to prove in the uncolored setting (see e.g. the recent complicated resolution of listing 4-cycles in the uncolored setting
[
3
,
2
,
52
,
33
,
5
]
). We leave as an open problem whether results as in this paper can be shown for the uncolored problem variant.

•

Induced vs non-induced:
In this paper we search for subgraphs that are not necessarily induced. Induced subgraph isomorphism behaves quite differently (see, e.g.,
[
75
,
38
,
39
,
43
]
), so we do not expect that similar results as in this paper hold for the induced version.

Further related work considers subgraph isomorphism with restrictions on the host graph (see, e.g.,
[
34
,
60
,
65
]
) or with respect to other parameters (see, e.g.,
[
65
,
37
]
).
Closely related are problems in which the pattern is not a fixed subgraph, but the goal is to detect the smallest subgraph from a family of subgraphs, e.g., finding the shortest cycle (see, e.g.,
[
62
]
).

Fixed-Parameter Tractability

In parameterized algorithms, a class
\cal C
of pattern graphs is called fixed-parameter tractable if there is a constant
c>0
such that
H
-subgraph detection can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(m^{c})
for each
H\in\cal C
. As the hidden constant may depend on
H
, this running time is typically written as
f(H)\cdot m^{c}
. Significant effort goes into optimizing the factor
f(H)
, e.g., the
k
-path pattern can be detected in time
\mathcal{O}(1.66^{k}\textup{poly}(n))

[
18
]
. Similar improvements of the factor
f(H)
have been found for the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{\tw(H)+1})
-time algorithm for
H
-subgraph detection
[
8
,
10
,
46
,
69
]
.

In contrast, in this paper we assume
H
to have constant size and therefore we ignore the constant factor
f(H)
.

Database Theory

One of the most fundamental operations on relational databases are join queries.
Without going into the details of their definition, it suffices to say that join queries are a generalization of subgraph listing to hypergraphs (specifically, subgraph isomorphism is the special case of join queries where all tables have two columns and where the query is self-join-free).

The last 15 years have brought two major advances for join queries in database theory. The first is the AGM bound, which bounds the maximal output size of a join for a fixed query and is tight up to constant factors
[
12
]
. More precisely, for a join query
Q
the AGM bound is a number
\textup{AGM}(Q)
such that on any input database of size
m
the output size is at most
\mathcal{O}(m^{\textup{AGM}(Q)})
.
The second major advancement was the development of worst-case optimal join algorithms, which solve a join query in running time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{\textup{AGM}(Q)})
, and thus are optimal up to logarithmic factors for queries attaining the worst-case output size
[
67
,
73
]
.

The natural next step is to study
output-sensitive
algorithms, for which the running time depends on the input size
m
and the output size
t
. Specifically, the next research challenge is to determine for each join query
Q
the optimal constant
c(Q)
such that it can be solved in time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{c(Q)}+t)
. (Alternatively, one can ask for the optimal preprocessing time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{c(Q)})
for enumeration with delay
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
.) Note that worst-case optimal join algorithms attain
c(Q)\leq\textup{AGM}(Q)
, and that any algorithm with
c(Q)\leq\textup{AGM}(Q)
is also a worst-case optimal join algorithm, so this challenge asks for a strengthening of worst-case optimal join algorithms.
Much of the recent interest in the fine-grained complexity of listing and enumeration in database theory can be seen as working on this challenge, see, e.g.
[
42
,
40
,
24
,
31
,
30
]
. Our results in this paper also contribute to this challenge, as we determine the optimal constant
c(Q)
for listing and enumeration for graph queries (more precisely, self-join-free join queries of arity 2) of subquadratic complexity.
We leave it as an open problem whether the results in this paper can be generalized to the general case of join queries.

Submodular Width

Submodular width is an algorithmic approach to subgraph detection (and, more generally, to join queries) with heavy influences from both parameterized algorithms and database theory.
Marx
[
64
]
defined the submodular width
\subw(H)
and showed that
H
-subgraph detection has time complexity
m^{\Theta(\subw(H)^{\Theta(1)})}
, where the lower bound assumes the Exponential Time Hypothesis.
The upper bound was improved to time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m^{\subw(H)})
by Abo Khamis, Ngo, and Suciu
[
56
]
; this is known as the PANDA algorithm, see also
[
57
]
for a simplified exposition.

Let us compare our results in this paper with the submodular width approach. It turns out that the exponents we obtain are exactly the submodular width. More precisely, in a future version of this paper we will show that
\min\{c_{W}(H),2\}=\min\{\textup{subw}(H),2\}
, assuming standard hypotheses from fine-grained complexity theory. That is, we could have obtained (some of) our results by proving bounds on the submodular width of certain families of pattern graphs and then invoking the PANDA algorithm. However, this would come with significant disadvantages: (1) The original PANDA algorithm works for detection, which was generalized to min-weight in
[
54
]
. However, for listing and enumeration the only known variant
[
17
]
requires additional constant factors in the exponent and thus would give significantly worse results compared to ours.
(2) PANDA has a large number of logarithmic factors hidden by the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
-notation, while our algorithms do not need any logarithmic factors.
(3) One would hope that applying the submodular width framework would result in a much simpler proof, as we could use the PANDA algorithm as a black box. However, the definition of submodular width is so complicated that analyzing the submodular width of the relevant families of pattern graphs would be essentially as complicated as writing a complete algorithm; in other words, following the submodular width approach would not make our paper significantly simpler. For these reasons, we are not following the submodular width approach in this paper.

Appendix C
Computing the Decomposition
D(H)

In this section we show that the decomposition
D(H)
defined in
Definition

1.3
can be computed in polynomial time (in terms of
|V(H)|
). This is not needed for our main result, because in this paper we always assume that
H
has constant size. In this section we make an exception, to show this interesting side result.

Lemma C.1

(
[
74
]
)
.

There is an algorithm that given a graph
H
, in polynomial time either decides that
H
does not have a clique separator or computes some clique separator
C
of
H
.

Lemma C.2
.

There is an algorithm that given a graph
H
, in polynomial time either decides that
H
does not have a clique separator or computes a minimal clique separator
C
of
H
.

Proof C.3
.

We apply the algorithm from
Lemma

C.1
.
In polynomial time it either decides that
H
does not have a clique separator or computes some clique separator
\bar{C}
of
H
. In the first case we are done, and in the second case we still have to transform
\bar{C}
into a minimal clique separator. To this end, we iteratively try to remove nodes from
\bar{C}
one by one while
\bar{C}
is still a clique separator. At the end of this process we get some clique separator
C
, for which
C\setminus\{b\}
is not a clique separator for every
b\in C
. Such a procedure can clearly be performed in polynomial time. We claim that such
C
is a minimal clique separator.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that
C
is not a minimal clique separator.
That is, there exists a clique separator
C^{\prime}\subsetneq C
. All nodes of
C
are adjacent, so all nodes of
C\setminus C^{\prime}
lie in one connected component of
H[V\setminus C^{\prime}]
. Let
a
be a node from some other connected component of
H[V\setminus C^{\prime}]
(there are at least two because
C^{\prime}
is a clique separator). Let
b
be some node of
C\setminus C^{\prime}
. As
b
and
a
lie in different connected components of
H[V(H)\setminus(C\setminus\{b\})]
, we have that
C\setminus\{b\}
is a clique separator in
H
, which leads to a contradiction because we assumed that
C\setminus\{b\}
is not a clique separator for every
b\in C
.

Lemma above gives an efficient way of computing
D(H)
: one searches for some minimal clique separator in
H
, and if it exists, recursively splits into smaller graphs, and if it does not exist,
H
does not have any clique separators, and thus
D(H)=\{H\}
. We formalize it in the following lemma.

Lemma C.4
.

Given a graph
H
, there is a
\poly(|V(H)|)
-time algorithm that computes
D(H)
.

Proof C.5
.

We develop a recursive procedure that finds
D(H)
. First, apply Lemma
C.2
. If there is no clique separator in
H
, return
D(H)\coloneqq\{H\}
because
V(H)
is a subset of nodes with no clique separator, and by maximality it is the only element of
D(H)
. Otherwise, Lemma
C.2
returns some minimal clique separator
C
, and we recurse into
H[S_{i}\cup C]
for all
i\in[k]
, where
S_{i}
are connected components of
H[V(H)\setminus C]
. Due to Lemma
1.4
, it is sufficient to return the union of all
D(H[S_{i}\cup C])
that we get from these recursive calls.

Set
n\coloneqq|V(H)|
. We now claim that the number of recursive calls in this procedure is polynomial in
n
, and as each terminal call of this recursion returns a single element in its
D
-set, and in the upper levels of recursion the
D
-sets are only united,
D
-sets in all recursive calls have sizes polynomial in
n
, and thus the whole procedure takes
\poly(n)
time.

It remains to show that the number of recursive calls is polynomial in
n
. We prove by complete induction on
n
that the number of non-terminal recursive calls
R(H)
for a graph
H
is at most
\overline{E}(H)\coloneqq{n\choose 2}-|E(H)|=\poly(n)
. That is, the number of non-edges in
H
. Consequently, the total number of recursive calls is
\poly(n)
because each non-terminal recursive call creates at most
n
terminal recursive calls.
We prove the step of induction. We assume that the claim is proven for all graphs
H^{\prime}
with
|V(H^{\prime})|\leq n-1
and prove it for
H
. If
H
does not have a clique separator, it has
R(H)=0\leq\overline{E}(H)
non-terminal calls. Otherwise,
R(H)=1+\sum_{i\in[k]}R(H[S_{i}\cup C])
, where
C
is some minimal clique separator in
H
, and
S_{i}
are connected components of
H[V(H)\setminus C]
. Note that if some pair of nodes
\{a,b\}
forms a non-edge in some
H[S_{i}\cup C]
, it also forms a non-edge in
H
.
Furthermore, any non-edge
\{a,b\}
from
H
can be a non-edge in at most one of
H[S_{i}\cup C]
because if
\{a,b\}\subseteq S_{i}\cup C
and
\{a,b\}\subseteq S_{j}\cup C
for some
j\neq j
, then
\{a,b\}\subseteq(S_{i}\cup C)\cap(S_{j}\cup C)=C
, which is impossible as
C
is a clique. Thus,
\overline{E}(H)\geq\sum_{i\in[k]}\overline{E}(H[S_{i}\cup C])
follows. Furthermore, for any
a\in S_{1}
and
b\in S_{2}
,
\{a,b\}
is a non-edge in
H
, but this non-edge is not present in any
H[S_{i}\cup C]
, thus
\overline{E}(H)\geq 1+\sum_{i\in[k]}\overline{E}(H[S_{i}\cup C])
.
Each of the graphs
H[S_{i}\cup C]
has at most
n-1
nodes, and thus by the induction hypothesis
\overline{E}(H[S_{i}\cup C])\geq R(H[S_{i}\cup C])
holds for all
i\in[k]
. Consequently,
\overline{E}(H)\geq 1+\sum_{i\in[k]}\overline{E}(H[S_{i}\cup C])\geq 1+\sum_{i\in[k]}R(H[S_{i}\cup C])=R(H)
, thus proving the claim.
